GAZBOT V7 — the Friday report

Week ending 2026-08-14  ·  Mon 10 – Fri 14 August 2026  ·  MNQ-led, MGC now captured  ·  PAPER  ·  46 plays  ·  10 sections

★ THE HONEST HEADLINE — the desk made money, and one desk made all of it

The whole desk booked $1,292.50 across 113 clean lots, and three of the five days were green — which is the thing Part 0 exists to show, because a year ago a green week was one day. But the money is not spread around, and the split is the first thing you should see: the day rider made $1,617.50 on 5 trades and the tournament lost −$325.00 on 108. Take the rider out and the week is red. (★ REV2 — one naming fix, used consistently from here on: the tournament is eight base gates filling six live slots. The scope doc still calls it “the six-gate tournament”; rgv_long and thrust_short were retired and replaced by exhaustion_short and the two abs_veto sides, so the roster is eight and the slate is six — see Part 1 §3.) Read every gate card in this report with that in mind — the tournament did not have a good week, it had a week that a second desk paid for.

There is no single honest total, so here are all of them, with what separates each from the next.

The 4 quarantined lots, named

They are the 13 August day-rider phantom re-book, flagged EXCLUDE:day_rider_phantom_rebook_20260813, and they are worth $1,551.00 — more than the entire week’s clean profit. That is why the quarantine flag exists and why every query in this report carries data_quality IS NULL. A report that quoted the raw table would have led with $2,843.50 and been wrong by $1,551.00. ★ THE SCORECARD — what survived the week

Lead with what survived the skeptic, never with the biggest number. Six results carried real weight into Saturday; here is where each one landed and the test that decided it.

The claimVerdict, and the test that decided it Where
The wide profit leg beats the tight one SURVIVED — the week’s best-evidenced result +$331.50 over 51 identical entries — same signal, same price, same stop, the only difference being where profit was taken. Placebo p=0.0040, sign test p=0.0235, all five daily folds positive and all four gate folds positive. The A/B legs are a scale-out, not an experiment, so the instruction is widen Lot A’s target, never drop Lot A.
★ REV2 — but it is ONE gate that carries it, so the play is scoped. Unpooled: exhaustion_short +$225.50 on 34 pairs and it survives its own battery (strip-best-3 +$127.50, worst leave-one-day-out +$101.00, 4 of 4 days green, sign test p=0.035), while abs_veto_short’s +$65.00 on 12 pairs turns −$34.00 once its best three are stripped, at 6 wins from 12. So MONDAY #1 widens exhaustion_short only and explicitly excludes abs_veto_short, whose ladder is going the other way as BUILD #12 — see Part 2 §3.		 Part 1 §2.2
Never bench a gate at face value — the standing rule, applied to the one properly red gate CONFIRMED, and it found the opposite of what was expected Every treatment aimed at exhaustion_short’s ENTRY failed against a 4,000-draw placebo — 13 cooldown cells, 15 streak-bench cells and 9 flow floors, best of them at the 85th percentile. The entry was never the problem. Under a wide stop and a far target the same 87 signals are worth +$3,459.98 against −$84.50 as traded, and the entries beat 60 of 60 random-entry controls. Under the live scalp exit those same entries sit at the 47th percentile — indistinguishable from random. The edge is invisible at the exit the desk uses. Part 1.5
“A wall of stops means bench the gate” REFUTED, on two independent populations Mechanically, on 104 signals, every one of fifteen bench rules loses money and the best sits at the 54th percentile of noise — benching after two stops cuts 18 winners to avoid 12 losers. Live, the same rule costs −$172 across the week and forfeits +$356 on Friday alone. The discriminator is the REGIME the losses happened in, not the count of losses. “Arm for periods” wins. Part 1 §2.4, Part 1.5 §3.2
The order book tells you which gold breaks will run REFUTED — and it is backwards On 397 MGC breaks, “the far side is empty” loses −$2,059.50 at the 4.8th percentile of its own placebo: 1,904 of 2,000 random picks did better. The feature carries real information and the sign is inverted — a gold level that breaks into a vacuum is one nobody is defending, and price comes straight back. The reformulation (fade it) is PARKED, not claimed. Movement 3
A better exit would have caught the runs we missed REFUTED — we are late, not sloppy The timing-decay ladder, n=57 at every rung on this week’s tape: perfect entries at the ignition minute pay +$3,799, at five minutes +$389.50, and at seven minutes −$1,073. Our gates fire minutes after the money has gone. No exit fixes being late. The idle-gate lab is an honest null: six mechanical fires for +$15.50, and −$94.50 once the single best is stripped. Movement 2
The router is earning its keep YES — and the gain is in its CLOCK, not its levels 1,148 ticks Tue–Fri (Monday is unverifiable — the systemd journal does not reach back past 08-10 23:11Z), zero systemd failures but 125 aborted decisions in one unbroken 10h20m block on 13 August, and 95% of ticks change nothing. Its thresholds are already at the best cell on both universes independently — there is nothing to win there. The timing is a different story: window 45 / step 5 / hold 3 is worth +$502 and +$399 across the two sets. ★ REV2 — and that gain is not where the router works. Rank the 40 days by session efficiency, hold out the trending eighth, and 45/5/3 scores −$13.50 and −$46.50 on those 8 days; every dollar of the +$502 comes from the flat 32. Take it for the mechanism (it smooths the boundary flicker), not for the number. Two honest holes: its leakage detector reported zero leakage while −$241.00 leaked through a gate outside its universe, and that 10h20m outage was indistinguishable from a calm day in every scoreboard. The three things this week actually taught the desk

1 · When a gate is red, check the exit before you touch the entry. Twenty separate entry treatments on exhaustion_short failed to beat a coin. The exit turned the same signals from −$84.50 into +$3,459.98. The desk has spent months tuning entries; this is the clearest evidence yet that the other half of the trade is where the slack is. And the mirror of it is in Movement 3: when the ENTRY is a coin flip, no exit saves it — median favourable excursion +3.28×ATR against median adverse −3.04×ATR is a symmetric distribution with nothing to build a stop around.

2 · A filter that beats a placebo can still be a calendar. The one treatment that cleared its placebo at the 97.6th percentile turned out to be re-discovering a floor that is already deployed, and the 17 signals it cut were 12-from-one-era. The placebo is necessary and it is not sufficient. Ask what the filter is selecting for, every time.

3 · Three instruments reported confidently and wrongly this week. The router’s leakage detector said zero on a week money leaked; the nightly rollup said $0/$0/$0 for a day whose journal had rotated away; selector_nightly counted a quarantined trade four extra times and inflated one day’s regret by 39%. None of them errored. ★ REV2 adds a fourth: two_ratchet_shadow_watch’s rMFE column stops measuring at the first 1-ATR adverse tick, so it read 0.81R on a trade that ran 87.5 points — and this report used that number to accuse its own live book of carrying mis-attributed fills. 0 of 26 trades fail the real test. A number that arrives without a failure mode is not a measurement.

★ WHAT TO ACTUALLY DO

Every row below mirrors reports/friday_v7/plays.json, which is the reconciled source of truth for this week; the same file drives the Monday playbook, so the two cannot drift apart. ★ REV2 — the card grew from 38 rows to 46. They are BUILD #11–#18. Every play carries its evidence tier and its kill criterion — if a play has no way to be proven wrong it should not be on the card.

The three that matter most

1 · Router timing: window 45 / step 5 / hold 3 (SATURDAY #1). Change the direction router's regime window from 30 to 45 minutes, its step to 5 minutes and its confirmation hold to 3 ticks. One constant block in the router service; restart required. +$502 / +$399 across the two universes over 40 days; 4-and-6 days helped, 0-and-1 hurt. ★ REV2 — ALL of it is non-trend-day money: hold out the trending eighth by session ER and 45/5/3 scores −$13.50 (live set) and −$46.50 (full set) on those 8 days, +$515.50 / +$445.50 on the other 32. Take it for the mechanism, not the number.

2 · Bench exhaustion_short against a proven counter-trend (SATURDAY #2). Add exhaustion_short to the router's managed set so it is benched when the router reads a trend running against it — the same treatment capitulation_long and rgv_short already get. n=26 / −$398.50 / −$15.33 per leg over 30 days ON THE LIVE TWO-LOT RULER (realised pnl_usd, re-derived leg by leg with the router's own regime label at entry); red on 7 of 8 days; strip-worst-day −$232.50, worst leave-one-out −$623.00. ★ REV2 — priced under the nightly's scalp-2R ruler instead, the same 26 legs are only −$168.93 (−$6.50/leg), so that ruler would WEAKEN this case, not inflate it. Separately, −$241.00 leaked through this gate unseen while the detector reported zero.

3 · Widen Lot A's target on exhaustion_short — do not drop Lot A, and do not touch abs_veto_short (MONDAY #1). Move exhaustion_short's tight leg out from 0.75R. The A/B legs are a scale-out on ONE signal with one stop, so 'B beat A' is an instruction to widen A, never to delete it. ★ REV2 — this play is now SCOPED: it applies to exhaustion_short only. abs_veto_short is EXCLUDED and is going the other way (BUILD #11). +$331.50 over 51 identical entries pooled — but unpooled, exhaustion_short carries +$225.50 on 34 pairs (strip-best-3 still +$127.50, worst leave-one-day-out +$101.00, 4 of 4 days green, sign test p=0.035) while abs_veto_short's +$65.00 on 12 pairs is −$34.00 once its best three are stripped, at 6 wins from 12. Standing caveats on every dollar on this card

Every repriced number is a CEILING. Fills are modelled at the exact stop/target/trail level with no slippage, and the desk's own standing finding is that live losses run 1.1–3.1× modelled. Treat the policy as the edge and the headline dollar as decoration. Anything on thin n is flagged in-row as a direction, not a measurement.

The fee is $1.50 per round trip and the multipliers are not the same. MNQ is $2.00 a point; MGC is $10.00. Every gold number in Movement 3 and in Part 2.5 Part A is priced at the gold multiplier — pricing gold with the Nasdaq one would understate it fivefold, and doing the reverse would flatter it fivefold.

Four method wounds are open and they bound everything above. signal_journal.suppressed_by is NULL in all 222 rows this week, so every claim about what a gate would have done unrouted is a reconstruction from the tape rather than a measurement — BUILD #6, and it is the same defect the scope flagged last week. The router’s leakage detector is scoped to a universe that barely trades, so its zero is an empty set and not a clean week — BUILD #3. The nightly rollup reports $0/$0/$0 when the systemd journal has rotated past the day it is backfilling, which is indistinguishable from a day with no value — BUILD #8. And selector_nightly counted a quarantined trade four extra times, inflating one day’s regret by 39% ($747.50 → $457.50 on 10 real trades) — SATURDAY #3. ★ REV2: the phantom is FOUR rows worth $290.00, not five worth $362.50 — the fifth day-rider row that day is the genuine claimed trade and must survive the fix.

Two sections of this report did not run. The rehabilitation phase timed out and Movement 3’s four MNQ hunts never started. Part 1.5 was rebuilt from the artifacts the timed-out phase had already saved, and Movement 3 is the one gold hunt that finished; both say so at the top. Nothing on this card rests on a section that is missing, and the gaps are named as gaps rather than reported as nulls — BUILD #2 is the reschedule.

§ The evidence behind those plays

Everything from here down is the working. Two halves, one document. It opens with Part 0, which answers the only question that survives past Friday — are we getting better? — day by day, green or red, straight off the books. Then the warm half: what the live desk actually did this week (Part 1, with the run charts inside it — every fill of the week marked on its own session’s price path, which is the fastest way to see what the prose is describing). Then the section that matters most — Rehabilitation (Part 1.5), where the week’s one properly red gate is walked through the full wash instead of being benched at face value, and where twenty failed treatments are printed by name because the failures are the point. Then the shadow board and the promotion battery (Part 2), the mid-week leads re-run adversarially on the week's own data including the whole gold programme (Part 2.5), and the Router Operation Review (Part 2.6). Then the cold, tape-first half: we ignore everything the desk did and ask, from the raw tape and order book, where was the money and did we show up? — on MNQ and on gold (M1) — then test whether the gates we already own could have caught the runs we missed (M2), and finally build gates from scratch and backtest them to death, the ones that failed included, that being the point (M3). Two of those did not fully run this week and both say so at the top of themselves. Plain English, honest money, every claim a table — and every reprice a ceiling.

P0 Are we getting better? — every day the desk has traded

One bar per trading day, green or red, total desk. The question every other section answers is what happened this week. This one answers are we getting better, which is the only question that survives past Friday.

The shape of it. Week 30 was the floor: 0 green days out of 6 and -3,083.00 — the stretch the operator remembers as “red for many many weeks”. The most recent week ran 3 green out of 5 at +1,292.50. Of 5 weeks on record, 2 closed net positive — and both of them are in the last three.

What changed underneath. The Claude-run router went live 2026-07-30, mid-W31 — the first green week is the one it landed in. Its measured effect is on the tail and the frequency of red days, not their depth: the worst day before it was -1,513.00, the worst since is -817.00, and green days went from 2 of 12 to 6 of 13. The day-rider joined on 08-06 as a second, slower engine and is unbeaten so far — on the worst day of the last week it was the only thing in profit.

Read it honestly. The cumulative total across all 25 days is still -2,708.50 — this is a desk that has stopped bleeding wholesale, not one that is paid yet. 5 weeks is a short sample, best day +1,349.00 and worst -1,513.00 are both single sessions, and PAPER fills flatter a real book. The trend is real; the destination is not reached.

P1 THE LIVE DESK — the week of 10–14 August 2026

Gaz, here is the week in one sentence: the desk made money, but almost none of it came from the gate tournament. The strategy book closed +$1,292.50, and +$1,617.50 of that is the day rider — five trades, five winners. The six-slot tournament that we spend nearly all our time tuning took 58 signals across 108 lots and lost $325.00. Take the rider away and this was a flat-to-slightly-red week for the gates.

The good news is that the two biggest things that went wrong this year are now measurably fixed. The stop bug is dead — 62 stops fired this week and every single one filled; we have now gone 393 trades and seventeen days without a single STOP_UNFILLED. And the wide leg of the scale-out beat the tight leg by $331.50 on fifty identical entries, which is the cleanest, best-evidenced number in this whole report because both lots took the same signal at the same price with the same stop — the only thing that differed was where we took profit. That result survived every robustness test I could throw at it.

The bad news is a bookkeeping one you already ruled on, and I am honouring the ruling exactly. On Wednesday a broken loop in the day rider re-booked one dead trade four times, writing $1,551 of profit that never existed and firing real orders behind it. The four rows are flagged and excluded from every number in this section. The real cost was −$794, and per your instruction it stays unbooked — so the strategy book and the account differ by $794 for 08-13 permanently and on purpose. I have labelled every total below with which book it is. I never show one under the other’s name.

THE ONE-PARAGRAPH VERSION

Strategy book +$1,292.50 / account book +$498.50. Day rider +$1,617.50 on 5 trades (5–0); tournament −$325.00 on 58 signals at −$5.60 a signal and a 43% hit rate. SURVIVED: the stop fix (62 for 62); the wide profit leg beating the tight one by +$331.50 on 50 paired entries (placebo p=0.004, every leave-one-day-out fold positive, still +$163 after deleting its five best); “arm for periods” beating “bench on losses” by $172 in hard money; and the router’s churn dropping from the 5-an-hour we flagged on 08-07 to 0.80 an hour. DIED: an efficiency floor at the entry that looked like +$361 until leave-one-day-out showed 89% of it came from Tuesday alone; and a break-even stop that “saves” $826 if you only count the losers it rescues and costs $435 once you apply it to the winners as well. THE REAL PROBLEM: $1,058 of this week’s losses came from trades that were up 39 points at their best and still went the full distance to the stop. That is an exit fault, not an entry fault.

2 · WHAT SURVIVED — leading with the things that held up

2.1   The stop bug is dead, and I can prove it

For most of July this desk was losing money to stops that did not fill — IBKR would not fire a resting stop’s trigger on the continuous contract, so the position rode naked until something else closed it. It cost us 26 trades and $1,377.50 across seven days in July. The last one was on 28 July at 08:02Z.

CheckNumberVerdict
Trades closed since the last STOP_UNFILLED393Zero recurrences in 17 days.
Stops fired this week6262 filled. 62 of 62.
Venue fill records for tournament lots this week216 of 216Every leg has both an entry and an exit execution on the books.

Call it fixed. This is the single biggest improvement in the desk’s honest P&L this quarter and it does not show up as a line item anywhere, because a bug that stops happening is invisible. It is worth saying out loud: had July’s failure rate continued, this week’s 62 stops would have produced roughly four unfilled ones and somewhere around −$200 of damage that had nothing to do with whether the strategies were any good.

2.2   The wide profit leg beat the tight one by $331.50 — the week’s best-evidenced result

Every gate on this desk runs as a scale-out: one signal opens two one-lot sub-slots. Lot A takes a tight fixed target and banks certainty; Lot B runs wider or trails, and rides the tail. They share the same entry, the same price, and the same stop. That makes this a genuinely controlled comparison — the kind we almost never get in live trading — because the only variable is where we take profit.

GateLot A (tight)Lot B (wide)Paired signalsA tookB tookB − A
exhaustion_shortfixed target at 0.75Rchandelier trail, k 1.533−$230.00−$4.50+$225.50
abs_veto_shortfixed target at 1.5Rfixed target at 2.5R12−$69.00−$4.00+$65.00
abs_veto_longfixed target at 1.0Rfixed target at 1.5R4+$0.50+$42.50+$42.00
capitulation_longfixed target at 1.5Rchandelier, k 1.51−$23.50−$24.50−$1.00
ALLidentical entries, identical stops50−$322.00+$9.50+$331.50

Where the money comes from is the important bit, and it is exactly where it should be. On the 37 pairs where the two lots did anything different, the losers were near-identical — both lots hit the same shared stop, typically within a dollar of each other. The entire difference is on the winners: A banks around $36–$42 and B banks $56–$78 on the same move. Splitting it out: on pairs where A won, B added +$11.52 a signal; on pairs where A lost, B added +$2.79. It is a profit-taking effect, not a risk effect.

The robustness battery on that $331.50

TestResultPassed?
Delete its best single pair+$289.50Yes
Delete its best three pairs+$220.00Yes
Delete its best five pairs+$163.00 (+$3.62 a signal)Yes — it is not one lucky trade
Leave-one-day-out, all five foldsworst fold still +$239.50Yes — every fold positive
Every day taken on its own+$29.50 / +$92.00 / +$69.50 / +$81.50 / +$59.00Yes — five days out of five positive
Leave-one-gate-out, all four foldsworst fold +$106.00Yes — not one gate carrying it
Chronological halves+$190.50 then +$141.00Yes — no decay
Placebo: randomly relabel which lot is A and which is B, 20,000 timesnull mean −$0.23, p = 0.0040Yes — 80 of 20,000 shuffles beat it
Sign test on the 37 pairs that differedB won 25 of 37, p = 0.0235Yes

Nine tests, nine passes. I could not kill this one and I tried hard. The honest caveat is that it is still one week and 50 pairs, and the mechanism is a profit-taking width — the sort of thing that can look great in a week where the winners happened to run. But the per-day column is what convinces me: it is positive on the chop day, the trend day and the drift day alike, which is not what a regime fluke looks like.

WHAT THIS ACTUALLY SAYS — and what it does NOT say

It does not say “drop Lot A.” The scale-out exists on purpose, and A’s job is to bank something certain. What it says is that A’s targets are set too tight for what this tape is offering. exhaustion_short’s A leg takes profit at 0.75R — less than it is risking — and it is the single biggest contributor to the gap. The specific, testable recommendation for Saturday is to widen the A targets one notch and re-measure, and the natural place to run that properly is the rehab section, not here.

★ REV2 — this result and Part 2 §3 point OPPOSITE ways on abs_veto_short. Here is which one governs.

Part 2 §3 looks at the same gate and proposes bringing its ladder IN, from 1.5R/2.5R to 1.0R/1.5R, because the wide target “converts winners into stop-outs”. Rev 1 turned the table above into a desk-wide instruction to widen Lot A. Follow both and you widen abs_veto_short on Monday while the shadow section is asking for it tightened. So I ran the same nine-test battery per gate instead of pooled:

gatepairsB − Astrip its best 3worst leave-one-day-outdays greensign test
exhaustion_short34+$225.50+$127.50+$101.004 of 4p = 0.035
abs_veto_short12+$65.00−$34.00+$7.503 of 5p = 1.00 (6 of 12)
abs_veto_long4+$42.00$0.00+$18.002 of 4p = 0.50
capitulation_long1−$1.00——0 of 1n=1

Pooled, the result is real. Per gate, only ONE gate carries it. exhaustion_short survives strip-best-3 with +$127.50 still on the table, is positive on every fold and clears a sign test. abs_veto_short’s $65 is three trades — strip its best three and it is −$34.00 — and the wide leg won 6 of its 12 pairs, which is a coin. abs_veto_long’s entire $42 is its best three pairs.

So Part 2 §3 governs on abs_veto_short and this table governs on exhaustion_short. There is no conflict once the pooled number is unpooled: the paired test has nothing to say about abs_veto_short (it dies on strip-best-3, which is the named cause of death), while §3’s case there is a fifteen-day live-versus-shadow divergence over 61 legs with a mechanism attached and, as of this revision, a fill measurement that rules out the alternative explanation. MONDAY #1 is now scoped to the gates the paired evidence actually supports — exhaustion_short, and abs_veto_long only if it ever reaches n ≥ 20 — and it explicitly excludes abs_veto_short, whose ladder is going the other way as a forward A/B.

Two bookkeeping notes: this splits 51 paired signals, not 50 (one pair differs by exactly $0.00 and was dropped from the original count); the totals are identical at +$331.50 either way. And “33 pairs” for exhaustion_short in the table above is 34 on the same pairing rule.

2.3   The day rider had its first proper week — with two caveats

Last week the rider had no ledger at all — it had never written a trade row in its life, and the report had to say so as a measurement gap rather than report a P&L. That is now fixed. It wrote five trades this week and they are all in the book.

Five for five, +$323.50 a trade. That is a wonderful week and I am not going to pretend n=5 is evidence. Strip the best trade and it is +$886.50 on four; strip the best two and it is +$452.50 on three. It stays positive, but with a sample this small that tells you almost nothing you did not already know. What it does confirm is the shape we already believed: one entry a day near the cash open, hold it, and let a trail take it off.

Two caveats you should hold in your head when you read that +$1,617.50.

(a) Two of the five are reconstructed, not observed. The 08-10 and 08-12 rows carry the exit reason CLOCK_FLAT_RECON — and there is no code path anywhere in this repo that emits that string. They were backfilled by hand after the CLOSED_ELSEWHERE bug meant the rider’s 20:40 flat never reached the ledger. They are our best reconstruction of $263.50, not a venue record of it.

(b) Four of the five have no execution record at all. I checked the fills table day by day against the tournament’s known leg count. On Monday through Thursday the fill count matches the tournament exactly — 36, 28, 60 and 18 fills for 18, 14, 30 and 9 legs — leaving no room for the rider’s executions. On Friday there are 76 fills for 74 tournament fills, and the two extra are rider-73 and rider-78, ingested at 17:56Z. Friday 08-14 is the first day in this desk’s history that the day rider’s executions reached the venue record. The fills-gap fix works; it just only covers one of the five trades below it.

2.4   “Arm for periods” beat “bench on losses” — the contradiction, settled in money

There has been an unresolved argument sitting in the scope for two weeks: three stopped trades in a row is a textbook “wall of stop, bench it” trigger, but arming is supposed to be a permission window where early losses are the price of the option. Those two policies are directly opposed. This week gave us four clean instances, so I priced the rule instead of arguing about it.

The rule tested: after K consecutive signals where every lot stopped out, bench that gate for the rest of the day.

DayGateWould have benched atSignals forfeited$ forfeited
08-10exhaustion_short14:55Z3−$92.50 (saved)
08-12exhaustion_short13:47Z11−$103.00 (saved)
08-14abs_veto_short14:20Z1+$11.50 (lost)
08-14exhaustion_short13:42Z12+$356.00 (lost)
NET EFFECT OF THE BENCH-ON-2-STOPS RULE, WHOLE WEEK−$172.00

The rule costs money, and the reason is one specific session. On Friday exhaustion_short opened with two stops back to back — 13:38Z for −$87.50 and 13:42Z for −$107.00 — which is exactly the pattern that screams “bench it.” Bench it there and you forfeit +$356.00, because from 14:19Z onward that same gate went on to take twelve more signals and finish the day +$310.00. The two opening stops were the price of the option, precisely as the arm-for-periods argument says.

So what is the discriminator? It is the tape the losses happen in, not the losses. On Friday the two opening stops happened right before the 14:00Z hour did −108pt at ER 0.22 — a genuine down-leg that a short gate is built for. On Wednesday the equivalent pair happened inside an hour doing −33pt at ER 0.05, which is nothing at all, and there the bench would have saved $103. The instrument to bench on is the regime, not the scoreboard. At K=3 the rule fires only once all week and saves $71.50 — but that is a single instance and I would not build policy on it.

3 · THE GATE CARDS

Two views. The first counts signals — one decision by one gate, whichever way its two lots ended up. That is the honest unit for judging a gate. The second counts lots, which is what the ledger holds and what you see on the dashboard. Confusing them is how we have historically double-counted a bad afternoon.

4 · WHERE THE $2,430 OF LOSSES ACTUALLY WENT

This is the part I would read twice. I took all 33 losing signals and asked one question of the raw tape: in the fifteen minutes after we entered, how far did the trade go our way before it went wrong? That single number separates an entry problem from an exit problem, and it splits this week almost perfectly into thirds.

BucketSignalsNetMedian best pointMedian worst pointDiagnosis
Never went more than 10pt our way12−$855.505.5pt39.4ptENTRY fault. Wrong from the first tick. No exit rule saves these.
Went 10–20pt our way10−$516.5014.2pt17.9ptNever enough to bank after costs. Marginal signals.
Went 20pt+ our way, then died11−$1,058.0039.2pt33.5ptEXIT fault. We had the money and gave every penny back.

The biggest single bucket of losses this week — $1,058 of it — is trades that were up a median of 39 points and still travelled the whole way to a full stop. On MNQ 39 points is $78 a lot. Eleven signals, twenty-two lots, and not one of them had any mechanism that could bank a partial or protect an entry price, because giveback is forced off on both lots by the scale-out config.

★ REV2 CORRECTION — that 39 points was mostly NOT ours to bank

The table above measures the favourable move over a fifteen-minute window from entry, and I flagged that choice at the bottom of this section. Re-measuring the same eleven signals over the window that actually matters — the time we were in the position — changes the diagnosis. The median in-trade favourable move is 7.25 points, not 39.2, and only 2 of the 11 ever went 20 points our way while we still held it. The median hold on these eleven was 92 seconds. Nine of the eleven made their 39-point move after our stop had already taken us out.

Reached 20pt+ onside11 of 112 of 11
Reached 12pt+ onside11 of 114 of 11

So this bucket is not “we had the money and gave every penny back”. For nine of the eleven the money arrived after we were flat. That makes it a stop-width problem, not a partial-banking one — and it is the same answer Part 1.5 §7 reaches from a completely different direction on a completely different sample (a 1.5×ATR stop turns the same gate’s 87 signals from −$84.50 into +$3,459.98). Two independent routes to “the stop is too tight” is the strongest thing in this section. The partial-banking test the old closing note called for has now been run — Part 3 Q1.

And the winners are leaving money on the table too

Same measurement, other direction. For each of the 25 winning signals I took the best price the tape offered in the fifteen minutes after entry, priced it at $2 a point less the round trip, and compared it to what each lot actually banked.

GateWinning signalsWhat the tape offered (per lot)A bankedA captureB bankedB capture
exhaustion_short16$1,634.50+$592.5038%+$795.5050%
abs_veto_short7$889.50+$250.5028%+$236.5036%
abs_veto_long2$129.50+$94.0073%+$136.00105%
ALL WINNERS25$2,653.50+$937.0037%+$1,168.0049%

Narrow it to the 22 winning signals where the tight leg hit its fixed target and it is starker still: the tape offered $2,448 and Lot A took $886.50 — 36% of it. Put the two findings side by side and the week has a single coherent message. We are cutting winners at a third of what is there, and letting 39-point winners travel all the way back to a full stop. Both are exit faults, in opposite directions, and they are the reason a book with a perfectly respectable 43% hit rate finished red.

ON THE WIN RATE — the standing rule, honoured

43% is not the problem and I am not treating it as one. Nothing in this section is judged on win percentage. Every verdict below rests on expectancy ($ per signal after the real $1.50 round trip) and on robustness — placebo, strip-the-best, leave-one-day-out, both-halves. Where I kill something, I name the test that killed it. For the record: the desk’s two best days this week ran at 75% and 53%, and its worst ran at 25% — but Monday lost money at 30% while Thursday made $768.50 at 40%. The hit rate is not carrying the information.

5 · THE FLAGSHIP CASE STUDY — Wednesday 12 August, the day the desk fought the tape

Wednesday is the most instructive session of the week by a distance. The tournament lost $660.00 across 16 signals at a 25% hit rate — the worst day in the ledger this month — and the reason is written all over the tape. Price travelled 256 points of range and finished 25 points from where it started. That is a net-to-range of 0.10, the lowest of the week, on the lowest 30-minute efficiency of the week (0.125 median). It was not a violent day and it was not a quiet day. It was a busy day going nowhere, which is the single worst environment for a book of fade gates.

Every tournament signal and the day rider, marked on the real 1-minute path. Arrows are entries, squares are exits — one square per lot, so a signal with two lots shows two. Green means the signal made money, red means it lost, purple is the rider. The cluster between 13:30Z and 14:55Z is where the $660 went.

5.1   The moment that says everything: 10 seconds, 4 points, opposite directions

At 13:50:01Z abs_veto_long bought MNQ at 29,960.25. At 13:50:11Z — ten seconds later — exhaustion_short sold it at 29,956.00. Same desk, same instrument, four and a quarter points apart, pointing opposite ways.

TimeGateSideEntryOut atHow it endedNet
13:50:01Zabs_veto_longLONG29,960.2513:51:27ZBoth lots stopped in 86 seconds−$133.00
13:50:11Zexhaustion_shortSHORT29,956.0013:53:58ZA took target, B trailed out+$123.00

The short was right and the long was wrong, and they very nearly cancelled out — we paid four round trips to discover which way the next ninety seconds went. The router armed abs_veto_long at 12:35Z, on what was at the time a genuinely unambiguous reading (60-minute bias +100pt at ER 0.38, sustained across three ticks), and benched it at 13:55Z — four minutes after the loss. That four minutes is the honest, measured cost of a polling router: $133.00. The router’s own graded ledger marks that bench ✓ GOOD, and it is right to — the gate never traded again. But the arm that preceded it is the reaction-lag bill, and this is the first time this month it has been anything other than free.

5.2   The bleed itself, signal by signal

TimeGateEntryBoth lots endedSignalRunning
13:31:03capitulation_long29,962.50Stopped in 4 seconds−$48.00−$48.00
13:40:11exhaustion_short29,863.75Stopped, 2m16s−$134.00−$182.00
13:47:27exhaustion_short29,911.00Stopped, 1m25s−$130.50−$312.50
13:50:01abs_veto_long29,960.25Stopped, 1m26s−$133.00−$445.50

14:06:49exhaustion_short29,903.50Stopped−$121.50−$444.00

14:21:27exhaustion_short29,877.75Stopped, 48 seconds−$91.00−$438.00

14:40:49exhaustion_short29,914.25Target + chandelier+$112.50−$539.00

Read the entry prices down that column and you can see the day. 29,863 → 29,911 → 29,960 → 29,956 → 29,903 → 29,932 → 29,877 → 29,915 → 29,884 → 29,914. It is a fifty-point band being sawn back and forth for an hour, and we sold every push up and got stopped by the next one. Five of the nine exhaustion_short stops that day were over inside three minutes.

The important thing about Wednesday is that nothing malfunctioned. Every stop filled. Every alarm fired — the bleed alarm went off at 13:49Z and the router responded to it inside nineteen seconds; the wall-of-stop alarm went off at 14:26Z and again at 14:27Z. The router looked at exhaustion_short bleeding $321 over 22 lots, said so in plain words in its own log, and correctly concluded it had no rule that authorised a bench — there was no trend running against the fade to point at, because there was no trend at all. The instruments all worked. The rule set had no answer for “busy and directionless.” That is the finding, and it is a rules gap, not a bug.

5.3   And that morning, the switch file flipped seven times for nothing

Before any of the above, between 08:25Z and 12:35Z, the router flipped abs_veto_short on and off seven times — five of those inside fifty minutes. It produced zero trades and zero dollars. Two adjacent readings show why:

Tick60-min direction60-min netERDecision
09:05ZFLAT+39pt0.252Re-arm — misses the ±40pt line by one point
09:15ZUP+42pt0.268Bench — clears the same line by two points

An arm/bench round trip decided by three points of a sixty-minute net — about one point of MNQ noise every twenty minutes. The direction state has a two-tick confirmation, but the ±40pt threshold underneath it has no hysteresis and no dwell at all, so a tape grinding either side of 40 manufactures churn that is perfectly correct by the rule and pure noise on the tape. It was free on Wednesday morning only because the gate happened to be silent. That afternoon, the 13:40Z re-arm off the same mechanism led directly to the 14:33Z fire that stopped for −$111.50. A dwell-time requirement on that threshold is the clearest single process fix in this section.

6 · THURSDAY — the trend day, the rider’s $731, and the phantom

Thursday is the mirror image of Wednesday: +355 points of net travel on a 460-point range, and one hour — 13:00Z — that did +286 points at ER 0.44 all by itself. It is the only genuine trend day of the week.

The day rider goes on at 13:38:21Z at 30,024.25 (purple) and you claim it at 14:26Z at 30,207.75 for +$731.00 — 183 points, near the top of the big hour. The tournament, in green and red, takes five signals around the edges for +$37.50 between them.

The tournament essentially sat this one out and that is the correct behaviour, not a failure. Five signals all week’s quietest count, +$37.50 net. Its gates are faders; a 355-point trend day is not their tape and the router had grind_long — the one gate built for it — benched on an ATR floor of 22 that the market never came near. The money on a day like this belongs to the rider, and the rider took it.

6.1   The blackout nobody noticed

Here is something I only found by counting log lines. The router was blind for ten hours and twenty minutes on Thursday. From 02:40:03Z to 13:00:02Z, 125 consecutive five-minute ticks returned ABORT: no JSON in output → no change. It did not crash, it did not alert, and it did not appear in any scoreboard — each tick logged as “no change,” which is exactly what a healthy quiet tick looks like.

Router health, this week — Mon–Fri, ROUTER LOG ONLYTicksNote
Total ticks1,440Five-minute cadence, all five days (5×288)
Genuine “no change”1,21084% of all five days — 95% of the 1,148 Tue–Fri ticks the front page quotes
ABORT — produced no decision at all125All on 08-13, one unbroken 10h20m block
Scheduled skips (CME halt / maintenance)459 a day, expected
Switch changes actually applied6067 individual gate flips — router log only, see the correction below
★ REV2 — which instrument these tick and switch counts came from

Two things in that table need labelling, because a later section counts the same events a different way and the two never pointed at each other.

The ticks are all five days; the front page’s are four. 1,440 is Monday–Friday. The scorecard at the front says 1,148 ticks Tue–Fri, because the systemd journal does not reach back past 2026-08-10 23:11 UTC, so Monday cannot be verified the second way and Part 2.6 §1 starts its reconciliation on Tuesday. Both numbers are right for their own window; neither is the week on its own. “Zero service failures” on the front page means zero systemd failures — the 125 aborted decisions in this table are the same event, counted honestly, and the front page now says both.

The switch counts here are the router’s own log, which under-reports. Its changed: field only records what the ROUTER changed, so the 22:00Z reactivation timer moves gates while every router tick that hour says “changed: none”. Part 2.6 §1 does the three-way reconciliation this desk’s standing lesson demands (router log under-reports, systemd journal over-reports, the modification time on gate_switches.env is the arbiter) and lands on 56 true switch events and 64 gate transitions for Tue–Fri — roughly 73 gate flips once Monday’s router-log 9 are added. Use Part 2.6 §1’s numbers. The 60/67 above is what one instrument saw, kept because the churn narrative in §8.2 is built on that same log and re-basing half of it would make the two disagree in a new way.

We got lucky on the timing. It recovered at 13:05:28Z, twenty-five minutes before the cash open and before the day’s big hour. Had it stayed down another forty minutes it would have been asleep through the only real trend of the week with abs_veto_long benched. Ten hours of silent failure that reads identically to ten hours of calm is a monitoring gap, and it is the most serious thing I found in the router this week — more serious than the churn, because churn at least announces itself.

6.2   The phantom — what happened, and the two books

You have already ruled on this one and I am not relitigating it, only recording it accurately. At 15:53Z the tournament opened an abs_veto_short position; the shared account net went non-zero; and the rider — whose manage branch did not yet test whether its own trade had already closed — woke back up on the dead 13:38 position and re-booked it once a minute for four minutes.

TimeRowBooked profitReality
15:53:23Zid 673+$372.00The 13:38 trade had already closed at 14:26Z on your claim. These four rows re-price a position that no longer existed, against a stale trail and a stale peak.
15:54:23Zid 674+$404.00
15:55:23Zid 675+$373.00
15:56:23Zid 678+$402.00
TOTAL FICTION—+$1,551.00All four flagged EXCLUDE:day_rider_phantom_rebook_20260813 and excluded from every number in this section.

The real cost was −$794.00, derived by subtraction: IBKR’s figure for the day is −$25.50, the tournament’s independently venue-verified leg is +$37.50, and the rider’s one real trade is +$731.00. Per your ruling of 2026-08-14 — “i dont want to book an 800 loss due to a system bug” — that $794 stays unbooked, because it was lost to a broken order loop rather than a trading decision, and the ledger is a strategy book by design. The obligation that comes with that is labelling, and I have honoured it everywhere: Thursday is +$768.50 of strategy and −$25.50 of account, and those two numbers never appear under each other’s name.

One correction worth banking. That night’s review concluded “no orders were placed — only the rows” and put the real cost at $0. That was wrong, and it was wrong for a knowable reason: the rider’s executions were not being recorded anywhere at the time, so there was nothing to check against. The reconciliation against IBKR on 08-14 is what produced the −$794. Where the same-night review and the next-day reconciliation disagree, the reconciliation wins — and the fills-gap fix that landed Friday is what makes that check possible going forward.

7 · FRIDAY — the day the short book was built for

Friday is the counter-example to Wednesday, and putting them side by side is the most useful thing in this section. Same book, same gates, same 43%-ish hit rate. Wednesday lost $660, Friday made $604.50. The difference is entirely in whether the market kept going after we fired.

The rider goes short at 13:38Z at 30,194.25 and trails out at 16:16Z at 30,085.00 for +$434.00 (purple). exhaustion_short takes 16 signals down the same slope in green and red for +$310.00. Note the two red arrows right at 13:38Z and 13:42Z — the two stops that a bench-on-losses rule would have acted on.

Hour (UTC)Net moveERWhat the desk did
12:00+4.0pt0.016One exhaustion_short signal, +$99.50. Best single signal of the day.
13:00−40.8pt0.083The open. Four signals, three of them stops. −$215.00. This is where the bench temptation lives.
14:00−108.5pt0.216The real down-leg. Three signals, +$68.00, and the rider running.
15:00−73.2pt0.204The leg continues. Five signals, +$252.00. The day’s payday.
17:00–19:00+61.4pt—Drift back up. Six signals, −$34.00. Small, choppy, roughly a wash.

The 14:00Z and 15:00Z hours are the entire day. Two hours of price going one way with enough persistence to be worth trading produced +$320.00 between them — more than the tournament’s +$170.50 for the whole day — plus all of the rider’s +$434.00. Everything else was noise around it. And crucially — the two stops at 13:38Z and 13:42Z that looked like a wall of stop happened eighteen minutes before the good part started.

The wall-of-stop alarm did fire on Friday, at 18:44Z, correctly reporting two stops in six minutes on both legs of exhaustion_short. By then the gate was already +$700 up on the day and the router left it alone; it was benched at 19:45Z on the stay-out meter after one more small loss. That sequence — alarm fires, router declines to act, gate keeps its profit — is what the “arm for periods” policy looks like when it works, and the router’s own ledger graded it ✓ GOOD for exactly that reason.

8 · THE DIRECTION ROUTER — the full trial read

The router’s own log records 60 switch changes this week, touching 67 individual gate keys. ★ REV2 — that is one instrument and it is the one that under-reports. The reconciled figure, arbitrated on gate_switches.env’s modification time, is 56 true switch events and 64 gate transitions across Tue–Fri, or about 73 gate flips for the week once Monday’s unverifiable 9 are added — Part 2.6 §1 has the full three-way table. Everything below is computed off the router log, so read it as the log’s view of its own behaviour, not as the count of what the tournament actually read. Here is what those changes bought.

8.1   Did the arming produce anything?

I took every “arm” event, found when that gate was next benched, and counted the trades in between. This is the honest measure of whether the router is doing work or just moving a file.

The churn problem is genuinely much better and the fixes that shipped after 08-07 deserve the credit. But it has not gone away, it has concentrated: abs_veto_short alone accounts for 35 of the 67 gate flips and 10 of the 13 fast flips (a reversal within twenty minutes of the previous one). Thirteen of sixty changes — 22% — were fast flips. Every one of them is correct under the rule as written, which is precisely the point: the rule’s edge is the problem, not the router’s judgement.

The router’s own graded scoreboard, all-time, stands at GOOD 41 · BAD 10 · CHURN 22 · NEUTRAL 36. That ratio is healthy on the decisions it grades. The gap is that a decision it never made — ten hours of aborted ticks — grades as nothing at all.

9 · TWO IDEAS THAT LOOKED GREAT AND DIED — with the test that killed each

Both of these are things I genuinely expected to survive. Neither did, and the way they failed is more useful than if they had worked.

9.1   An efficiency floor at the entry — PARKED, killed by leave-one-day-out

The observation that started it is real and stark. I measured the 30-minute efficiency of the tape immediately before every signal. Forty-eight of this week’s 58 signals — 83% of them — fired into a tape with an efficiency below 0.10, which is a market going nowhere. And that bucket is where the entire loss lives.

Efficiency of the 30 min before entrySignalsNet$ / signalWin%
Below 0.10 — a dead tape48−$686.00−$14.2938%
0.10 to 0.209+$229.00+$25.4467%
Above 0.201+$132.00+$132.00100%

Put a floor at 0.10 and the week goes from −$325.00 to +$361.00 on ten signals. That is a $686 swing and it is exactly the sort of number that gets deployed on a Saturday and hurts us the following Thursday. Here is why I am not deploying it.

TestResultVerdict
The winners test — does it keep the trades that pay?Keeps 7 of 25 winning signals, and 27% of gross winningsFAILS. It wins by deleting the winners.
Leave-one-day-outDrop Tuesday and it collapses from +$361.00 to +$38.50FAILS. 89% of it is one session.
Parameter sweep — plateau or step?0.06→+$62 · 0.08→+$169 · 0.10→+$361 · 0.12→+$430 · 0.15→+$95 on n=2FAILS. A step, and the edge only appears as n collapses — the classic curve-fit tell.
Placebo — best 10 by efficiency vs 20,000 random 10-signal subsetsp = 0.0476Marginal at best, one-sided, on the best subset.
Is it efficiency, or is it just “not Wednesday”?Still adds +$28.12/signal with Wednesday removedPasses — the one test it does pass.

Verdict: PARKED, cause of death leave-one-day-out. Tuesday alone carries $322.50 of the $361.00, and any rule that keeps only seven of twenty-five winners has to clear a much higher bar than one week and ten surviving signals. What would revive it: re-run across at least three weeks and require n ≥ 40 surviving signals with every weekly fold positive at a floor somewhere in 0.08–0.12. If it holds there it becomes a shadow candidate, not before. The observation underneath it — that we fire 83% of our signals into a dead tape — stands on its own and is worth acting on some other way.

9.2   A break-even stop — PARKED, killed by applying it to the winners too

Section 4 showed $1,058 of losses on trades that were up a median 39 points. The obvious fix is to move the stop to break-even once a trade is a certain distance onside. I replayed it on the 5-second bars, requiring the favourable move to happen in a bar strictly earlier than the exit bar so there is no cheating inside a bar.

Break-even armed atLosing lots rescued“Saving”Honest result — rule applied to every lot
+8 points20+$826.00—
+10 points17+$733.50−$304.50
+12 points15+$602.50−$435.50
+15 points11+$410.50−$489.50
+20 points7+$314.50+$103.00

That middle column is a lie and the right-hand column is the truth. Count only the losers a break-even stop rescues and it looks like $826 of free money. Apply the same rule to every lot — including the winners that dipped back through their entry price before running — and it costs between $304 and $490 at every threshold that matters. The one positive cell, +20 points for +$103, is non-monotonic against its neighbours, which is what noise looks like.

Verdict: PARKED, cause of death the apply-to-all-lots test. What would revive it: a version that arms on something other than a bare price touch — a dwell requirement (price holds beyond +X for N seconds), or arming only when the 30-minute efficiency clears a floor so it fires on moves that are actually going somewhere. The give-back problem it was aimed at is real and worth $1,058; this particular instrument just is not the answer, and the proper hunt for one belongs in the rehab section.

10 · WHAT I AM NOT SURE ABOUT

Standing habit: the caveats go in the section, not in a footnote nobody reads.

  • Everything here is one week and it is in-sample. 58 signals, 108 lots, five sessions. The A-versus-B result is the only thing with enough paired structure that I would defend it hard; everything else is an observation awaiting a second week.
  • Per-gate samples are small to the point of being decorative. abs_veto_long is five signals, capitulation_long is one. I have given them cards for completeness and graded neither. The day rider is five trades — a lovely five, and still five.
  • Two of the rider’s five trades are reconstructed, not observed, and four of five have no venue execution record. $263.50 of the $1,617.50 is our best backfill of what happened.
  • The break-even and wall-of-stop counterfactuals are replays, not trades. They assume our fills would have been where the bars say. The break-even one in particular does not model queue position at the entry price, which on a fast reversal is exactly where you would not get filled — so if anything it flatters the rule I am already rejecting.
  • The signal journal cannot tell us what the benches saved. All 222 rows have a NULL block reason, so “what would the benched gate have done” is not answerable from that table and I have not pretended otherwise anywhere in this section.
  • The 15-minute window on the MFE work is a choice. Most of these trades close inside five minutes, so it is generous to the ceiling and therefore harsh on our capture percentages. A shorter window would make our capture look better; I picked the one that flatters us least.

THE ONE STONE STILL UNTURNED — ★ REV2: turned over, and it is on the card

We now know where the money goes. What had not been tested was a partial: banking one lot of the two at a fixed points-onside and letting the other run its existing exit. The scale-out already gives us two lots for free, so it costs nothing structurally, and unlike a break-even stop it does not require being right about where the pullback ends.

It has now been run — all 57 signals, winners included, on 5-second bars, with the favourable touch required in a strictly earlier bar than the exit bar. Banking Lot A at +20 points takes the week from −$325.00 to −$105.50 (+$219.50), keeps 25 of 25 winning signals green, is positive on every leave-one-day-out fold, and beats 400 of 400 random-time controls. It also fires on only 12 of 57 signals, 78% of the gain is Monday, and strip its best three trades and the gain goes to −$45.00. That is a SHADOW, not a deploy, and it is now BUILD #11 on the card. The full working, including why the +20 cell is a spike and not a plateau, is Part 3 Q1.

The new stone: nine of the eleven “exit fault” signals made their move after our stop had taken us out (see the correction in §4). Nothing in this report has priced a re-entry rule — the same signal, taken again, after the stop. That is next week’s question.

THE RUN CHARTS — every fill of the week, marked on the day’s own price path

P1.5 LIVE-DESK REHABILITATION — exhaustion_short, and the honest failures behind it

★ READ THIS FIRST — where this section came from

The dedicated rehabilitation phase of tonight’s run timed out at its 90-minute ceiling and never wrote its chapter (rehab: rc=-1 90.0m artifact=MISSING in data/friday_durable.log). It did, however, finish and save the entire battery it was told to run — 104 live exhaustion_short signals with the tick path after each one, four treatment grids, a 4,000-draw placebo behind every cell, two independent exit sweeps and a random-entry control. This section is assembled from those finished numbers, read out of the JSON at build time rather than retyped, so the figures are the study’s own. What is missing is the prose that phase would have written, and two rehabs it never got to. Those are named in the gap box at the end rather than quietly skipped.

One gate was properly red this week and it is the same one the scope flagged: exhaustion_short, which lost −$218.50 across 71 closed lots on the live desk and has now been red for long enough that “bench it” is the obvious call. The standing rule says we do not get to make the obvious call. So the study took every one of its 104 signals back to 24 July, rebuilt each one from the tape, and asked the four questions in order: are we counting malfunctions as strategy, is the true cost right, is the bleed in the BASE, the SIGNAL or the EXIT — and does any proposed fix survive a placebo. The answer is unusually clean, and it is not the answer anyone expected. Every treatment aimed at the ENTRY failed — cooldowns, benching after a run of stops, and a tighter flow floor all lose to random. The entry is not the problem. The exit is: the same signals, entered identically and held with a wide stop and a far target instead of scalped, are worth $3,459.98 against −$84.50 as the desk actually traded them, and the entries beat 60 of 60 random-entry controls under the same exit. The gate is rehabilitated as a SHADOW candidate, not deployed — and the reason it is not deployed is in §7.

1 · Normalise the malfunctions first — and this week there are none to strip

The first step of the wash is always to take the software failures out of the strategy verdict, because a gate that was never given a working stop has not been tested. Last week that step mattered enormously. This week it is a no-op, and that is itself the finding. Across the whole week the desk filled 62 of 62 stops, with no STOP_UNFILLED in 393 trades and 17 days — the resting-stop-on-a-concrete-future fix has held. So none of the numbers below need a malfunction adjustment, and every dollar exhaustion_short lost is a strategy dollar. Of its 104 signals, 52 ended at the stop — almost exactly half — and all of them were real fills at real prices.

2 · The corrected-cost reconstruct — what the gate really is

Priced honestly at $1.50 per round trip and $2.00 a point, over the full signal history rather than just this week, exhaustion_short is not a disaster. It is something more awkward: a coin-flip that pays slightly less than nothing.

The gate, as tradedsignalsnet $win %$ per signalended at the stop
LIVE as traded104−$158.0050.0%−$1.5252 of 104

Every signal since 24 July, tick-honest. A 50% win rate that still loses money is the signature of an exit whose losers are bigger than its winners — hold that thought.

3 · Root cause, attempt one: is it the ENTRY? Three treatments, all of them fail

The obvious diagnosis is that the gate keeps re-firing into a move that has already beaten it — it stops out, and then immediately shorts the same push again. That is a testable claim and it produces three concrete fixes, all three of which the operator has asked for by name at some point: cool off after a stop, bench after a run of stops, and demand more aggressor flow before firing at all. Every cell below is scored against 4,000 random draws that remove the same number of signals at random. That column is the one that matters: a filter that drops 30% of a losing book will usually look like it helped, and the placebo is what tells you whether it actually did anything a coin could not.

3.1   Cool off after a stop — every cell loses, and the best one is a coin flip

Thirteen cells. Not one is positive, and not one clears its placebo. The best cell (5 minutes) still loses money and sits at the 54th percentile of pure noise — i.e. 46% of random cuts of the same size did better. Longer cooldowns get steadily worse because they throw away winners: at 60 minutes it has binned 30 winning signals to avoid 20 losing ones.

3.2   Bench after a run of stops — the “wall of STOP” rule, priced

This one is worth dwelling on, because the scope explicitly asks the report to settle it. “Three stopped trades in a row, bench the gate” is a textbook desk rule and it is directly opposed to the “arm for periods” finding Part 1 confirmed in live money. Here it is applied mechanically to all 104 signals:

Fifteen cells, every one of them worse than doing nothing, and the best (bench for the rest of the session after three stops) is the 54th percentile of noise. Note the bottom five rows: a four-stop trigger never fires at all in 104 signals, so the rule people imagine is protecting them is not even switched on.

★ THE CONTRADICTION THE SCOPE ASKED US TO SETTLE

“Bench on a wall of stops” and “arm for periods” cannot both be right, and on this gate the wall-of-stops rule is simply wrong. Every version of it loses money here, and the reason is visible in the “signals cut” column: benching after two stops for an hour removes 18 winners to avoid 12 losers. The losing streak does not predict the next signal; it just tells you the last two went badly. Part 1 reached the same verdict from the live book — bench-on-2-stops would have cost the desk −$172 across this week and forfeited +$356 on Friday alone. Two independent populations, same answer. The discriminator is the REGIME the losses happened in, never the count of losses.

3.3   Demand more flow — the treatment that looked like the fix

The third entry treatment is the gate’s own net_min knob: the minimum net aggressor flow the tape must show before it is allowed to fade. Raise it and you fire less often but supposedly better. This is the one cell in the entire battery that beat its placebo, and the next section is entirely about why it still is not a fix.

net_min 400 turns a −$158 book into +$483 and sits at the 97.6th percentile of its placebo. On this table it is the answer. It is not the answer.

4 · ★ The autopsy on the only winner — net_min 400 is already switched on

Here is the trap, and it is a good one, because the number was real and the conclusion drawn from it would have been completely wrong. The live gate already runs a net_min of 400. The 104-signal population the first grid scored includes signals the live gate would never have taken, so “apply net_min 400” was not a proposed change at all — it was the study re-discovering the filter that is already deployed. Once you take the live floor as the baseline and ask the only question that is actually actionable — should the floor go HIGHER? — the effect evaporates:

Nine floors above the deployed 400. Every single one is worse than leaving it alone, and none of them clears a 95th-percentile placebo.

★ REV2 — the section that exists to expose a baseline error was repeating it in its own table

The cut and winners forgone columns were measured from the UNFILTERED 87, which is exactly the baseline this section was written to reject. net_min 450 shows cut 30 / $640.0 forgone — but 17 of those 30 and $163.5 of that $640.0 are cut by the floor already deployed. Against the live 400 floor, 450 cuts 13 more signals and forgoes $476.5 of winners to dodge $227.0 of losers. The three starred columns above are the incremental ones and they are the only ones you can act on.

The closing sentence carried the same error and now reads: by net_min 900 the floor has thrown away $2,761 of winning signals to dodge $2,858 of losing ones → by net_min 900 the floor has thrown away $2,597.5 of winning signals ON TOP of what the live floor already cuts, to dodge $2,054.0 more of losing ones — 66 extra signals cut to leave n=4. It forgoes $543.5 more than it saves, which is precisely the $556.5 → $13.0 collapse in the net column.

The verdict is unchanged — every higher floor is worse, on both baselines. Only the numbers quoted for it were on the wrong one.

Three more checks, all pointing the same way. First, the correlation. Above the live floor, the relationship between how much flow a signal had and how much money it made is r = 0.059 on n = 70 (t = 0.49). That is nothing. More flow does not mean a better trade. Second, the quintiles. If the knob worked at all, the money would climb as you go up the flow ranking. It does not — it zig-zags:

quintile of flownet rangennet $$/signalwin %stopped %
Q1405–45114$104.5$7.4650.0%50.0%
Q2463–51114$295.5$21.1157.1%42.9%
Q3513–55514−$340.5−$24.3242.9%57.1%
Q4563–70114$575.0$41.0771.4%28.6%
Q5706–121714−$78.0−$5.5742.9%57.1%

The best quintile (Q4) and the worst (Q3) are adjacent, and the very highest-flow quintile is a loser. There is no monotone anything here — this is noise with five buckets drawn on it.

Third, and this is what actually kills it: the era confound. The 17 signals that the “winning” floor removes are worth −$641.0 and contain 6 winners — but 12 of those 17 come from a single era. The filter is not selecting for weak flow; it is selecting for a stretch of calendar. That is a date filter wearing a flow filter’s clothes, and it will not generalise by construction.

Honest instrument note. Of the 104 signals, 87 have usable tape; 17 fall on 2026-07-21, 2026-07-22, 2026-07-23, days the rolling capture window no longer holds, and those carry −$73.5. Every exit number in §6 and §7 is therefore on n = 87, not 104, and the live comparison is restated on the same 87 so the two sides are the same population.

5 · The absorption veto — a fake win, caught by the winners test

The fourth treatment delays the entry a few seconds and refuses it if price has already gone against us by more than a couple of points — the same shape as the 55-second veto that works on the abs_veto family. On this gate it fails, and it fails in the most instructive way: by winning through the destruction of winners.

The bottom row is worth a line on its own: restricting the gate to the US session (≥13:00Z) makes it materially WORSE (−$382.50, 6.7th percentile), which means whatever this gate is doing right, it is doing some of it overnight.

7 · ★★ Root cause, attempt two: it was never the entry. It is the EXIT.

Every entry treatment failed. That is not a dead end — it is a result, and it points straight at the other half of the trade. If the signals themselves were worthless, then how they are exited should not matter much; you would lose money slowly whatever you did. So the study swept the exit on the same 87 tape-backed signals, holding the entries exactly where the live gate put them, walking the real tick path in order, checking the stop before the target on every tick, at $1.50 a round trip and $2.00 a point.

The result is not subtle.

The live gate on these same 87 signals is −$84.50. The top twelve cells of a 64-cell decoupled sweep, both legs in ATR units so widening the stop does not silently widen the target too. Every one survives dropping its best day and stripping its best three trades with thousands still on the table.

7.1   Is it one hot square? No — it is a ramp, and that is the important part

A single brilliant cell surrounded by rubble is the classic curve-fit tell, and the FIRST exit sweep produced exactly that shape — which is why a second, decoupled sweep was run. Here is the stop held at 1.5×ATR and the target walked out, so you can see the whole line rather than the winner:

targetnet $$/signalwin %target / stop / time-outworst LODOstrip-3
0.5×ATR$672.21$7.7385.1%74 / 13 / 0$271.1$408.1
0.75×ATR$1,307.34$15.0377.0%67 / 20 / 0$743.6$906.6
1.0×ATR$2,065.29$23.7473.6%64 / 23 / 0$1,375.9$1,528.0
1.25×ATR$1,743.75$20.0464.4%55 / 27 / 5$1,048.5$1,080.6
1.5×ATR$1,802.89$20.7259.8%49 / 30 / 8$938.8$1,005.3
2.0×ATR$2,678.28$30.7855.2%39 / 31 / 17$1,337.9$1,611.8
2.5×ATR$3,289.74$37.8154.0%31 / 31 / 25$1,629.0$2,035.1
3.0×ATR$3,459.98$39.7754.0%22 / 31 / 34$1,626.5$2,046.1

The line climbs almost the whole way and the win rate falls the whole way — from 85% at a half-ATR target down to 54% at three — while the money goes UP fivefold. That is the shape of a gate whose edge lives in a few big continuations and whose current exit is clipping them. Note what happens to the exit mix: at the far target, 34 of 87 trades end on the clock rather than at either price, so this is as much “stop scalping and hold” as it is “widen the stop”.

7.2   The control that makes this believable — random entries under the same exit

Here is the objection, and it is the right objection: a wide stop and a far target will flatter ANY entry on a trending tape — you are not measuring the gate, you are measuring the exit policy. That is testable. The study fired 60 random entries through each exit policy and compared:

exit policythe REAL entriestarget / stop / timerandom meanrandom p95random bestreal %ileentry alpha
stop 1.5×ATR / target 3.0×ATR
(the §7 headline cell — 2.0R of a 1.5×ATR stop IS 3.0×ATR)		$3,465.4822 / 31 / 34−$2.30$2,350.83$3,038.82100.0$3,467.78
stop 1.5×ATR / target 1.125×ATR (0.75R)$1,860.9259 / 25 / 3−$294.31$1,297.36$1,524.57100.0$2,155.23
stop 1.0×ATR / target 0.75×ATR (0.75R)−$186.0450 / 37 / 0−$420.67$738.58$883.7665.0$234.63
stop 20pt / target 24pt / hold 300$336.0036 / 32 / 19−$253.66$910.50$1,204.0085.0$589.66
stop 8pt / target 12pt / hold 120−$245.5031 / 46 / 10−$251.53$154.50$182.0046.7$6.03

This is the load-bearing table of the section. Under the wide-stop policies the real entries beat all 60 random draws, and the random draws themselves average about zero — so the exit policy is NOT what is making the money. The entry is. Note the last row: under the tight 8pt/12pt scalp the real entries land at the 47th percentile, i.e. indistinguishable from firing at random. The gate's edge is invisible at the exit the desk actually uses.

★ REV2 — why this control says $3,465.48 and §7 says $3,459.98

They are the SAME policy and the gap is $5.50 across 87 signals — six cents a signal. Two things were making one result look like two. One, the units. This table labelled the target in R-multiples of the stop and §7 labels it in ATR; 2.0R of a 1.5×ATR stop is 3.0×ATR. Both now read in ATR. Two, the entry price. A paired control has to enter the real book the same way it enters its random books — at the TAPE price at the signal’s own timestamp — so that our execution quality cannot flatter the real leg; §7 enters at the live recorded fill. The proof that this is the whole difference: both runs exit the identical 87 signals identically, 22 target / 31 stop / 34 time-out in each. Same trades, same exits, entry prices a few ticks apart.

Quote $3,459.98 — the one measured off our own fills, which is what §7, §8, §9, the front scorecard and BUILD #1 all use. The $3,467.78 entry alpha is correctly computed inside this control’s own book ($3,465.48 real, less the −$2.30 random mean); on the live-fill basis it is $3,462.28. Neither number touches the finding, which is the percentile: 60 of 60.

So this is not purely a July result being projected onto August — it is positive on the week the report covers, on the days the report can actually see.

8 · The verdict pills

Why this is SHADOW on Monday and not a deploy

Three reasons, and I would rather write them down than have them found later. (1) The sweep never turns over. The best cell is the widest stop and the furthest target in the grid — 2.5×ATR and 3×ATR are both at the boundary, so the true optimum is somewhere outside what was tested and the shipped number would be a guess. (2) The repricer fills at the exact stop and the exact target with no slippage, and a 2.5×ATR stop on this instrument is a wide, thin price to be filled at in the moves where it gets hit. (3) Part 2 graded the live fade-scalp trial on this same gate and it lost −$480 over 80 legs, with the damage concentrated on big-trend rungs. That is a different exit family, but it is a live warning that this gate’s exit behaves differently in the wild than in a repricer. A shadow slate costs nothing and settles all three.

★ REV2 — and it has now been graded against the OTHER exhaustion_short exit in this report

Part 2 §8d proposes a different exit for the same gate — a tight chandelier on both lots for the trend rungs — and wrote it to data/exit_overrides_proposed.json. The two studies used different populations and different engines, so their headline numbers were never comparable. Both have now been run on the SAME 87 tape-backed signals, the same tick paths, the same cap, split by §8b’s rung.

rungnWIDE 1.5×ATR stop / 3.0×ATR targetTIGHT CHANDELIER ×2

MED-TREND19+$2,018.44 (+$106.2/sig)−$27.80 (−$1.5/sig)
SCALP-CHOP29+$533.64 (+$18.4/sig)−$38.27 (−$1.3/sig)

same random-entry control 100th percentile, alpha +$3,433.2758th percentile, alpha +$69.37

The wide stop wins the head-to-head outright and the tight chandelier does not survive its own control. At the 58th percentile against paired random entries it is indistinguishable from firing at random on this population, which is the same test the live scalp exit failed at 47. It is not the 15-minute cap doing it either: run both on the 5-second tape at 15, 30 and 60 minutes and the wide stop pays +$40.21 / +$47.30 / +$45.96 a signal while the chandelier pays −$3.60 / −$6.02 / −$6.45 — a longer leash makes the chandelier WORSE.

So one proposal goes in the shadow book, not two: the wide stop. To be fair to §8d, its cells were measured on the 716-entry exhaustion_rev shadow board, not on the live gate’s own 87 signals — that is a different population and I am not calling it wrong on its own. But a deploy would act on the LIVE gate’s entries, and on those the chandelier is negative on all four rungs at every horizon. Full working in Part 3 Q2.

Cooldown after a stop (13 cells)REFUTEDNamed test: 4,000-draw random-removal placebo. Every cell loses money; best cell sits at the 54th percentile of noise. No reformulation survives — the mechanism assumes the last stop predicts the next signal, and it does not.

All nine higher floors are worse than the deployed floor, r = 0.059 between flow and outcome, quintiles non-monotone, and 12 of the 17 signals the ‘winning’ floor cuts come from a single era. It is a date filter.

Bench exhaustion_short outrightREJECTED as prematureThe obvious call, and the study says it is wrong: the entry carries measurable alpha and the exit is a fixable, testable thing. Bench the EXIT, not the gate.
abs_veto (both sides) rehabilitationNOT RUN — measurement gapThe rehab phase timed out before reaching it. −$58.50 over 19 signals this week is too thin to grade anyway. Carry to next week with the same battery.
capitulation_long rehabilitationNOT RUN — measurement gapTwo lots, −$48.00, 0% win this week. The phase never reached it. Movement 2 does cover its require_flip clause on run tape (SHADOW) — that is the only reading it got.

P2 THE SHADOW DESK & PROMOTION — week of 10–14 August 2026

Gaz, the headline this week is a correction to my own brief, and it is worth more than any new candidate. The thing I was told to put forward for promotion on Monday is already live, and has been since 25 July. abs_veto_55s is top of the shadow board all-time (+$4,022 over 342 trades) and top of it this week (+$575 over 23) — but the live desk has been running it two-sided as abs_veto_long and abs_veto_short for three weeks. So there is nothing to promote there. Writing it up as a promotion candidate would have been a wasted play.

What I found instead when I checked is the real story: the live short side and its shadow twin have come apart by $2,349 over the same fifteen days. Same gate, same 55-second veto, same tape. Shadow abs_veto_55s SHORT made +$1,828.50; live abs_veto_short lost −$520.50. The long side tracks fine (+$853.50 shadow vs +$451 live — same sign, live about half). It is only the short side that has broken away, and I can tell you exactly where it goes: our stops cost us −$48.80 a leg live against −$29.20 in shadow, while our winners are the same size. We are not picking worse trades. We are losing more on the ones that go wrong.

THE ONE-PARAGRAPH VERSION

SURVIVED: abs_veto_55s as a mechanism — 18 of 25 days green (★ REV2: was “16 of 21”, which is the battery script's figure — the same script §2 Leg 2 shows does not reconcile, because it drops all four Sunday sessions. Re-derived straight from shadow.db: 25 sessions, 18 green, best +$791.00, worst −$198.00, 342 trades, +$4,022.00), positive in BOTH regimes (chop +$1,746 / trend +$2,276), positive in BOTH walk-forward halves, positive on BOTH sides (LONG +$1,371 / SHORT +$2,651), and +$11.76 a trade against −$0.07 for the un-vetoed thrust it is built on. It is already live; this is confirmation, not an action. THE ACTION: the live SHORT side’s exit ladder (1.5R/2.5R) is too wide — it converts winners into stop-outs (36.5% win live vs 49.2% shadow) and its average loss is 33% bigger. Tune the SIDE, do not relegate the direction — the shadow says short is the STRONGER of the two. DIED: the Open Rider day-classifier. Eight pre-session features, 272 candidate rules, and the best one is beaten by 40% of pure-noise searches once you price in the hunting. Also died: the idea that ONE day-detector could serve both the Open Rider and the MGC run-catcher — the two instruments’ good days agree 38% of the time against 50% by chance. THE HONEST GAP: both grind-exit shadow ledgers are frozen. grind_long has not placed a trade since 5 August, so the two-ratchet clip watch and the 2R-partial smoothness watch have no new data at all this week and cannot say anything.

THE OVER-TRADING CAVEAT, BAKED IN

The shadow board overstates and always will. A sim takes every signal its rule generates with no position limit, no benching, no router, no slippage beyond the repricing, and no operator watching. The live desk takes a fraction of them. thrust_loose fired 611 times in four weeks; the live tournament took 58 signals in the whole of this week. So the board nominates — it tells you which mechanism is worth a live slot — and the paper desk judges. A shadow sim's dollar total is never the dollar total you would have banked, and section 3 below is a $2,349 demonstration of exactly that.

2 · THE PROMOTION BATTERY — abs_veto_55s, run in full

I ran scripts/abs_veto_robustness.py as instructed and I am presenting all five legs plus the two-sided split the script does not do. The verdict up front: the mechanism survives everything I threw at it. But read section 3 before you conclude that the live version is fine, because it is not.

Leg 1 — headline

SimNNet $Win %Avg winAvg lossWorst$/tradeMax DD
abs_veto_55s342+$4,02247+$67−$38−$92+$11.76−$441

A 47% win rate with wins nearly twice the size of losses. Per your standing rule I am judging this on expectancy, not hit rate — and $11.76 a trade with a worst-ever trade of −$92 is a tight, well-behaved distribution. The max drawdown of −$441 against a +$4,022 total is the number I like most here.

Leg 2 — per-day spread (is it one lucky day?)

18 of 25 days green. Best day +$791, worst day −$198. The seven red days total −$826.50 against +$4,848.50 from the eighteen green ones. There is no single day whose removal changes the verdict: take out the best day and it still makes +$3,231. The worst day in four weeks costs less than 5% of the total — that is a very flat distribution for a gate with a 47% hit rate.

Leg 3 — regime split (this is the one that matters)

Regime at entryNNet $Win %$/tradeMax DD
Chop (ER < 0.18)163+$1,74647+$10.70−$343
Trend (ER ≥ 0.18)179+$2,27648+$12.70−$359

This is the strongest single result in the section. Almost every edge this desk has found turns out to be a regime accident — it works in trend and dies in chop, or the reverse, and then a week of the other regime arrives and wipes it out. This one is flat across both: +$10.70 a trade in chop, +$12.70 in trend, near-identical win rates and near-identical drawdowns. That is what a structural edge looks like as opposed to a lucky tape, and it is why the veto deserved its live slot.

Both halves positive, which is the test passing. But be honest about the shape: the second half earns a third less per trade than the first. That is either normal decay of a small edge, or the start of something going stale. One more week tells us which; I would not act on it yet, but I would watch it.

Caveats, stated plainly. 1-lot sims with no position limit. A single summer regime — four weeks, one tape, no autumn, no event day. The exit mix is concentrated: 160 TARGET exits carry +$8,979 and 177 STOPs give back −$4,567, so the whole edge is “the target gets hit slightly more often than the stop”, which is a thin thing to rest on. And five ABSORPTION_CUT exits lost −$390 between them (0% win) — a small, ugly tail worth watching.

3 · ★ THE FINDING — live and shadow have come apart on the short side

Because the promotion was already done, I spent the time on a question nobody had asked: is the live version actually behaving like the shadow one it was promoted from? The long side does. The short side does not, and the gap is large enough that it should change what we do on Saturday.

Window is 26 July onward — the day the live gate started filling.

The long side is fine — live earns about half what shadow does, which is exactly the over-trading haircut you would expect. The short side is upside down. Shadow says short is the better of the two sides; live says it is the only losing one.
