Our winners are the same size as shadow's. Our losers are a third bigger. Live TARGET pays +$64.70 against shadow's +$61.90 — identical for practical purposes. Live STOP costs −$48.80 against shadow's −$29.20. And the win rate is 36.5% live against 49.2% shadow, on the same signals. Both facts point the same way and it is not the entry:

THE DIAGNOSIS

The live short side runs a wider profit ladder than the long side — from data/exit_overrides.json, abs_veto_short is A 1.5R / B 2.5R while abs_veto_long is A 1.0R / B 1.5R. A wider target does not widen the stop, but it does change which trades reach it: positions that would have banked at 1.0R are still open at 1.5R, and a chunk of them travel all the way back to the stop instead. That converts winners into full-size losers, which is precisely the signature in the table — same winner size, more of them turning into losers, bigger average loss. The short side's entry is the best thing on the shadow board. Its exit ladder is the widest on the live desk. Those two facts do not belong together.

★ REV2 — I inferred that from the exit-reason mix and never measured a single fill. Now measured: it is the ladder, not the fills.

The objection is fair and it is this desk’s own standing finding: live losses run 1.1–3.1× modelled, and “we get filled worse than the repricer assumes” produces exactly the table above — same winner size, bigger losers. So I measured every measurable live leg against the level it was supposed to fill at. R is stop_atr_mult (=1.0) × the ATR-14 the gate would have read at the entry minute, recomputed with deciders._atr’s own halt-aware rule; the target level is the ladder that was genuinely running (plain 1.5R/2.5R before 2 August, the quiet-tape clip — Lot A a flat $40, Lot B max(1.75R, $60) — after it).

exitlegsmeasuredmedian slipmean slipp90worst$ cost
STOP3535−0.95pt+0.68pt+10.80pt+19.84pt−$47.32
TARGET1111−0.25pt−4.32pt+2.25pt+3.25pt+$95.11
every measurable leg4646−0.60pt−0.52pt  +$47.78 in our favour

Positive = filled worse than the level. Across 46 legs the desk fills a median 0.60 points BETTER than the repricer assumes, worth +$47.78 over the window. 24 of the 46 filled at the level or better. The tail the standing finding warns about is real but small: 12 stop legs filled more than a point beyond their stop and cost −$194.18 between them, and the other 23 gave $146.86 back. Re-running the shadow twin with the measured slippage applied moves it from +$1,828.50 to +$1,965.84 — the gap gets marginally WIDER, not narrower.

Verdict: the exit-ladder diagnosis stands. Fills explain none of it, so the forward A/B on the ladder is testing the right thing. Full working in Part 3 Q3.

One correction to the headline gap while I am here. The “63 legs / −$520.50” above includes two quarantined rows (EXCLUDE:md_stream_atr_corruption_20260804, the two MAX_HOLD legs, −$255.50). On the clean ledger the live short side is −$265.00 over 61 legs and the gap is $2,093.50, not $2,349. The normalisation below already strips those two legs, so its −$159.00 strategy-only figure is right either way — it is only the headline that double-counts rows the rest of this report excludes.

Normalising the software failures first, per the standing rehab rule

Before blaming strategy, strip the malfunctions. Two STOP_UNFILLED legs (−$106) and two MAX_HOLD legs (−$255.50) are handling faults, not the gate's opinion about the market:

Live abs_veto_shortNet $
As booked−$520.50
Add back STOP_UNFILLED (2 legs)+$106.00
Add back MAX_HOLD (2 legs)+$255.50
Strategy-only result−$159.00

So the short side is a −$159 strategy problem wearing a −$520 coat. Seventy percent of the headline loss is four legs of plumbing. That does not make it fine — −$159 against a shadow twin's +$1,828.50 is still a wide gap — but it changes the size of the fix we should be looking for.

One hypothesis I tested and rejected. The abs_veto_short ATR ≥ 16 floor was deleted on 1 August, and the live short's bleed does start right after: +$13.80 a leg before (26 legs, 4 days) against −$23.80 a leg after (37 legs, 9 days). Tempting. But the shadow counterfactual does not support it — since 1 August the entries that floor would have blocked (ATR < 16) are positive in shadow: +$233.50 over 38 trades. The deleted floor let in a weaker population, not a losing one. So the floor is a dilution story at most, and the exit ladder remains the better explanation. Restoring the floor is not my recommendation.

4 · RELEGATION — tune the SIDE, never relegate a DIRECTION

The standing principle says a gate runs two-sided with independently-tuned per-side thresholds, and that you never relegate a direction because it had a bad week — you tune the side. This week gives that rule its cleanest demonstration yet, from fresh numbers.

Live gateSideLegsNet $ (all-time)Win %$/legRead
abs_veto_longLONG102+$451.0044.1+$4.40working — hold
abs_veto_shortSHORT63−$520.5036.5−$8.30tune the exit, do not drop the side
exhaustion_shortSHORT146−$158.0047.9−$1.10near flat — the exit trial is the issue (§7)
rgv_longLONG60−$691.0046.7−$11.50router-only, per the standing call
rgv_shortSHORT43−$416.0044.2−$9.70regime-dependent, not defective
grind_longLONG98−$454.0031.6−$4.60silent since 5 Aug — see §6
capitulation_longLONG32−$85.5034.4−$2.70barely trades now
nipc_shortSHORT16−$337.006.2−$21.101 winner in 16 — thin, but ugly

If we judged by direction we would relegate the short side of abs_veto this weekend, and it would be the worst decision we made all month. The shadow board says that same short side, on the same signals, is the best-performing thing we own (+$2,651 all-time, +$15.50 a trade, 49.7% win). The direction is not the problem. The side's exit ladder is. That is the two-sided-gate rule doing real work rather than being quoted.

What I would actually do, stated as a proposal and not a deploy: bring abs_veto_short's ladder in from 1.5R/2.5R towards the long side's 1.0R/1.5R, and run it as a forward A/B against the current wide ladder rather than switching outright. The evidence for the tighter ladder is a diagnosis (winner-size identical, loser-size 33% worse, win rate 13 points worse), not a backtest of the narrower setting, and I would rather prove it forward on paired legs than assert it now.

⚠ A counting note that applies to this whole table: the live desk runs a two-lot A/B scale-out, so a single signal writes two rows. Everything above is counted in LEGS, not signals. The A and B legs are a scale-out of one decision, not an experiment with two opinions — so “63 short legs” is roughly 32 short decisions, and every per-leg figure is about half a per-signal figure.

5 · ★★ THE OPEN RIDER — is a juicy day callable BEFORE the session commits?

Your framing: “open rider best shadow again ... but i know they bleed on other days. thats where im saying we need detection some how to block open rider if conditions arent juicy.” You are right on both halves and I verified both from real_pnl before going anywhere near a detector.

First — confirming the shape you described

DayNNet $Win %$/tradeCharacter
2026-08-1016−$1,076.5012.5−$67.28bled
2026-08-1118+$59.0044.4+$3.28scratch-green
2026-08-1219−$1,147.5026.3−$60.39bled
2026-08-1315+$1,688.5073.3+$112.57owned the board
2026-08-1419−$2.5042.1−$0.13flat
Week87−$479.0039.1−$5.51ungated cells 54–57

Two green days paid +$1,747.50 across 33 trades. Three red days lost −$2,226.50 across 54. Add the 19 gated trades and the family is −$481.50 over 106, which is the −$482 in your note. The average hides the strategy completely — a −$5.51-a-trade line conceals a strategy that pays +$112 a trade on its day and loses $60–67 a trade on the wrong one.

The oracle. Trading only the green days is +$1,747.50 a week, several times over your “few hundred a week” bar. It cheats outright — the labels only exist after the close — so it is a ceiling, not a plan. But it does settle the important question: the prize is real and the whole problem is day selection, not entry timing and not exit tuning.

Escaping n=5 — the replay

Five days cannot test a day-classifier. But the Open Rider is a pure clock rider: no signal, no gate. Every N minutes between 13:00 and 14:45 UTC it goes with the sign of the last 15 minutes, stop at 2.0×/3.0×ATR, target coupled at 2R, 45-minute cap. That is fully deterministic, so I replayed it over every day of tape we hold — 41 trading days, 19 June to 14 August, on 5-second bars from the V5 archive + lake + hot union (scripts/odr_day_classifier.py). That turns 5 day-labels into 41.

Before using it I checked it against the five real forward days:

DayReal NReal $Replay NReplay $Sign agrees?
08-1016−$1,076.5022−$1,180.50yes
08-1118+$59.0018−$285.10no (both near zero)
08-1219−$1,147.5021−$916.20yes
08-1315+$1,688.5013+$1,420.40yes
08-1419−$2.5018+$301.10no (both near zero)
Total87−$47992−$660−$5.51 vs −$7.18 /tr

Honest read of the validation: good on the days that matter, poor on the days that don't. The three days with real money in them all match in sign and roughly in size. The two mismatches are both days where the true answer is “about zero” and the replay says “about zero the other way”. Overall level is close and slightly pessimistic. Good enough to hunt a day-classifier on; not good enough to quote a replay dollar total as if it were money. Across all 41 days the replay makes +$19,247 on 753 trades with 24 green days — a number I am deliberately not putting forward as an edge, because it is in-sample on an optimistic 5-second-bar fill model.

The hunt — eight causal features, and the trap

Everything below is measured strictly before 13:00 UTC, the instant the rider's first entry fires. That is the causality line and I held to it. I searched every feature against a threshold in both directions — 272 candidate day-rules in total.

Pre-13:00 featureRank corr. with day $Best rule foundDays keptRule $Naive placebo
Gap from prior close+0.057keep ≥ 4.475pt36+$23,2791.4%
ATR-14 at 13:00+0.140keep ≥ 9.48pt33+$22,1043.5%
Run-in efficiency (2h)−0.249keep ≤ 0.17937+$21,0718.3%
Overnight roundtrip+0.232keep ≥ 11.2429+$20,0475.3%
Overnight efficiency−0.171keep ≤ 0.04725+$18,8725.1%
Overnight range−0.084keep ≥ 224.8pt35+$19,53116.5%
Overnight net move−0.143keep ≤ 277.8pt33+$18,82617.9%
Run-in range (2h)+0.056keep ≤ 207pt35+$21,4485.3%

Read the “naive placebo” column and it looks like we have something: the gap rule is beaten by only 1.4% of random day-removals of the same size, ATR by 3.5%. That column is a trap and I nearly walked into it. It tests the winning rule as if I had picked it in advance. I did not — I searched 272 of them and kept the best. With 272 tries, a 1-in-70 result is roughly what noise produces on its own.

THE SEARCH-AWARE PLACEBO — and it kills the classifier

The correct control is to run the entire search against shuffled day labels and ask how often the hunt finds something this good on pure noise. I did that 2,000 times (scripts/odr_day_placebo2.py):

 Best rule found
The real search (gap ≥ 4.475)+$23,279
Same search on shuffled labels — median+$22,819
Same search on shuffled labels — 90th percentile+$25,137
Same search on shuffled labels — max of 2,000+$28,887

The real search is beaten by 40.0% of pure-noise searches. It sits almost exactly on the median of what random data produces. There is no day-classifier here. Not a weak one — none.

Why this was never provable this week anyway — the power limit

The day-to-day standard deviation of Open Rider P&L across the 41 replayed days is $1,384. Against that noise:

To detect an edge of…Days needed (~80% power)Weeks
$300 a day16733
$500 a day6012
$800 a day235

The forward book has five days. That is 8% of what a $500-a-day effect needs. So the deliverable here was always going to be the method plus what would prove it — not a shipped rule — and I want to be clear that the negative result above is a genuine negative on 41 days, not a shrug about five.

What WOULD prove it

  1. Pre-register one feature, not eight. The search is what killed this. Pick the single most mechanically plausible one — my vote is ATR-14 at 13:00, the only feature with both a positive rank correlation and a physical story (a dead tape cannot pay a momentum rider) — fix the threshold at 9.5pt now, and let it run forward untouched. One pre-registered rule needs no multiple-comparison discount.
  2. Give it 60 sessions. Twelve weeks at the current trade rate. That is the honest cost of an answer at the effect size that would matter.
  3. Judge it on the days it removes, not the total. Log, per skipped day, what the rider would have made. A day-filter that removes eight days and misses two of the best is a bad filter even if the total goes up.

★★ Could ONE day-classifier serve both the Open Rider and the MGC run-catcher?

This was the most valuable question in the brief, because the same shape turned up twice this week on two instruments: the Open Rider lives on a minority of days, and docs/MGC_LEADS_2026-08-14.md found gold's run-catcher does too (run-days pay, the rest bleed). If the same days are juicy on both, one detector is worth more than either gate. I tested it on the 16 days where we hold both MNQ and MGC tape (scripts/odr_mgc_shared_day.py), pricing gold correctly at $10.00/point.

TestResultMeaning
Green odr day = big MGC run-day?38% agreechance agreement is 50% — 12 points WORSE than a coin
Rank corr. (odr day $, MGC run $)−0.129nil, and faintly negative
Rank corr. (odr day $, MGC day range)−0.129nil
Rank corr. (odr day $, MGC day efficiency)−0.341gold's cleanest days are mildly BAD for the rider
MNQ ATR at 13:00 → MGC run size+0.213the only faint positive; nowhere near usable

The answer is no. A good day for the MNQ Open Rider is not a good day for the MGC run-catcher; if anything they lean slightly opposite. Two strategies with the same shape of problem do not have the same solution. Gold needs its own detector built on gold's tape, exactly as the MGC notes already insist about thresholds. This is 16 days, so it is a PARK and not a refutation — but it is 16 days pointing consistently in one direction, and it should stop us building a shared detector on the assumption the days coincide.

The drift gate (sims 60–63) — live one day, and it has not fired

CellUngated $Ungated NGated $Gated N
odr_c10_s20−$141.505−$141.505
odr_c10_s30+$226.004+$226.004
odr_c5_s20−$8.505−$8.505
odr_c5_s30−$78.505−$78.505
Total (08-14 only)−$2.5019−$2.5019

Identical, cell by cell, trade for trade. The gate has rejected exactly zero entries. The gated arms have existed for one day (14 August) and on that day the drift detector never disagreed with the rider's own direction, so nothing was blocked. That is not evidence the gate works and it is not evidence it doesn't — it is one day of no information.

⚠ Never compare gated against ungated on all-time totals. The gated arms cover one day and the ungated five; an all-time comparison would measure the calendar and report it as a gate effect. The only legitimate comparison is the overlapping window, which is the table above. What would prove it: a day where the drift detector confirms a direction and the rider's 15-minute momentum points the other way. Until that day happens the gate is untested by construction.

The secondary axis — stop width, and why I am not leading with it

StopNNet $Win %$/trade
2.0×ATR58−$874.5031.0−$15.08
3.0×ATR48+$393.0050.0+$8.19

Real, and it points the same way on the replay (41 days: 3.0× makes +$29.59 a trade against +$22.61, at a 50.3% win rate against 41.8%). The wider stop also beats the tighter one on 4 of the 5 days. But look at the sizes before acting on it:

EffectSpread
DAY effect (best day − worst day)$2,836
STOP-WIDTH effect (3.0× − 2.0×)$1,268

The day effect is 2.2× the stop-width effect. Tuning the stop while the day problem is unsolved is polishing the smaller lever, and I would rather say that than lead with the tuning because the tuning is the part that has a tidy number attached.

⚠ Every Open Rider arm is flagged n < 40, below this desk's threshold for a forward A/B to claim anything. No promotion follows from this week, on any cell, in either direction.

★ REV2 — the card asked for a LIVE change here and this section forbids one. The card was wrong, and it was pointed at the wrong engine.

Rev 1 carried “Open Rider: stop at 3.0×ATR, not 2.0×” in the MONDAY at-the-desk window, mode config, owner “day rider config”. Two things are wrong with that.

One: this section says no promotion follows, and it means it. Every arm is n < 40 and the DAY effect is 2.2× the stop effect. A play cannot cite a section whose own conclusion is “no action, in either direction”.

Two — and this is the serious one: the Open Rider is NOT the live day rider. They are different strategies with different stops. The Open Rider (odr_*) is a shadow family: it re-enters every N minutes between 13:00 and 14:45 UTC on the sign of the last 15 minutes, with a stop at 2.0×/3.0×ATR, a coupled 2R target and a 45-minute cap — 87 shadow trades this week. The live day rider (day_rider.py) takes one position a session on the drift detector, has no ATR stop of that kind at all, and exits on the ATR-scaled trail (arm 4×ATR, trail 2×ATR off peak, ARM_ATR_MULT/TRAIL_ATR_MULT in the module) or the 20:40 UTC hard flat — 5 live trades this week, 5 winners, +$1,617.50. Typing a 3.0×ATR stop into “day rider config” would have changed the desk’s only profitable engine on the strength of a different strategy’s shadow arms.

The play is now BUILD, as a third shadow arm at 3.0×ATR beside the existing 2.0×, which is what the evidence supports and what it costs nothing to run. Nothing on the live rider changes.

6 · GRIND EXIT SHADOW — the two-ratchet runner-clip watch

I ran scripts/two_ratchet_shadow_watch.py without --ping as instructed. The result is dominated by one fact that has nothing to do with the two-ratchet:

⚠ THE LEDGER IS FROZEN — grind_long HAS NOT TRADED SINCE 5 AUGUST

The grind family's last live fill was 2026-08-05. Not a single grind trade this week, or last. So both grind-exit shadow ledgers contain zero new data since the previous report, and neither can say anything new. I checked this was not a tooling artefact: the 5-second tape is complete through 14 August (the loader unions V5 archive + lake + capture, and every weekday 10–14 August is present), so the silence is real — the gate is not firing, the watch is not broken.

On the verdict logic in the scope, zero clips over a genuine trend week would be evidence for reconsidering the two-ratchet. I cannot give you that, because there was no trend week — there was no week at all. Three runners appeared, all before 5 August, and on all three the two-ratchet actually did better than the live exit (+$144 vs +$23, +$348 vs +$244, +$62 vs +$53). Yet it still finished $294 behind overall. Where it lost is instructive: two trades on 30 July where the live book records +$159 and +$156 while the reconstruction says −$31 and −$58.

⚠ INSTRUMENT WARNING — DO NOT ACT ON THE −$294

The watch prints its own cross-check: reconstructed-pure minus actual-live = −$406, where zero is correct. The instrument's error is larger than the signal it is measuring. Five of 26 trades carry the whole discrepancy, and two of them exited MANUAL_CLAIM — you closed them by hand, which no tape reconstruction can ever reproduce. A third looked worse still: the 30 July 11:11 trade books +$159 live on a trade whose rMFE column reads 0.81R, about $23 a lot at ATR 14.1. Verdict: the two-ratchet comparison is UNMEASURED this window, not lost.

★ REV2 — that “not physically reachable” call was WRONG, and withdrawing it is good news for the live book

Rev 1 said the +$159 trade was not physically reachable on the tape and pointed at the fill mis-attribution mechanism. That is a live-P&L integrity allegation and it deserved a check rather than a footnote, so every one of the 26 grind_long trades in this window has now been scored against its own FULL-LIFE excursion — the highest price the tape printed between that trade’s own recorded entry and its own recorded exit, which is the most generous ceiling there is.

trades checkedbook MORE than their tape could paythe 30 July 11:11 trade
260booked +$159.00 against a ceiling of +$173.50

Nothing in the grind book is unreachable. That trade entered at 27,670.00 and the tape printed 27,757.50 while we held it — 87.5 points, not 11.4. The rMFE column reads 0.81R because two_ratchet_shadow_watch.rmfe() stops measuring at the first 1-ATR adverse tick: it is a pre-stop excursion, not the trade’s excursion. Price dipped an ATR against us early, then ran 87.5 points.

And the exit reconciles exactly to the deployed rule. Peak 87.5pt at ATR 14.1 is 6.21R, past the pure-6.0 chandelier’s lock, so the trail firms to lock_k 0.5×ATR = 7.05pt; 27,757.50 − 7.05 = 27,750.45 and we exited at 27,750.25. The other two large books check out the same way (+$156.50 against a $200.50 ceiling; +$305.00 against $339.50).

So there is no measurement gap on the card and grind_long’s all-time −$454.00 over 98 lots stands. What there IS is an instrument note with teeth: the watch’s rMFE column is truncated by construction and must never be read as “how far this trade went”. Full working in Part 3 Q8.

7 · GRIND EXIT SHADOW — the 2R-partial smoothness choice

scripts/partial_shadow_watch.py, same frozen window, same caveat — nothing after 5 August. It covers 40 legs across the 5 days grind actually traded.

MetricBaseline (deployed 2-lot chandelier)2R-partial (shadow)Difference
Net $−$1,382.40−$1,074.90+$307.50
Daily std $42.383.3worse (+97%)
Max drawdown $−$1,382.40−$1,074.90+$307.50 better
Worst day $−$336.40−$286.00+$50.40 better
Green-day %0%0%no difference

The choice you were being asked to make is not available on this data, and I would rather say so than manufacture a recommendation. The 2R-partial was framed as a variance lever — give up a little mean to get a smoother curve. Over these five days it does the opposite on the headline variance measure: the partial's daily standard deviation is nearly double the baseline's, not lower. It does show a shallower drawdown and a better worst day, and it gives up no mean at all here (it is $307.50 ahead, because banking Lot A early on five straight losing days is simply less bad).

That last clause is the whole problem. Grind lost money on all five days it traded — green-day percentage is zero for both arms. On a book with no winning days, "bank half the position early" wins automatically and tells you nothing about what happens on a runner day, which is the only day the question is actually about. The mean give-up that the original backtest measured (~$665 over 3 weeks) is entirely a tail forfeit on runners, and there are no runners in this sample.

My recommendation: no decision this week. Not "adopt", not "reject" — the data cannot separate them. The 2R-partial stays in shadow. What would make it decidable: grind_long trading again at all, and then a week containing at least a few rMFE ≥ 4R runners.

8 · ★ THE REGIME-FLEX EXIT LAB

8a · Grading the live exhaustion_short fade-scalp trial on actual fills

The trial (Lot A at 0.5R, Lot B at 1.5R) went live on 31 July. Graded on real fills, split by the exit config that was genuinely running at the time:

ConfigLegsNet $Win %TARGETSTOPCHAND
PRE — single slot58+$271.0057142510
DEFAULT — A2.5 + wide chandelier8+$51.0050040
TRIAL — A0.5 / B1.580−$480.004123476

The live trial is losing: −$480 over 80 legs (roughly 40 signals, since these are A/B legs of one decision). But a raw before/after comparison is not fair — the trial ran across different days and different regimes than the period before it. So I repriced every exhaustion entry we hold (717 of them: 105 live signals plus the 612-strong exhaustion_rev shadow board) on the forward tape under both configs, so the only thing that differs is the exit:

RungN0.5R hit1.5R hitStop firstTRIAL $/sigPRIOR $/sigDelta
BIG-TREND2879%64%21%+$22.24+$42.80−$20.56
MED-TREND16962%35%38%−$16.73−$15.15−$1.58
SCALP-CHOP51968%42%32%−$0.57−$1.54+$0.97
ALL71667%41%33%−$3.49−$3.02−$0.48

This is the ladder argument in one table, and it says you were right. Overall the trial is near-neutral (−$0.48 a signal). But it is not uniform: it wins slightly in chop (+$0.97) and it gives up −$20.56 a signal on BIG-TREND, cutting that rung's return roughly in half. Scalping at 0.5R on a day that is genuinely trending throws away the only trades that pay for the rest. A single exit config sprayed across all regimes averages the tape it should be ON with the tape it should be different on — which is exactly the failure the backtest discipline exists to prevent.

8b · The per-rung R grid — and the window extension

I built scripts/exit_ladder_grid.py to sweep Lot-A × Lot-B across 8 × 7 R-combos plus two chandeliers per gate family per rung. Two deliberate departures from the existing adaptive_exit_bt.py, both aimed at the BIG-TREND n problem you flagged:

  1. The tape. The old lab reads capture.db, whose 5-second bars begin 15 July. This one runs on the full 45-day union (19 June – 14 August).
  2. The entries. Added the V5 archive's fut_shadow_sim_trades — 4,270 MNQ entries from 29 June to 15 July, thirteen trading days the V7 board does not contain. I verified these reprice correctly before using them: V5 entry prices match our own tape to a median 1–2.5 points on every day but one, and the ATR units are comparable (V5 median 13.0pt vs V7 13.4pt).

Robustness on every cell: parameter plateau (I rank by the neighbourhood mean, so a lone spike cannot win), strip-the-best-3-days, leave-one-day-out worst case, and the V5 archive as an out-of-sample leg.

FamilyRungNBest policy$/sigStrip-3LOO worstV5 legVerdict

The single most important line in that table is what it says about thrust. Across the whole grid the thrust family's SCALP-CHOP cell ranges from −$39.4 to −$40.6 a signal — that is the entire spread across 58 different exit policies. No exit rescues an un-vetoed thrust. The fix for that family was never the exit; it was the 55-second veto, and section 2 is the proof. That is a satisfying piece of consistency between two independent studies in this report.

And the shape of the exhaustion result is the ladder you described. On both trend rungs the winner is a tight chandelier on both lots — ride it, trail it, do not scalp it — and the neighbourhood confirms it is a plateau rather than a spike (BIG-TREND: tightChand×2 +$22.8, A1.5/tightChand +$20.6, A1.25/tightChand +$19.3, A1.0/tightChand +$18.8). That is the same conclusion 8a reached from a completely different direction, and it is the opposite of the 0.5R fade-scalp currently deployed.

8c · ★ BIG-TREND — the honest answer after extending the window

I EXTENDED THE WINDOW AS INSTRUCTED. THE RUNG IS STILL NOT PROVEN.

I want to be exact about this because the brief asked me to finally settle it. I added 13 trading days and 4,270 archive entries and ran on 45 days of tape instead of 22. It was not enough, for a reason that will not go away: BIG-TREND is only 2.96% of all minutes on this tape (ER ≥ 0.50; MED-TREND is 20.2%). The rung is structurally rare, so entry populations land in it a few dozen times a month no matter how much history you load. And the two independent windows disagree:

exhaustion, BIG-TRENDNBest policy$/sig
V7 board (16 Jul – 14 Aug)27tight chandelier ×2+$22.8
V5 archive (29 Jun – 15 Jul)22A 0.5R / B 1.0R−$8.0

Both cells are about n=25. One says ride, the other says scalp. That is not a proof and it is not a refutation — it is an unresolved rung, and the honest thing is to say so rather than pick the half that agrees with the story. What would settle it: roughly 120 BIG-TREND entries, which at 3% of minutes and current fire rates is about 8–10 weeks — or a deliberate archive pull of the 6–8 highest-ER days from the wider B2 history, scored on their own.

One methodological caveat I owe you on the V5 leg: the archive's strategies are not our gates. I mapped them by mechanism (thrust-family to thrust, dip/pullback to rgv, passive-chop to exhaustion) and that mapping is a judgement call, which is exactly why the V5 column is reported beside the V7 one and never merged into it. Some V5 cells are plainly not comparable — thrust STAY-OUT shows +$238.8 a signal at a 100% win rate on n=19, which is a tiny-n artefact of a violent day, not a finding.

8d · DELIVERABLE — the per-rung policy

I built the policy file with a rule that refuses to change anything that did not earn it: a cell only gets a change if it is positive on its home rung and positive after stripping its best 3 days and positive leave-one-day-out. Written to data/exit_overrides_proposed.json — a proposal. I did not touch the live data/exit_overrides.json; deploying is a Saturday decision, not a side-effect of writing a report.

everything elseall rungsNO CHANGE — holds at the live configHOLDfailed at least one robustness leg

Three shadow changes, zero adoptions. That is a thinner deliverable than the brief hoped for and I am not going to inflate it. Every cell that survived the V7 robustness battery was contradicted by the V5 out-of-sample leg, and under this desk's own rules a candidate that fails its out-of-sample leg goes to shadow, not to production. The one change I would argue hardest for is exhaustion_short on the trend rungs, because it is the only proposal supported by two independent studies — the live trial grade in 8a (−$20.56 a signal from scalping BIG-TREND) and the grid in 8b (tight chandelier wins that rung on a plateau).

★ REV2 — graded against the OTHER exhaustion_short exit in this report. The chandelier loses, and its proposed rows come out of the shadow book.

Part 1.5 §7 reaches a different answer for the same gate — a 1.5×ATR stop with a 3.0×ATR target — and the two were never graded against each other because they were measured on different populations with different engines. Both have now been run on the LIVE gate’s own 87 tape-backed signals, same tick paths, same cap, split by the §8b rung. Per signal:

The chandelier does not survive its own control on this population — at the 58th percentile it is indistinguishable from firing at random, and it is negative on all four rungs. It is not the 15-minute cap: on the 5-second tape at 15 / 30 / 60 minutes it pays −$3.60 / −$6.02 / −$6.45 a signal, i.e. a longer leash makes it worse, while the wide stop pays +$40.21 / +$47.30 / +$45.96.

In fairness to 8b: its cells were scored on the 716-entry exhaustion_rev shadow board, a much larger and different population, and I am not claiming it is wrong there. But a deploy acts on the LIVE gate, and on the live gate’s own entries this policy is negative everywhere. The two exhaustion_short rows in data/exit_overrides_proposed.json should not be shadow-slated; the wide stop should. The grind_long MED-TREND row is untouched by this — different gate, not tested here, and it is now on the card as a shadow slate in its own right. Full working in Part 3 Q2.

The one stone still unturned. Every study in this section measured the exit or the day and treated the entry as given. But the two clearest results both point at the entry population instead: no exit saves the un-vetoed thrust, and no pre-session feature calls the Open Rider's day. The Open Rider takes its direction from a raw 15-minute momentum sign with no quality test at all — and I never asked whether the losing days are days when that particular signal is unusable, as opposed to days that are simply bad. That is a per-signal question, not a per-day one, and it has roughly twenty times the sample size: 106 trades instead of 5 days. If the Open Rider is rescuable, I now think that is where it happens, and it is the first thing I would run next week.

P2.5 MID-WEEK MUSINGS — the leads, tested hard · week of 10–14 August 2026

Gaz, three things came up mid-week that were worth a night each: gold, grind's on/off switch, and a measuring instrument this very report leans on. I re-derived every number below from the tape and the ledger tonight — nothing here is inherited from the mid-week chat notes, and where my numbers disagree with those notes I say so and show you which one is right.

What survived. Two things, and both are gold. First, the MGC day rider: pick a direction an hour into the US session and hold it to the 20:40 flat. Its threshold grid is a plateau — all fifteen cells I swept make money, from +$552 to +$1,742 over sixteen sessions — and at the loose end it beats the best always-on constant on both hit rate and dollars while trading half as often. Second, the coil bouncer: 154 trades, a median trade of +$7.75, beaten by 0 of 12 placebo runs, and its mirror image loses $924. Small money, but real shape. On the MNQ side one clean thing survived too, and it is a negative: don't buy a grind long that is fighting the last thirty minutes. That single veto turns grind's tick-honest book from +$2,407 into +$3,674 on the same 26 days, and it does it while keeping every one of the ten best trades.

THE ONE-PARAGRAPH VERSION

GOLD: its big moves are slow grinds, not thrusts — median efficiency 0.20, four of the top five ran for 3 to 24 hours. Boarding them is already solved; the money question is which days to show up for. The day rider is the best shape gold has ever shown us (plateau, not a peak) and goes to shadow. GRIND: the "ER 0.35 floor" in the brief does not exist — it was deleted on 1 August and had been broken since 29 July. What actually decides whether grind trades is the open-hour watcher's break-trio, and on 26 days of tape that trio would have let grind fire four times. The mechanism that works is not a trend confirmation at all, it is a counter-trend veto. DIED: the ER floor as an enable mechanism — at 0.35 it throws away nine of the ten best trades, its strip-3 is −$1,044, and 182 of 400 random subsets of the same size beat it. BROKEN INSTRUMENT: selector_nightly.py counted four quarantined phantom trades on 13 August, inflating that day's regret by 39% — and that file feeds the router-value figure in this report.

Housekeeping for the reader: the full router operation review is its own section (P2.6) and I have not repeated it here. Money framing for the week — the desk booked +$1,292.50 across 113 legs from 10–14 August (day rider +$1,617.50 on 5 legs, tournament −$325.00 on 108), so the leads below are being judged against a desk whose engine is currently the rider, not the gates.

PART A — GOLD. Everything you have not seen yet, from the start.

You have never been shown a gold section, so this one starts from nothing. We have been capturing MGC (COMEX gold, $10.00 a point — five times MNQ's $2.00) since 4 August, plus an older block recovered from the retired V5 desk. That gives me 18 trading days of gold bars and 20 of ticks, spanning 7 July to 14 August with a hole from 18 July to 4 August where nothing was captured. Sixteen of those days have a full US session. Sixteen sessions is thin and I will keep saying so. Nothing in this part is live or proposed for live; it is all shadow-or-nothing.

FIRST, WHY THIS IS NOT JUST ANOTHER SWEEP — six gold attacks are already dead

On 5 August six separate attempts to build a gold gate were closed out, each with a named test. That matters here because it tells you what not to re-run, and because the two survivors below are a different shape of idea, not a re-tune of a dead one.

the attackhow it died — the named test
momentum / direction gate5 of 72 cells positive; the best strips to $16; plain always SHORT (−$532) BEAT the directed gate (−$1,274)
arm-then-confirm delaythe confirmed side is a coinflip — 48–50% across 24 cells
router-filtered (live meters)placebo-controlled and beaten by 31–80% of random removals
L2 book direction / vetoNULL and well powered — the edge flips sign across 5 disjoint samples
fader on extension + clockHurst on our own gold tape is 0.489 — a random walk, so there is nothing to fade
thrust-continuationcatches 22 of 22 runs, but the losers are indistinguishable from the winners (ATR effect size +0.21)

The one survivor of that whole round was an oracle day-filter — given foresight of which days contained a run, +$779 with strip-best-day +$356. That is exactly your "let the router switch it off", and it is still the live thread. Everything below is downstream of it.

FINDING 1 — GOLD'S BIG MOVES ARE GRINDS, NOT THRUSTS

I ran the run-catcher over the last nine days of gold tape tonight. It finds every directional leg worth ≥$300 on one lot that never gave back more than a third of itself while it was alive. Fifteen qualified. Here are the five biggest, and the shape of them is the finding:

start (UTC)dirpoints$ 1 lotminutesefficiencypre-60m rangemins to 20% mark
08-05 01:33UP225.4$2,2541,4740.127.1×ATR68
08-10 14:09UP110.4$1,1047260.168.6×ATR19
08-14 07:31UP65.2$6523670.206.6×ATR29
08-11 04:33DN63.3$6332060.235.1×ATR16
08-07 12:29UP62.8$628180.749.1×ATR1

Read the efficiency column. A move with efficiency 0.20 travels five times its net distance getting where it goes — up, back, up, back, up. Four of these five ran for between three and twenty-four hours. Only one (08-07) is a thrust, and it is the smallest of the five. The median of the top five is efficiency 0.20, and they all start from tape that is already moving — the hour before ignition is 7× a normal ATR, not a quiet coil.

Boarding them is not the problem. The gate fires inside 5 of the 5, and price reaches the 20% mark of the eventual move in a median of 19 minutes, leaving roughly $520 gross still on the table. We are not missing these because we cannot see them.

AND HERE IS WHERE I HAVE TO CORRECT THE MID-WEEK NOTE

The mid-week note said the exit was the cap: that switching from a 2.5×ATR trail to a wide 5×ATR stop held eight hours moves the ceiling from about $83 a week to about $346 a week. I can reproduce the direction of that, but only inside the oracle, and the note did not make that boundary loud enough. Here is the matrix, on identical entries, with both columns shown — ALL is every signal a live gate would take, ORACLE is only the signals that landed inside one of the week's top-five runs (labels you can only know afterwards):

exitnwin%ALL netmedian tradeORACLE nORACLE net
window A — 7–14 August (the mid-week note's own window), 49 signals, ~1.2 weeks
2.5×ATR trail · 1.5 stop · 3h (baseline)4936.7−$275−$19.8013+$143
no trail · 5×ATR stop · 3h4934.7−$131−$56.5013+$382
no trail · 5×ATR stop · 8h4922.4−$609−$75.8013+$589
no trail · 5×ATR stop · 12h4914.3−$2,076−$87.9013−$464
window B — 5–14 August (two days more tape), 69 signals, ~1.6 weeks
2.5×ATR trail · 1.5 stop · 3h (baseline)6934.8−$352−$23.7021+$220
no trail · 5×ATR stop · 8h6926.1+$1,336−$81.9021+$3,051
no trail · 5×ATR stop · 12h6920.3+$2,611−$89.7021+$4,357

Three honest readings of that table.

  1. The exit really does matter, and a trail really is wrong for gold. On the oracle column, in both windows, moving off the trail and onto a wide stop held for hours multiplies the take — +$143 to +$589 in window A, +$220 to +$3,051 in window B. That part of the mid-week claim stands.
  2. But the exit alone does not make it a strategy. On the ALL column in window A, every single cell loses money. The wide stop without the day filter takes bigger losses on the ordinary days than it wins on the run days. The exit was a cap, not the cap — you still need the filter, so the order of the gold problem is unchanged: day selection first.
  3. The hold cap is not identified at this sample size. Window A says 12 hours destroys it (−$464 even on the oracle); window B says 12 hours is the best cell (+$4,357). The whole difference is one run — the 08-05 move that ran 225 points over 24 hours. Adding two days of tape flipped the conclusion. That is the clearest statement I can make about how thin this is.

The hold-time curve on window B makes the same point — it is not a smooth curve, it is a staircase built out of a handful of trades:

hold cap2h4h6h8h10h12h
net (all 69 signals)+$283+$517+$762+$1,336+$2,139+$2,611
win rate39.1%34.8%29.0%26.1%23.2%20.3%

Note what holding longer does to the feel of it: the win rate falls from 39% to 20% as the money rises. That is a strategy where four trades in five lose and the fifth pays for everything. On our own judging rule that is not a disqualification — expectancy is what counts — but it is exactly the shape you will find hardest to sit through, and you should know that before it ever goes near a live switch.

FINDING 2 — THE MGC DAY RIDER, AND WHY A PLATEAU IS THE EVIDENCE

This is the best-shaped thing gold has produced. The rule is simple enough to say in one line: at 13:30 UTC start watching; an hour in, ask the live drift detector which way the day has efficiently already moved; if it is confirmed, take two lots that way and hold to the 20:40 flat. One trade a day, never overnight, and it finishes inside the session you watch.

The thresholds were derived from gold, not borrowed from MNQ. You asked for that specifically. The method was to find what percentile MNQ's live constants occupy in MNQ's own distribution, then read gold's number at the same percentile — transferring the selectivity, not the number. Tonight's derivation:

efficiency p50roundtrip p50ATR p50sessions
MNQ0.1100.41837.8 pt49
MGC0.1240.4113.6 pt16

MNQ's live ER floor of 0.15 sits at MNQ's 65th percentile; its roundtrip floor of 0.45 sits at the 53rd. Read off gold's own distribution those percentiles give ER 0.155 and RT 0.417 — within a whisker of MNQ's numbers. Gold's ATR is ten times smaller and the thresholds barely move, because efficiency and roundtrip are ratios and the size cancels out. That had to be measured rather than assumed, and it is the answer to "why are you using MNQ thresholds": we are not — they simply landed in the same place.

Now the grid. I swept five efficiency floors against three roundtrip floors around that derived pair, scoring every cell on the same sixteen sessions, always against the best always-on constant:

ER floorRT floordays fireddirection held to the flatnetmedian dayMFE (watching)strip-best-day
0.0930.4178 / 1687.5%+$1,742+$280+$3,852+$899
0.1090.4178 / 1687.5%+$1,742+$280+$3,852+$899
0.1240.4178 / 1687.5%+$1,742+$280+$3,852+$899
0.0930.3509 / 1677.8%+$1,259+$279+$3,923+$416
0.1400.3507 / 1685.7%+$1,395+$279+$3,327+$552
0.0930.4807 / 1685.7%+$899+$279+$2,921+$552
0.1550.4806 / 1683.3%+$552+$223+$2,396+$225
…and the eight cells not shown, which sit between these. All 15 of 15 cells are positive. Range: +$552 to +$1,742. Held: 77.8% to 87.5%. Worst single day in every cell: −$619.
CONTROL — best constant (always short)—16 / 1656.2%+$1,520———

Why I am making a fuss about the plateau. When a swept grid has one good cell surrounded by bad ones, you have found a fluke and fitted it. When every cell in the neighbourhood pays, you have found a structure and the exact number does not matter much. This is the second kind. The evidence is the plateau, and the peak cell is not the recommendation — if this ever ships, it ships from the middle of the plateau, not from +$1,742.

And against the control: gold drifted down over this sample, so always short scored +$1,520 with a 56.2% day-hit-rate. That is the bar, not a coinflip. The loose cells clear it on both counts — +$1,742 against +$1,520, at 87.5% against 56.2%, while trading on half the days. Trading half as often for more money is the part that makes me take it seriously.

How long to wait before calling the direction is its own axis, and it has a shape too:

decide atdays firedheld to the flatnetMFEworst adversevs best constant
+30 min9 / 1677.8%+$1,585+$5,175−$1,440+21.5pp
+45 min10 / 1660.0%+$826+$4,820−$860+3.8pp
+60 min6 / 1683.3%+$552+$2,396−$936+27.1pp
+90 min5 / 1640.0%−$845+$1,839−$1,068−28.8pp
+120 min4 / 1625.0%−$950+$1,006−$1,360−50.0pp

Waiting too long is fatal — by 90 minutes in you are buying the end of the move and the direction holds only 40% of the time. Between 30 and 60 minutes it works. The +45 minute cell is the honest warning sign: it dips to 60% held while its neighbours either side are 77.8% and 83.3%. With sixteen sessions, one day landing differently moves a cell by six percentage points, so do not read that dip as structure — read it as the noise floor of this sample.

The number you will care about most is the gap between what the strategy books and what you could claim. Median MFE per fired day is +$453 against a median adverse excursion of −$400. The profit is genuinely there to be taken, but only behind a wide stop and only if someone is watching — which is what you said you do. An unattended tight version of this gets stopped out of the same days.

FINDING 3 — THE COIL BOUNCER (the strongest standalone gold gate, and it is small)

Different idea entirely: when gold's 20-minute range squeezes to 0.6× its own two-hour median, fade whichever edge of the coil price is sitting on, targeting the middle. It trades compression, which is why it is not the same thing as the extension-fader that died in the earlier round.

ndaysgreen dayswin%$/tradenetstrip-best-daymedian trade
coil bounce (fade the edge)154171051.9%+$3.05+$470.42+$289.42+$7.75
mirror control (break OUT of the coil)15417433.1%−$6.00−$923.75−$1,059.61−$12.54

Three things make this the most trustworthy gold result I have, even though it is the smallest: the median trade is positive (+$7.75 — it is not one lucky day carrying 153 losers); it survives stripping its best day (+$289 of the +$470 remains); and a placebo of twelve random-time trade sets with the same count per day has a median of −$214 and beat it 0 times out of 12. On top of that, doing the exact opposite loses $924 — the mirror control is the cleanest evidence that it is the coil doing the work and not the clock. At roughly $137 a week it is below the bar you have set for going live, and it stays in the shadow book.

FINDING 4 — THE LONDON-OPEN BREAK FADE (survives its placebo, still fails as a trade)

Gold's opening-range breaks fail: taking the continuation wins 6.2% / 25.0% / 37.5% of the time across the three anchors I tested. So the trade is to fade them. Only one of the three anchors holds up:

anchorngreen dayswin%$/tradenetstrip-best-day
LONDON open16743.8%+$31.40+$502.42+$125.01
RTH open16637.5%−$7.32−$117.04−$346.83
PM fix16318.8%−$31.88−$510.07−$587.71

The London cell is beaten by 0 of 7 shifted anchors (the seven fakes score −$325, −$112, −$305, +$99, −$156, −$228, −$100) — so the anchor is carrying it, not the general shape of the day. That is a real result. But look at the honest side: nine of the sixteen trades lose, so the median trade is a loss, and stripping the best day takes +$502 down to +$125. Sixteen trades is one trade a day for three weeks. It is a lead, not a strategy.

THE THREE TRAPS THAT BIT DURING THIS WORK — check anything new against them

WHAT IT WOULD COST TO FIND OUT — and the answer is days, not configurations

Every gold result above is limited by the same thing, and it is not cleverness. It is sixteen sessions. Look at what that does: adding two days of tape flipped the exit matrix's conclusion about hold time; the day rider's grid moves by a whole cell when one day lands differently; the London fade rests on sixteen trades. We have already swept the configurations — fifteen threshold cells, five decision times, twelve exit rules, three anchors. More sweeping on this sample cannot tell us anything new; it can only fit it harder.

The good news is that the missing ingredient is the cheap one. Capture is already running for both symbols and costs nothing extra. At five sessions a week, gold reaches n = 40 sessions around the third week of September, which is roughly where a day-rider hit rate of 87.5% stops being a story about eight days. Everything above goes in the shadow book now — free, riskless, and accumulating exactly the thing it lacks. The one thing I would build rather than wait for is the causal day filter for the run catcher, because that is the piece the whole gold problem still turns on, and it needs to be placebo-controlled against discarding the same number of firings at random.

PART B — THE STANDING GRIND LEAD: which enable-mechanism actually works?

The question on the table: what best captures grind's trend-day upside while killing its chop-churn — (a) trend-day-only arming, (b) its own entry trend-confirmation, or (c) the current ER-0.35 floor? I built the book to answer it properly: every grind_long signal on 26 days of tick-covered tape, fired exactly as the live gate fires it (slope 0.4, fast-slope, ATR ≥ 22 — the floor that is genuinely deployed), then exited tick by tick as the live managed slate exits: Lot A a 2.5R scalp, Lot B the loose-then-lock chandelier (3.5 → 6.0R → 0.5), both behind the native 1-ATR stop, $1.50 a round trip per lot. That is the LIVE/MANAGED grind, not the unmanaged grind_fast shadow, which flatters itself and would have sent me the wrong way.

FIRST, A CORRECTION: OPTION (c) DOES NOT EXIST

There is no ER-0.35 floor on grind. deciders.ER_FLOOR is an empty dictionary. The floors were deleted on 1 August, and the audit note in the code says they had not blocked a single live trade since 29 July anyway, because the dual-slot cutover broke the lookup. When they were re-derived on the blocked book, grind's ER ≥ 0.35 turned out to have been blocking a profitable population (+$2,923 over 173 signals). So the brief's option (c) is a ghost — and tonight's book says deleting it was right, which I will show in a moment.

What actually decides whether grind trades is two things: the ATR ≥ 22 floor inside the gate, and the on/off switch outside it. The 0.35 number does still exist — but in open_hour_watch.py, as one leg of the break trio that is allowed to arm a benched momentum gate: last-hour ER ≥ 0.35 AND last-hour ATR ≥ 18 AND a new session extreme is taken. So the real question (c) is "is the break trio the right arming test", and that is answerable.

THE BOOK ITSELF — where grind's money actually is

nnet$/tradewin%strip-3-bestworst leave-one-day-out
grind_long, live-managed, 26 tick-days133+$2,407+$18.1031.6%+$346+$1,368

Two facts about that book decide everything else. It wins under a third of the time and it is carried by runners: 28 trades reached 4R or better and those 28 carry +$11,991, meaning the other 105 bleed −$9,584. Strip the three best trades and +$2,407 becomes +$346. Any filter that touches the runners kills the gate, which is why every mechanism below is scored on "how many of the ten best trades does it keep".

Split by regime at entry — ATR level, efficiency and range position, exactly as the discipline requires — the shape is not what the folklore says:

regime at entrynnet$/trade
dead-chop (ER < 0.10)40−$802−$20.00
normal-chop (ER 0.10–0.20)31−$358−$11.60
in-between / building (ER 0.20–0.35)35+$897+$25.60
clean-trend (ER ≥ 0.35)14+$262+$18.70
violent-whipsaw (top-quartile ATR, ER < 0.15)13+$2,408+$185.20

Grind's money is not in "clean trend". The 14 clean-trend entries made +$262. The 13 high-volatility, low-efficiency entries made +$2,408 — the entire book. That is the chandelier catching a big swing on a violent day, and it is the single most important thing in this table, because every mechanism in the brief is a trendiness test, and trendiness is not where the money is.

THE THREE MECHANISMS, SCORED AND SWEPT

Every threshold is a guess until it is swept, so each mechanism was scanned across its range rather than tested at one number. Placebo column = how many of 400 random subsets of the same size beat the real one; if a mechanism cannot beat coin-flip removal it has no claim.

mechanismn keptnet$/tradestrip-3top-10 winners keptplacebo
BASE — deployed, ATR ≥ 22 only133+$2,407+$18.10+$34610 / 10—
(a) trend-day-only — arm once the session so far has proven efficient
session-ER ≥ 0.0296+$2,780+$29.00+$7188 / 1097 / 400
session-ER ≥ 0.0545+$2,630+$58.50+$7583 / 1040 / 400
session-ER ≥ 0.0813+$10+$0.70−$8340 / 10221 / 400
(b) entry trend-confirmation — require a 15-minute impulse our way
net15 ≥ 0.20×ATR118+$198+$1.70−$1,8628 / 10391 / 400
net15 ≥ 0.50×ATR109+$299+$2.70−$1,7627 / 10374 / 400
net15 ≥ 1.00×ATR96+$1,380+$14.40−$6817 / 10249 / 400
(c) the ER floor at entry — the mechanism the brief names
ER30 ≥ 0.2533−$558−$16.90−$2,1182 / 10331 / 400
ER30 ≥ 0.3514+$262+$18.70−$1,0441 / 10182 / 400
the deployed lever for comparison — raise the ATR floor
ATR ≥ 24107+$2,518+$23.50+$45810 / 10116 / 400
ATR ≥ 3433+$1,766+$53.50−$2947 / 1052 / 400

(c) is dead, and I will name the tests. At ER ≥ 0.35 it throws away nine of the ten best trades in the book — that is the fake-filter rule failing outright. Its strip-3 is −$1,044, meaning what is left after removing three trades is a loser. And 182 of 400 random removals of the same size beat it, so it is not distinguishable from deleting trades at random. The ER ≥ 0.25 cell is worse still (−$558, beaten by 331 of 400). This independently confirms the 1 August decision to delete the floor, on a different population and a different exit model.

(b) is worse than dead — it is backwards. Requiring a 15-minute impulse our way takes +$2,407 down to +$198 and 391 of 400 random subsets beat it. The reason is visible in the trades themselves: the ten worst fires had 15-minute impulses of +$113, +$139, +$147, +$192 and +$263 points, which are bigger than the winners'. Confirming the impulse means arriving later into the same move and buying the part that reverses. This is the "the losers are indistinguishable" pattern showing up on MNQ exactly as it did on gold.

(a) is directionally real but noisy. Requiring the session to have shown some efficiency improves things at 0.02 and 0.05 (+$2,780 and +$2,630 against the base's +$2,407, on 96 and 45 trades of the 133), but the curve is not monotone — 0.04 dips to +$1,944, 0.06 falls to +$1,204, 0.08 collapses to +$10. That is not a plateau, it is a wobble. And the 0.05 cell keeps only 3 of the 10 best trades, which is the fake filter tell again.

AND THE ARMING TEST WE ACTUALLY RUN: THE BREAK TRIO ADMITS ALMOST NOTHING

Scored the way it is used — scan the session, the first minute all three legs pass arms the gate for the rest of the day — here is what the trio does to 26 days of grind signals:

nnet$/tradewin%
every grind fire133+$2,407+$18.1031.6%
armed by the trio (fires after the arm)4+$100+$24.9025.0%
never armed — refused129+$2,308+$17.9031.8%

My replication of the trio fired on 3 of 45 tape-days, and on just one of the 26 days grind actually signalled. (The live watcher's version armed once more than mine did — on 13 August at 13:58 — because it reads its efficiency and its session extremes over slightly different windows than I can reconstruct; so treat the "4 fires" as approximate and the order of magnitude as the finding.) The order of magnitude is the finding: the arming test in force is so strict that grind is, in practice, a gate that does not trade. The live ledger agrees without any modelling — grind_long has not taken a single live trade since 5 August, seven sessions ago, and the Paris-midnight service now start-benches it every night by design ("momentum start-benched per MONDAY #2 — router arms on evidence").

What that bench has cost, priced honestly:

windowgrind signalstick-honest value if takenwith the veto below
this report week, 10–14 Aug10−$66+$64
since the bench, 6–14 Aug20+$490+$912
router era, 30 Jul onward51+$814+$1,688

So: this week the bench cost nothing — it saved $66. Over the nine sessions since the bench began it cost about $490, and almost all of that is two trades: 6 August 13:38 (+$664) and 13 August 13:41 (+$447). Both are chandelier runners on high-ATR entries. Notice the timing on the second one: the open-hour watcher armed grind on 13 August at 13:58 — seventeen minutes after that signal fired. The trio was not wrong about the day; it was late. That is grind's real problem in one line: the gate lives or dies on two or three runner entries a month, and an arming test that needs a proven trend cannot be early enough to catch the entry that starts it.

THE MECHANISM THAT DOES WORK, AND IT IS A VETO

Since every positive confirmation failed, I swept the negative: rather than requiring proof of trend, refuse the entries that are fighting the recent tape. Two cheap conditions at signal time:

armnnet$/tradewin%strip-3worst LODOtop-10 keptplacebo
BASE133+$2,407+$18.1031.6%+$346+$1,36810 / 10—
skip if the 30-min net is worse than −1.0×ATR125+$3,134+$25.1032.8%+$1,072+$2,09410 / 1027 / 400
…and only in the top 40% of the hour's range97+$3,674+$37.9035.1%+$1,613+$2,7519 / 1016 / 400

The first line is the one I trust. It removes eight trades spread across seven different days — every one of them stopped out, seven of them losers — for a saving of +$727 — and it keeps every single one of the ten best trades. It reads in plain English as: don't buy a continuation long while price has just fallen more than an ATR over the last half hour. The sweep behind it is a genuine plateau — every threshold from −2.0×ATR to −0.5×ATR improves the book, so nothing hangs on the literal:

30-min net floor−2.0×ATR−1.5×−1.25×−1.0×−0.75×−0.5×
net (no range condition)+$2,636+$3,014+$3,144+$3,362+$3,362+$3,282

The second condition is a peak cell and I am flagging it as one. Requiring price in the top 40% of the hour's range scores +$3,674 at exactly 0.60, but 0.65 falls to +$2,266 and 0.70 to +$1,008. That is a spike with cliffs, i.e. a fitted literal. Ship the veto, do not ship the 0.60.

Robustness on the veto pair, stated plainly: it improves both halves of the sample when split by date (first thirteen days +$965 → +$1,063; last thirteen +$1,442 → +$2,611), its worst leave-one-day-out is +$2,094 (i.e. no single day is holding it up), and 27 of 400 same-sized random removals beat it — about a 7% chance of arising by luck, which is suggestive rather than conclusive. And the honest caveat that matters most: the whole effect is eight trades. Eight. That is why this is a shadow arm and not a Saturday deploy.

THE ANSWER TO THE GRIND QUESTION, IN ONE PLACE

(c) the ER-0.35 floor: it does not exist, and tonight's book says good — as a filter it is REFUTED (kills 9 of the 10 best trades; strip-3 −$1,044; beaten by 182 of 400 random subsets). (b) entry trend-confirmation: REFUTED — it is backwards; the losers have bigger impulses than the winners, and 391 of 400 random subsets beat it. (a) trend-day-only: PARKED — real but wobbly, and it drops the runners; revive it if a genuinely trend-heavy month gives the session-ER measure enough days to show a plateau instead of a wobble. What to do instead: keep the ATR ≥ 22 floor, keep the router's on/off, and add the counter-move veto as a shadow arm SHADOW. And take seriously that the current arming test is so strict grind has been a dead gate for nine sessions — that may be the right call on a chop fortnight, but it is a decision being made by a threshold nobody chose for that purpose.

PART C — A BROKEN INSTRUMENT UNDERNEATH THIS REPORT

One more lead, and it is the least glamorous but the most immediately actionable: the nightly exit-selector rollup counts quarantined trades, and this report reads that rollup to produce its router-value figure.

On 13 August the day rider's re-booking bug wrote four phantom trades that were flagged EXCLUDE:day_rider_phantom_rebook_20260813 and deliberately never deleted. The selector's own query (scripts/selector_nightly.py) filters on exit_reason but never filters data_quality — the one filter this desk's P&L rules mandate. So it counted all five day-rider rows for that day, and because it reprices every trade off bars they all collapsed to the same synthetic row: five identical entries of chosen −$31.50, regret $72.50. ★ REV2 — and only FOUR of those five rows are phantoms. The trade ids flagged EXCLUDE:day_rider_phantom_rebook_20260813 are 673, 674, 675 and 678. The fifth day-rider row on that date is id 668 — the genuine 13:38 LONG that Garrath claimed at 14:26 for +$731.00 — and it is not quarantined and must not be deleted. So the phantom is 4 × $72.50 = $290.00, the corrected regret is $457.50 and the corrected trade count is 10.

13 August selector fileas publisheddedupederror
trades counted1410+4 phantoms
regret$748$458inflated 39%
selector P&L−$228−$102overstated 2.2×

I checked every one of the eleven selector files against the ledger: 13 August is the only contaminated one (the two quarantined rows from 4 August fall outside that file's window), so the week's total regret of $7,903 is overstated by $290 rather than by anything dramatic. But 39% of that single day's regret — $290.00 of $747.50 — is one dead trade counted four extra times, and any selector tuning done off that day is chasing a phantom. 48% of that single day's regret ★ REV2: the 48% figure counted five rows as phantom and so deleted the real +$731 claim along with them. $290 phantom / $457.50 corrected / 10 real trades is the arithmetic, and it is the arithmetic SATURDAY #3's verification criterion ("confirm regret drops from $748 to $458") is written against. Two other things I would fix while in there: the file set is gappy (27–31 July, 3–7 August, 13 August) because there is no systemd timer for it at all despite the name "nightly", and its only consumer is this report.

Related and worth stating as a rule rather than an incident, because it recurs: a shadow variant is only as trustworthy as its pure-reconstruction column. The two-ratchet ledger ships recon_pure_usd, so its stop-leak could be measured and turned out to be one trade; the partial ledger ships none, so its "exits earlier" delta cannot be falsified at all. The grind-exit ledgers themselves belong to the shadow section and I have not re-run them here.

The one stone still unturned, and it is the same stone on both instruments: a causal day classifier. Gold's run-catcher and MNQ's grind both live or die on a minority of days, and both currently rely on an arming test that either fires too rarely (the break trio) or requires foresight (the oracle). Everything else in this section is either incubating or answered.

P2.6 THE ROUTER OPERATION REVIEW — week of 10–14 August 2026

Gaz, the router had a quiet, disciplined, slightly profitable week, and then I found two things underneath it that matter more than the week itself. The decisions it actually made were worth +$51.50 — its benches blocked $611 of losers and gave up $410 of winners (+$201), its armings paid $716.50 and cost $866 (−$149.50). That is a rounding error, and on a week where the tournament book closed −$325.00 over 108 trades a rounding error is not a bad result. The nightly rollup, which prices what the benched gates would have done, is more generous: +$400 net over the four days it can actually see.

The first thing underneath. The router is managing an empty room. Its managed universe is capitulation_long on the down side and rgv_short on the up side. Those two gates took 2 trades between them all week. Meanwhile exhaustion_short — which is an up-fader, exactly the shape the router exists to bench — took 71 of the week’s 108 trades, and 12 of them fired straight into a confirmed TREND_UP for −$241.00, at −$20.08 a trade. The nightly leakage detector reported zero leakage every single day, because it is only allowed to look at the two gates that never traded. That is not a small number and it is not a one-week fluke: over 30 days the same gate has 26 counter-trend fires for −$398.50, negative on 7 of the 8 days it happened, and it survives both strip-the-worst-day and leave-one-day-out. That is the Saturday play, and it is the best-evidenced thing in this section.

The second thing underneath. I do not fully trust the ruler the nightly uses, and I can prove it with the desk’s own trades. The nightly prices every blocked signal under a plain scalp-2R / 1-ATR exit. So I took the week’s 108 real live trades — same entries, same tape — and repriced them under that identical exit. They go from −$325.00 to +$459.50. The ruler is $7.26 a trade more generous than what the desk actually achieves, which means every “wins we missed” figure in the nightly is inflated. But — and this is why I segmented it before saying anything — the gap is almost exactly zero in chop (−$0.28 a trade over 65 trades) and huge in trend (+$25.02 in TREND_UP, +$11.38 in TREND_DOWN). The router benches in trend. So the over-benching it gets accused of is measured with a ruler that is at its most generous precisely where the router does its work.

THE ONE-PARAGRAPH VERSION

SURVIVED: the durable router as a mechanism. 5-minute systemd tick ran 288/288 on three days and 284/284 on the fourth, zero service failures; 95% of ticks changed nothing and Friday changed almost nothing at all (6 transitions in 24 hours) on the day the desk made its second-best number. The untradeable meter genuinely works over 22 days — STAY-OUT days averaged −$651, CAUTION days −$212, TRADEABLE days +$7, cleanly monotone — and the 65 cutoff sits in the middle of a flat plateau from 50 to 75, so it does not want tuning. THE ACTION: put exhaustion_short into the router’s UP-side managed set. 26 counter-trend fires over 30 days, −$398.50, −$15.33 a trade, red on 7 of 8 days, survives strip-the-worst. THE SECOND ACTION: the 40-day sweep says leave ER 0.15 / net 30 exactly where they are and change the timing — window 30→45 min, step 15→5 min, hold 2→3 marks. That is worth +$502 on the live set and +$399 on the full set, and it independently arrives at the two fixes the ledger identified by hand: a longer window kills the boundary flicker, a shorter step cuts the actuation lag. WHAT ERRED: 62% of the week’s 64 gate transitions were dead — no trade, no blocked signal, nothing. abs_veto_short alone was flipped 36 times and 28 of those flips produced literally nothing. Actuation lag cost −$242.00, all of it on the bench side. THE HONEST GAP: on Thursday the router was brain-dead for 10 hours 20 minutes — 125 consecutive ticks aborted on an expired OAuth session while systemd reported every one of them as a success. It cost $0, and that was luck, not design.

Counting the switch changes — and why it has to be done three ways

The ledger learned this the hard way on Thursday morning and it is worth restating, because a churn metric read off the wrong instrument is worse than no churn metric. The router’s own log under-reports: its changed: field only records what the router changed, so the 22:00Z reactivation timer moves gates while every router tick that hour says “changed: none”. The systemd journal over-reports: tournament.py initialises its last-known set to empty, so the first tick after any restart logs a phantom change. Here is the reconciliation.

Paris dayrouter log saysjournal raw restart artifacts22:00Z timerTRUE switch events gate transitions
Tue 08-112122102021
Wed 08-121619211619
Thu 08-131215111518
Fri 08-14451156
Tue–Fri5361 535664

The three instruments disagree by up to 25% on any given day and they agree within one event on the week. The arbiter is the modification time on gate_switches.env — the file is what the tournament actually reads. Fifty-six real switch events, sixty-four individual gate flips, over five days.

The thrash check — and it fails on one gate

Sixty-four flips sounds like a lot. Spread across 1,148 ticks it is not: the router changed nothing on 95% of its ticks. The rate per day was 0.88, 0.79, 0.75 and 0.25 changes an hour — well under the 5-an-hour that got flagged on 08-07. But the rate is not the problem this week. The concentration is.

gatetransitionsbenchesarms DEAD ($0 either way)dead%blocked signalslive trades
abs_veto_short361818 2878%320
abs_veto_long954 222%107
exhaustion_short743 229%1555
grind_long633 467%140
capitulation_long633 467%12
TOTAL643331 4062%4384

abs_veto_short accounts for 56% of every flip the router made this week, and 78% of its own flips produced nothing whatsoever — no trade taken, no signal blocked. Eighteen benches and eighteen arms, and twenty-eight of those thirty-six were pure motion. Sixteen of them lasted under thirty minutes. This is the same disease the 08-07 review named — churn is invisible at $0 because each half of a round trip grades NEUTRAL in isolation — and it is now concentrated in one gate rather than spread across three.

The cause is not judgement. It is the threshold. The direction rule requires 60-minute net above 40pt and ER at or above 0.20, and this week’s tape sat on top of both bars for hours at a time. Wednesday morning is the clean example, straight from the log: UP +63pt/ER 0.385 → FLAT +38pt/ER 0.222 (net leg fails by 2 points) → UP +60pt/ER 0.324 → FLAT +39pt/ER 0.252 (fails by 1 point) → UP +42pt/ER 0.268 → FLAT +5pt. Five flips in fifty minutes, and three of them turned on a single point of net. Each individual read was correct.

Read plainly: the router got materially better at not being wrong and materially worse at not being busy. Two bad calls in five days is a good ratio. Fifteen churn grades in five days, against seven in all prior history, is not.

2 · WHAT PAID AND WHAT ERRED, IN DOLLARS

Every one of the 64 flips is graded here on its own terms. A bench is judged on what it blocked — each SUPPRESSED-OPEN in the benched window, repriced tick-honest at $1.50 a round trip and $2 a point. A negative blocked number means it stopped a loser, which is the point. An arm is judged on the gate’s real live P&L over the window it was armed.

Note what is missing from that table: twenty-one benches. Nothing wanted to open during them, so they are neither good nor bad — they were free. The router’s standing asymmetry says a wrong bench costs opportunity while a wrong arm costs real money, so when unsure, sit benched. That policy is why two-thirds of its benches are free, and it is the right policy. But it also means the bench half of the scoreboard is being judged on 12 events, not 33.

The single most important line in that table is that the two biggest armings were not the router’s. They were the 22:00Z reactivation timer, which brings gates back on schedule regardless of what anyone thinks. It armed exhaustion_short into Wednesday for −$367.50 and into Friday for +$310.00. Net across the week the timer is roughly a wash — but it is the largest single lever on this desk and nothing grades it, because it is not a decision, it is a clock.

3 · NET ROUTER VALUE — THE NIGHTLY ROLLUP

Every data/router_nightly/*.json and data/selector_nightly/*.json for the week. Two things to say before the numbers. First, none of these files existed for this week when I started — the last router nightly on disk was 2026-08-07. I backfilled all five days. Second, Monday is a measurement gap, not a zero. The nightly reads blocked signals out of the systemd journal, and the journal does not reach back past 2026-08-10 23:11 UTC. Monday’s file reads $0 / $0 / $0 because there was nothing to read. It is not a quiet day; it is a blind spot, and I am reporting it as one.

★ THE RULER PROBLEM — and why I re-measured it before believing the over-bench

That exhaustion_short line says the router benched $721 of winners. But exhaustion_short was the desk’s worst gate this week — 71 live trades for −$218.50, minus three dollars a trade. Both things cannot be true of the same gate on the same tape unless the difference is the exit. The nightly prices every blocked signal under a plain scalp-2R with a 1-ATR stop. The live desk does not use that exit.

So I ran the control. Same 108 live trades, same entries, same tape, repriced under the nightly’s exit.

gatenLIVE exit $$/trade scalp-2R $$/tradedifferencescalp better on
exhaustion_short71−218.50−3.08+177.00+2.49+395.5042/71
abs_veto_long9+6.00+0.67+203.50+22.61+197.505/9
abs_veto_short26−64.50−2.48+128.00+4.92+192.5020/26
capitulation_long2−48.00−24.00−49.00−24.50−1.000/2
TOTAL108−325.00−3.01 +459.50+4.25+784.5067/108

The nightly’s ruler is $7.26 a trade more generous than the desk. Every “wins we missed” number in this section is measured with it, so every one of them is too big — which means the router’s true net value is higher than the +$400 reported. But before recommending anything off that, I segmented it, because a blanket number across mixed regimes is the exact bug the standing backtest rule exists to prevent.

segment at entrynLIVE $/tradescalp-2R $/tradegap per tradereading
CHOP (router label)65+0.65+0.36−0.28Zero gap. In chop our live exits are as good as the ruler.
TREND_UP23−13.98+11.04+25.02The whole gap lives here.
TREND_DOWN20−2.27+9.10+11.38 
and the same cut on ATR/ER rather than the router’s own label, as a cross-check:
violent-whipsaw (ER<0.20, ATR≥12)47−10.40+1.99+12.39 
clean-trend (ER≥0.35)22+9.98+22.95+12.98Live makes money here — just less than half what was on offer.
normal-chop (ER<0.20, ATR 6–12)25+4.72+2.10−2.62Live BEATS the ruler here.
in-between (ER 0.20–0.35)14−12.39−13.68−1.29Both lose. Nobody’s regime.

So the honest statement is narrow and useful: our live exits are fine in ordinary chop and leak badly once the tape moves. That is an exit finding, not a router finding, and I am handing it to the rehab section rather than acting on it here. What it means for the router is precise: the wins-missed figure is inflated exactly in trend, which is the only regime where the router does anything. The $721 exhaustion_short over-bench is mostly the ruler. Do not tighten the router on the strength of it.

★★ THE LEAKAGE THE DETECTOR CANNOT SEE

Every nightly this week reported leakage_n = 0. Four days, zero leaked losers. That reads as a router with nothing getting past it. It is not. The detector is only allowed to look at capitulation_long and rgv_short — the two gates in its managed set — and those two took two trades between them all week. It is reporting zero because it is looking at an empty room.

Here is every live trade this week, laid out against the router’s own regime label at the moment of entry.

gateCHOP n / $TREND_UP n / $TREND_DOWN n / $router-managed?
abs_veto_long4 / +44.005 / −38.000 / 0.00no (momentum, not a fader)

capitulation_long0 / 0.002 / −48.000 / 0.00YES — and it took 2 trades all week
exhaustion_short47 / −54.0012 / −241.0012 / +76.50NO — and it is an UP-fader that took 71 of 108 trades

Read the exhaustion_short row across. Aligned with a down-trend it made +$76.50. In chop it lost a trivial −$54.00. Fired into a confirmed up-trend — fading a move that was proven to be going the other way — it lost −$241.00 on twelve trades, twenty dollars a trade. That is textbook counter-trend fader bleed, it is precisely what this router was built to prevent, and the router is not allowed to touch it.

Then I checked whether that is a one-week accident, across the full 30 days of tape.

Every single fader on this desk loses money firing counter-trend, and all four lose by a similar amount — fifteen to fifty-six dollars a trade. Seventy-six fires, −$1,589. The router currently manages fourteen of those seventy-six. The other sixty-two, worth −$1,097, belong to two gates it is not allowed to see — and one of those two is the busiest live gate on the desk.
