The selector lost to a fixed always-SCALP rule on four of five days and by $1,267 on the week. It beat fixed-WIDE, which is something, but the thing it is supposed to be — an adaptive chooser — is currently worse than picking one exit and never thinking again. And the wrong-pick pattern is completely systematic: on Wednesday all five of the worst picks were exhaustion_short choosing TIGHT when SCALP or WIDE was best, and on Friday all five were the same gate doing the same thing. One gate, one mode, one direction of error. That lines up exactly with the exit finding above — the live exits are too tight for the tape once it moves.

⚠ THURSDAY’S SELECTOR NUMBERS ARE POLLUTED — the standing bug confirmed again

Thursday shows 14 trades and $747.50 of regret. Five rows carry the same day-rider decision at $72.50 of regret each — and FOUR of the five are phantoms. The rows flagged EXCLUDE:day_rider_phantom_rebook_20260813 are ids 673, 674, 675 and 678; the fifth, id 668, is the real 13:38 LONG that was claimed at 14:26 for +$731.00. So the phantom is 4 × $72.50 = $290.00, which is 39% of the day’s regret. Thursday also carries $1,551 of data_quality-flagged rows that the P&L module correctly excludes and selector_nightly.py does not. This is the known selector-nightly-ignores-data-quality defect, unfixed, and it is still inflating regret. Read Thursday’s selector row as $457.50 of regret on 10 real trades, not $747.50 on 14.

★ REV2: this paragraph previously said “$362.50, 48% of the day’s regret… roughly $385 on 9 real trades”. That counted the genuine claimed trade as a phantom too. $290 / $457.50 / 10 is the arithmetic, it agrees with Part 2.5 Part C, and it is what SATURDAY #3’s verification (“confirm regret drops from $748 to $458”) will actually produce once the fix lands. On the old 48% restatement that check would have FAILED.

4 · THRESHOLD TUNING — THE 40-DAY SWEEP

scripts/router_study.py --days 40, both universes, thresholds and timings. The shipped config is ER≥0.15, |net|≥30pt, 30-minute window, 15-minute poll, 2-mark hysteresis, fast-exit off. The fast replay validated identical to the live direction_router.replay_marks before anything was swept, so this is the real router being measured, not an approximation of it.

Set A — the LIVE managed set (capitulation_long, rgv_short)

Baseline with no router at all: −$302. Router as shipped: +$190, a swing of +$492, from blocking 14 trades (losers −$630, winners +$138).

router Δ vs no-routernet≥20net≥30net≥40net≥50net≥60
ER≥0.15+461+492 ★shipped+428+316+158
ER≥0.20+224+176+152+152+152
ER≥0.25+152+152+152+152+152
ER≥0.30+152+152+152+152+152
ER≥0.35+152+152+152+152+152

Set B — the FULL historical fader set (+ rgv_long, + exhaustion_short)

Baseline −$722. Router as shipped: +$584, a swing of +$1,307, blocking 57 trades (losers −$1,994, winners +$687). Note this set is 2.7× the size of the live one — because it includes the two faders the router is not currently allowed to manage.

router Δ vs no-routernet≥20net≥30net≥40net≥50net≥60
ER≥0.15+1,300+1,307 ★shipped+1,294+1,120+858
ER≥0.20+1,044+995+976+1,014+816
ER≥0.25+787+787+806+806+744
ER≥0.30+334+334+334+334+428
ER≥0.35+428+428+428+428+428

Both grids say the same three things. One: ER 0.15 is not a peak, it is a cliff edge — and we are on the right side of it. Everything at ER 0.20 and above is worth a third to a half as much, on both sets independently. Two: the net threshold barely matters between 20 and 40 — on the full set those three cells are +$1,300 / +$1,307 / +$1,294, thirteen dollars apart across the whole range. That is a genuine plateau and 30 sits in the middle of it. Three: the shipped cell is the best cell on both sets. Whoever set ER 0.15 / net 30 got it right and there is nothing to gain by moving either.

The timing sweep — where the actual gain is

900 configurations, thresholds crossed with timings. The joint winner is the same config on both universes, which is the first thing that made me take it seriously:

knobshippedjoint bestwhat the change does in plain terms
ER threshold0.150.15unchanged
net threshold30pt20ptinside the flat plateau — a $13 difference on the full set. I would not take this one.
lookback window30 min45 minA longer window is a smoother read. This is the direct fix for the boundary flicker that produced 36 abs_veto_short flips.
poll step15 min5 minMatches the actual systemd tick. The router already runs every 5 minutes; its regime replay pretends it runs every 15.
hysteresis hold2 marks3 marks3×5min = 15 minutes of confirmation instead of 30. Faster to act, on a steadier signal.
fast_exitoffoffunchanged — still dormant, as it has been since it backtested +$0
 LIVE managed setFULL historical set
router value, shipped config+$190+$584
router value, joint-best config+$692+$984
improvement+$502+$399
trades blocked (shipped → best)14 → 2757 → 70
days helped / days hurt4 / 06 / 1
worst single day$+0−$10
days out of 40 that changed at all47

Now the honesty. That +$502 rests on four days out of forty. Thirty-six days are completely unaffected. It never hurt a single day on the live set and hurt one day by ten dollars on the full set, which is as clean a robustness profile as you can ask for — but it is clean because it is rare, not because it is reliable. Four positive events is not a proven edge; it is four positive events.

THE MODERATE RECOMMENDATION — and what I deliberately left out

TAKE: window 30→45 min, step 15→5 min, hold 2→3 marks. Three reasons, in order of weight. (a) It is the joint optimum on both universes independently — the live set and a set 2.7× larger with two different gates in it. (b) It never hurt a day on the live set and cost $10 on the worst day of the full set. (c) Most importantly, it is not a number found by searching — it is the mechanical fix for two problems this section documents from live evidence and not from the grid: a 45-minute window smooths the exact one-point boundary flicker that generated 36 flips on one gate, and 5-minute steps close the actuation lag that cost −$242 this week. When a grid search and a ledger written by hand arrive at the same answer from opposite directions, that is the strongest signal available here.

DO NOT TAKE: net 30→20. It is worth $13 on the full set and $31 on a live set of 14 trades. That is noise, it moves us to the edge of the plateau rather than the middle, and taking a free-looking knob change for no reason is how a config gets overfitted one dollar at a time. Leave ER 0.15 and net 30 exactly where they are.

⚠ SATURDAY, NOT NOW, AND WITH THE CAVEAT WRITTEN DOWN. The market is shut, so a Saturday deploy carries no live risk and gets a full session to be watched from a clean start. And the caveat that must go in the commit message: this was fitted on 40 days that are overwhelmingly ONE regime. This week alone ran 84% CHOP. The router is a trend instrument being tuned on a range tape. Re-run this study after the first genuine trend week and be ready to revert — the revert is a three-constant edit.

Is it blocking enough? The leak classification

every managed-fader trade, classifiedLIVE set n$FULL set n$whose problem is it?

The single biggest bucket of remaining loss is the near-miss leak: 30 trades, −$1,876 on the full set. These are counter-trend fader fires that the threshold missed by a whisker. That is the argument for loosening, and the grid answers it directly — ER 0.15 / net 20 blocks 13 more trades on each set and captures part of it, which is where the joint-best config’s gain actually comes from. It is also the argument I am declining, because the same grid says the money in that change is thirteen dollars on 40 days. The bigger prize is not a lower threshold. It is pointing the existing threshold at exhaustion_short.

5 · THE UNTRADEABLE-DAY METER, REVIEWED

src/gazbot7/untradeable.py. Three meters — ROUND-TRIP (does the day go anywhere), GIVE-BACK (how much of its own move it hands back), STOP-RATE (what fraction of recent trades stopped) — blended into a 0–100 score. 65 and above is STAY-OUT, 45 and above CAUTION. Five days is far too few to judge it, so I scored all 22 trading days the tape supports and put each against the money.

datescoreverdictchopgive-back stop-rateround-tripday $n
07-1629TRADEABLE2719530.73−535.0022
07-1748CAUTION4544600.55−152.0027
07-2094STAY-OUT9999730.01−1,732.5057
07-2122TRADEABLE1910530.81+52.5059
07-2253CAUTION5550530.45−329.5023
07-2346CAUTION5138530.49−398.0046
07-2426TRADEABLE2619400.74−578.5047
07-2747CAUTION4840600.52−226.5040
07-2856CAUTION6262330.38−54.0023
07-2923TRADEABLE255530.75+638.5017
07-3013TRADEABLE44470.96+1,172.0091
07-3186STAY-OUT9084800.10−905.0035
08-0334TRADEABLE4910530.51−460.5032
08-0422TRADEABLE2315330.77+330.5016
08-0544TRADEABLE5512870.45−817.0029
08-0678STAY-OUT8681580.14−137.5010
08-0715TRADEABLE165330.84+227.0023
08-1055CAUTION6236800.38−244.5018
08-1150CAUTION6550200.35+371.5014
08-1253CAUTION4946730.51−660.0030
08-1321TRADEABLE2014360.80+37.509
08-1477STAY-OUT8475670.16+170.5037

Does it discriminate? Yes — cleanly, and monotonically

verdictdaystotal $$ per daytrades$ per tradegreen days
STAY-OUT (≥65)4−2,604.50−651.12139−18.741 of 4
CAUTION (45–64)8−1,693.00−211.62221−7.661 of 8
TRADEABLE (<45)10+67.00+6.70345+0.196 of 10

That is a real instrument. Every step down the score is a step up in money, on every measure — dollars per day, dollars per trade, and share of green days. A STAY-OUT day costs nineteen dollars a trade; a TRADEABLE day breaks even. On the five days of this week alone the relationship looked like pure noise, which is exactly why five days was never going to answer the question.

Which of the three meters actually did the work?

Each meter split at its own median across the 22 days, high half against low half:

meterweight in the scoreHIGH half $ per dayLOW half $ per dayseparationverdict
STOP-RATE20%, and excluded below n=10−522.72+36.46$559.18★ The best discriminator of the three — and the one the module demotes.
ROUND-TRIP / chop40%−407.60−12.88$394.73Works. Correctly weighted heavily.
GIVE-BACK40%−368.45−16.14$352.32Works, slightly the weakest of the three.

All three discriminate, which is the first real finding — none of them is dead weight. The second is more awkward: the STOP-RATE meter separates best and it is the one carrying the least weight. It gets 20% when it votes at all, and since the 2026-08-05 change it is excluded entirely below ten trades — which on this week’s volumes means it was excluded on the days with the fewest trades, and the LOW half of its split is the only bucket in this entire table that is positive.

I am not recommending a re-weight, and the reason is the same n=22 discipline I applied everywhere else. The stop-rate meter is the only one of the three that is endogenous — it measures what the desk just did, not what the tape did. On a day the router has already benched everything, the stop-rate reads off a tiny sample of whatever was left armed. The 2026-08-05 change that demoted it was made for exactly that reason and it was right to make it. What this table justifies is re-testing the weight, not changing it. SHADOW

Does the 65 cutoff want tuning? — the actionable test

The full-day score is hindsight. The question that matters is the one the router faces live: at 15:00Z, does the meter predict the money still to come? The router only allows a stay-out past 15:00Z, so this is the exact decision. I swept the cutoff against post-15:00Z P&L over all 22 days.

cutoffdays benched$ avoided (post-15:00Z, on the benched days)days traded$ earned on them
3517 of 22+1,771.005+59.00
4012+1,562.5010−149.50
4510+1,642.0012−70.00
506+1,279.0016−433.00
556+1,279.0016−433.00
606+1,279.0016−433.00
65 ★ shipped6+1,279.0016−433.00
706+1,279.0016−433.00
755+1,279.0017−433.00
803+209.0019−1,503.00
85–901+282.0021−1,430.00

Baseline — trade every day after 15:00Z, no meter at all: −$1,712.00 over 22 days.

The answer is no, and it is a clean no. Everything from 50 to 75 gives an identical result — the same six days benched, the same $1,279 avoided. Sixty-five sits in the dead centre of a 25-point plateau. That is what a well-set threshold looks like: you can be off by ten in either direction and nothing happens. Leave it.

The grid does show cutoff 35 avoiding more — $1,771 — and leave-one-day-out picks 35 in 17 of 22 folds, which looks like a strong signal until you read what it is actually saying. Cutoff 35 benches seventeen of twenty-two days. That is not a stay-out rule, that is closing the afternoon desk. And it wins for a reason that has nothing to do with the meter: trading after 15:00Z lost −$1,712 across these 22 days full stop. On a sample where the activity is a net loser, any rule that does less of it scores well. The LOO test is picking up the base rate, not the instrument. I am refusing that recommendation and naming why.

TWO HONEST HOLES IN THE METER, NEITHER OF WHICH CHANGES THE CUTOFF

(1) It rests on one day. Of the $1,279 the 65 cutoff avoids, $1,009 — 79% — is 2026-07-20 alone. Strip that single day and the rule is worth $270 across the remaining five. The meter is real (the 22-day verdict table is monotone and that is not one day), but the dollar case for the cutoff is one day. Say it plainly and do not build on it.

(2) The module and the router use different numbers. untradeable.py defines STAY-OUT as score ≥65. The router’s live operating trigger, in its own words all week, is “two consecutive ≥55, past the 15:00Z cutoff”. Two thresholds, ten points apart, for one decision. Because 50–75 is a flat plateau neither is wrong and this has cost nothing — but it means the dashboard and the router can disagree about whether today is a stay-out day, and nobody would notice. Pick one number and put it in one place. SATURDAY, 10-MINUTE FIX

This week’s two misses, named. Friday scored 77, STAY-OUT — and the desk made +$170.50, its second-best day. Wednesday scored 53, only CAUTION — and lost −$660.00, the worst day of the week. Two clean misses in five days, in opposite directions. Against the 22-day record I read those as variance rather than a broken instrument, but they are exactly the pair that would make you distrust it if this week were all you had.

6 · THE BENCH-DECISION REVIEW — grading the week’s actual benches

Thirty-three benches. Eight blocked money (+$611), four gave money away (−$410), twenty-one were free. Beyond the arithmetic, four patterns are worth naming because each one is a rule.

What made a bench GOOD

Duration. Look at the two best calls — grind_long benched at 08-11 13:40 for 48.3 hours and again at 08-13 15:35 for 31.4 hours. Between them: 14 blocked signals, −$328. Every single one of the eight good benches ran for at least half an hour and six of them ran for over 20 hours. The good benches are the ones nobody touched. Compare the churn column: twelve benches lasted under thirty minutes and exactly one of those twelve blocked a single signal.

What made a bench BAD — and it is one gate, again

Three of the four over-benches were exhaustion_short, and together they cost $360.50. The worst was the 08-11 15:00Z stay-out: the meter read 80 after 78, past the cutoff, on a genuinely awful day (239pt of range for −6pt of net), and it was a correct call by the written rule — and it benched the one gate that was working for seven hours and gave up $183. That is the meter and the gate disagreeing, and the meter winning. Which is the right outcome to keep, because the same instrument is what stopped Wednesday being worse. I am recording this as the cost of a rule I want kept, not as an error to fix.

The bug that produced both ❌ BAD grades

Both bad calls this week are the same mechanism, both on Monday, and it has now fired three times in one day: arming a gate because its bench lapsed. The router’s own words at 17:45Z — “the 17:15 stay-out that benched exhaustion_short has now lapsed for three ticks, so I restore it.” Nothing made a case for the gate. The meter merely stopped making the case against it. A lapsed bench returns a gate to benched-by-default, not to armed. The gate was at that moment the day’s worst performer at −$137.50 with 4 of 6 lots stopped; it then took four more lots and stopped all four, for −$92.50. Had the 17:15 bench simply been left alone the window is flat. The same shape produced the 08-13 17:50 restore of abs_veto_short for −$139.50. Across the week this one pattern is worth −$232.00.

The bench that finally had a price — and it was positive

Two consecutive ledger entries this week assert that the long-side bench cost “exactly $0, zero SUPPRESSED-OPEN”. Friday afternoon produced 240 of them, all abs_veto_long, in two bursts at 15:46 and 16:01. The structural reason is worth carrying forward because it decides which benches can ever be free: deciders.py holds an ATR floor for grind_long (22.0) and capitulation_long (10.0) and for nothing else. On a tape running 2.6–12.5pt ATR that floor refused grind_long 1,932 times all by itself — its bench was free because the gate could not have fired anyway. abs_veto_long has no floor, so it was eligible the entire time, and its bench was the only long-side bench that could have a price. It priced GOOD: −$310 blocked across the week, and roughly $180 of that on Friday alone. Never assume a bench is free — price it from SUPPRESSED-OPEN per window.

7 · THE DURABLE ROUTER AND ITS ARCHITECTURE

Did the 5-minute tick manage correctly?

UTC dayticks firedexpectedservice failures ticks that made no decision%reading
08-11288288093%The 9 are the scheduled CME maintenance-halt skips. Perfect.
08-12288288093%Perfect.
08-13288288013447%See below.
08-14284284093%Perfect (partial day to 23:35Z).

On the narrow question the operator asked — did the timer manage correctly, without thrash — the answer is an unqualified yes. 1,148 ticks, every single one fired on schedule, zero service failures, zero timeouts, zero non-zero exits. Median tick 16 seconds against a 280-second timeout. The plumbing is not the problem and has not been for weeks.

⚠⚠ AND THAT IS EXACTLY WHY THURSDAY IS THE WEEK’S MOST IMPORTANT FINDING

On 2026-08-13, from 02:40:03Z to 13:00:02Z — ten hours and twenty minutes — the router made no decisions at all. One hundred and twenty-five consecutive ticks aborted on the same line:

ABORT: no JSON in output -> no change | raw='Failed to authenticate: OAuth session expired
and could not be refreshed'

Every one of those 125 ticks was reported by systemd as a success. “Starting… Deactivated successfully… Finished… Consumed 2.5s CPU time.” The service health was flawless. The service was doing nothing. A 2-second tick against a 16-second median is the only tell there was, and nothing was watching for it.

What it cost: $0 realised. Zero trades fired in the whole window. The switches froze in the state the 02:30Z tick left them, and the only thing that state blocked was one abs_veto_long signal at 12:31:05Z, which reprices at −$24.50 a lot — so the frozen bench made $49.00 on the two-lot gate.

⚠ That is luck and must be recorded as luck. The outage sat almost entirely inside the Asia block, where new entries are refused whatever the switches say, and the rest of it was a dead London morning. Run the same ten-hour blackout starting at 13:00Z instead of 02:40Z and it covers the US open. The correct reading is not “$0, no harm” — it is “a ten-hour unmonitored failure of the desk’s number-one lever, which happened to land in the only ten hours of the day where it was survivable.”

ALREADY FIXED — NOT AN ACTION scripts/router_health_check.py shipped the same day (commit 07cf6ef, “alarm the one thing nothing was watching”), with its timer first firing at 15:31:33Z — two and a half hours after the outage cleared. It alarms on an ABORT streak of 3 (≈15 minutes of a frozen router) and on a silent log, pages once on entry and re-nags hourly. It has run every two minutes since. Do not re-recommend this; it is done. The open question it raises is the generalising one: what else on this desk reports success while doing nothing?

The actuation-lag cost, quantified

The router decides on a 5-minute tick and needs two consecutive confirming marks, so a genuine regime change takes up to 30 minutes to reach gate_switches.env. Here is what that delay actually cost, measured as what happened in the ten minutes immediately before each flip took effect.

when (UTC)gateflipwhat slipped through$reading
08-12 13:55abs_veto_longBENCH2 live trades−133.00Both taken in the last 10 minutes before the bench landed.
08-13 15:58abs_veto_shortBENCH2 live trades−101.00The execution-pathology bench — the trades were already on by the time it fired.
08-14 19:45exhaustion_shortBENCH2 live trades−31.00 
08-11 14:10abs_veto_longARM1 missed signal+65.50Being late DODGED a loser.
08-12 12:35abs_veto_longARM1 missed signal+55.00Dodged another.
08-12 13:40abs_veto_shortARM1 missed signal−49.50Missed a winner.
08-13 13:35abs_veto_longARM1 missed signal−48.00Missed a winner.
★ TOTAL ACTUATION-LAG COST FOR THE WEEK−242.00 bench-late −$265.00  ·  arm-late +$23.00

The lag is asymmetric and the asymmetry is exactly the one the router’s own policy assumes. Being late to BENCH cost real money every single time — three flips, six trades, −$265, no exceptions. Being late to ARM was free: four flips, two missed winners worth $97.50 and two dodged losers worth $120.50, netting +$23 in the desk’s favour. So the standing rule — when unsure, sit benched one more tick, and re-arm the moment evidence is real — is now measured, not assumed. It is worth about $242 a week to make the bench side faster and roughly nothing to make the arm side faster. That is a direct, independent argument for the step 15→5 change in section 4, arrived at from live money rather than from a grid.

The architecture question the week keeps asking

Two of the week’s three structural problems are not solvable by tuning the router, because they are not the router’s decisions. The 22:00Z reactivation timer made the two largest armings of the week (−$367.50 and +$310.00) and it is a clock, not a judgement — nothing grades it, and it re-arms gates the router benched hours earlier with no memory of why. The lapsed-bench unwind (−$232.00 this week) is the same defect in a different costume: a bench expiring is treated as an argument for arming. Both are the same bug — a permission that expires into the wrong default. The fix is one line of policy rather than any new mechanism: a bench that lapses returns a gate to BENCHED-BY-DEFAULT, and an arm needs a positive case made fresh. The router’s prompt already says this in words; the 22:00Z timer overrides it in code.

8 · VERDICT AND SATURDAY RECOMMENDATIONS

The verdict on the week: the router is doing its job, its job is currently very small, and the biggest gains available are in what it is pointed at rather than how it is tuned. It was worth +$400 by the nightly, +$51.50 by direct grading of its 64 decisions, and it did that on a tape that was 84% chop — the regime where a direction router has least to do. It thrashed one gate badly and left the rest alone. Its thresholds came out best-in-grid on both test sets. And it spent ten hours dead on Thursday without anyone knowing.

The honest frame on all of it. This is one week, 108 tournament trades, 64 router decisions, on a tape that was 84% chop. Every threshold sweep here is fitted on 40 days of overwhelmingly one regime and is in-sample to that regime. The two backfilled nightly rollups are tick-honest estimates — a benched gate’s trade never ran, so its P&L is a reconstruction, and section 3 shows the reconstruction is systematically optimistic in trend. Where a number rests on a single day I have said which day and what it is worth without it. The one recommendation I would still make if you told me half of this was wrong is #1 — a live up-fader firing into confirmed up-trends, red on 7 of 8 days, invisible to the detector built to catch exactly that.

M1 The biggest runs on MNQ this week — did we show up?

Forget what the desk did this week — start from the raw tape. MNQ printed 68 runs of 1.5×ATR or bigger in the last seven days (a 15-minute move of at least 44 points, against a typical 15-min range of 29). That is the whole tape, not a top-ten. We caught 3, we were positioned against 5, and we sat out 60. The runs we aligned with banked +$237 of honest money — but that is only 50% of their $470 hindsight ceiling, and the 60 we sat out left $8024 on the table. ★ REV2 — the bleed is NOT the fading. On this week’s census the five runs we were positioned against came to +$91.50, not a loss — and even that is generous, because +$434.00 of it is the DAY RIDER, a different desk that happened to be short inside one of the windows. Strip the rider out and the tournament’s fading around the runs is −$342.50 on 5 runs, which is real but small. The money on the table is the 60 runs we were not in at all and their $8,024 ceiling. This is a not-showing-up problem, not a fading problem, and that is what Movements 2 and 3 go after.

THE SAME CENSUS ON GOLD — MGC, the second instrument

M1 The biggest runs on MGC this week — did we show up?

Forget what the desk did this week — start from the raw tape. That is the whole tape, not a top-ten. ★ REV2 — gold gets its own words. The MNQ template produced nonsense here and it has been rewritten. We caught 0, we fought 0 and we sat out all 74, and there is nothing surprising in that: the desk has never placed a single trade in gold. Not one MNQ gate is even eligible on MGC — they are wired to the Nasdaq slate, and the scope is explicit that a gold gate must be invented fresh rather than ported. So 100% of the $10,649 single-lot ceiling is on the table by construction, and any sentence about “what the fading gave back” is meaningless, because there was no fading, no catching and no position of any kind.

The honest question this census can answer is a different one: which of these 74 runs would the gold candidates in Part 2.5 Part A have been in? That is where the MGC day rider and the coil bouncer earn or fail to earn their shadow slates (BUILD #5), and it is the only way this table becomes a measurement rather than a list. Note the multiplier while reading every dollar below: MGC is $10.00 a point, five times MNQ — a 12-point gold run is $120 a lot, not $24.

THE FULL CENSUS

What the runs were — by cause

Each run gets a cause fingerprint from its pre-run tape. This is the map for where to hunt a new gate.

ClusterRunsWhat it is
UNCLASS41no clear tape tell (quiet ignition)
OPEN/NEWS15the 13:00–15:00 UTC cash-open / data window
VACUUM10moved AGAINST the tape (a snap-back / stop-run)
FLOW-LED6aggressors drove it (a continuation)
VOL-EXPANSION2amplitude broke out of a coil
HONEST MONEY — ceiling vs what we banked
RunsHindsight ceilingWhat we really made
Caught (aligned)0$0+$0  (0%)
Fought (against)0$0$+0
Sat out74$10649$0 ← the money on the table

★ REV2 — there is no fading on gold to give anything back. Every row above is a run the desk was not in, could not have been in, and has no instrument pointed at. The line that used to sit here was the MNQ template with the numbers swapped.

What the book column says now that it exists. Across all 74 runs the far-side depth share has a median of 0.505 — dead centre — and the side price ran toward was thin (below 0.50) before 30 of 74 runs, 41%. Split by direction it is 16 of 36 up-runs and 14 of 38 down-runs. The gold book does not telegraph a run at 250 ms resolution; it is a coin. That is the same answer Movement 3 reaches from the other end and much harder: on 397 book breaks, “the far side is empty” loses −$2,059.50 at the 4.8th percentile of its own placebo, i.e. the feature carries real information and the sign is inverted. A gold level that breaks into a vacuum is one nobody is defending, and price comes straight back. Read this column as a null with a hint of an inversion, and read Movement 3 for the test that priced it.

The next two movements ask whether we can do better — first with the gates we own, then with new ones.

M2 The idle-gate lab — could the six gates we already own have caught the 60 misses?

Movement 1 froze the tape: 68 big runs on MNQ this week, and we sat out 60 of them, carrying a hindsight ceiling of $8,024 on a single lot. Before anyone invents a new gate, this movement asks the cheaper question: we already own six gates — if we had simply left all six armed all week, would any of them have been in those runs? So I took all six live tournament gates straight out of deciders.py, fired them mechanically (exactly as the desk runs them — live parameters, live ATR floors, the live 55-second absorption veto, the live 5-second exhaustion confirm and its counter-regime veto) and again ungated (the raw gate shape with every filter stripped off), in-direction only, anywhere in the ten minutes before each run ignited, and repriced every exit tick-by-tick on this week's real capture.db trade prints under each gate's deployed two-lot exit. The mechanical answer is +$15.50 on six fires across 57 testable runs — and strip the single best trade and that becomes −$94.50. Ungated it is +$379.50 on 27 fires, +$69.50 once you remove the best three. That is an HONEST NULL, and this week I can tell you the mechanism in one line with a number behind it: this week's runs ignited out of unusually quiet tape (median peak ATR in the ten minutes before ignition: 12.0 points), and the desk's two biggest magnitude floors are set at 22 and 20 points — they are calibrated to stand down in exactly the tape the runs come out of.

LEAD WITH WHAT SURVIVED

Four things came out of this alive. Three of them are small and one of them is a correction to what this section told you last week, so I want all four in front of the null rather than buried behind it.

  1. The two short FADERS were the only gates that made money, and they were perfect: 3 fires, 3 winners, +$302.50. exhaustion_short fired twice for +$192.50 and rgv_short once for +$110.00, all three mechanically, all three with both lots reaching target. Every other gate lost. That is three trades, so it is a SHADOW lead and nothing more — but it is the first time in this section that the mechanical book has had a green column at all, and it is worth saying that the fade gates fired 9–10 minutes ahead of ignition and still won, which the timing ladder says should be nearly impossible.
  2. The 08-08 grind_long ATR 10 → 22 revert is VINDICATED on run tape. With no floor at all grind fires 5 times for −$178; with the deployed ATR≥22 floor it fires once for −$120. The floor blocked four runs that were collectively losing money. This section is usually where floors get accused of blocking the good stuff; this week the floor is doing its job.
  3. Last week's headline — "grind_long's ext_hi 2.0 ceiling is the single filter standing between us and these runs" — does NOT hold on this week's tape. The ceiling was deleted on 08-08 (it now falls back to 4.0). I re-fired grind under the old cell, the new cell and a 3.0 cell on the same 23 up-runs: all three produce the identical single fire and the identical −$120. The binding clause is not the extension ceiling at all — it is "VWAP slope not up", which killed grind on 14 of its 23 eligible runs. Removing ext_hi bought us nothing here.
  4. capitulation_long's require_flip is the one live filter that cost real money on this population: +$158.50. Ungated the gate fired six times for +$116.50; mechanically it fired zero times. Three of those six were blocked by the ATR≥10 floor (and that floor saved $42 — two losers and a small winner), but the other three were blocked by require_flip, and two of them were $97 winners. Thin — three trades — and require_flip was re-derived on the whole book on 08-02 with an ordering-correct win rate, so this number must not be allowed to overturn it. It is a shadow question, not a change.

How the test was run — so you can trust the sign

Everything below comes from two scripts run once each against the frozen census: scripts/idle_gate_backtest_wk33.py and its companion scripts/idle_gate_timing_decay_wk33.py. No number in this section is carried over from any prior week; where I quote last week it is to contradict it, and the contradiction is computed here.

ComponentRule
PopulationThe 60 sat-out runs of the frozen Movement-1 census, read straight out of the locked census stdout — not re-derived, not re-filtered, not re-run (run_census.py is a multi-minute tick crunch and the whole point of freezing it is that every movement reads the same snapshot).
Testable subset57 of 60. capture.db holds five trading days of ticks and its first print this week is 08-10 00:00:00 UTC. Three runs sit before that or need a pre-window that does — 08-09 22:02, 08-09 23:47 and 08-10 00:02 — and they are excluded rather than faked. I checked the Parquet lake for them too: it has no 2026-08-09 tick file, so the Sunday-evening reopen genuinely has no tick tape anywhere on this desk. The 57 testable runs carry $7,619 of the sat-out ceiling and the three dropped runs carry $406. (The census headline is $8,024 and the printed per-run ceilings sum to $8,025 — a dollar of rounding, not a discrepancy.)
Why capture.db and not the lakeThe census window is 08-09 → 08-14, i.e. this week, which is strictly inside capture.db's five-trading-day window — and capture.db is the only source that has Friday 08-14 at all (the lake's last mirrored tick day is 08-13). For a one-week study it is both legal and the more complete source. Anything multi-week would have to go to the lake.
GatesAll six live tournament slots: grind_long, capitulation_long, abs_veto_long on the long side; rgv_short, exhaustion_short, abs_veto_short on the short side. The actual decider functions are imported and called — there is no re-implementation to drift out of sync. (nipc_long/nipc_short ship benched and are not part of the live six.)
DirectionIn-direction only. A long gate is tested only on the 23 up-runs, a short gate only on the 34 down-runs. We are asking "could it have been in the move", not "could it have been near it".
WindowThe ten minutes before ignition. The three bar gates are tested on each of the ten completed 1-minute bars; the two tape gates (capitulation, exhaustion) are tested every 5 seconds across the whole window. First fire wins.
Mechanical modeLive params and live filters, as deployed today: grind ATR≥22 and no ext ceiling (both changed on 08-08); capitulation ATR≥10 + require_flip; abs_veto amp_floor 0.0004 plus the 55-second absorption veto and its 55-second entry delay; rgv_short ATR≥20; exhaustion_short plus its 5-second confirm-veto and its veto_counter_regime entry veto (that last one was missing from last week's harness — it is in now, and it blocked nothing).
Ungated modeThe raw gate shape. ATR floors off, amplitude floor off, extension ceiling off entirely, require_flip off, all three vetoes off. The gate's core geometry is kept — this is "filters off", not "a different gate".
ExitEach gate's deployed two-lot exit from data/exit_overrides.json — Lot A fixed-R, Lot B chandelier or fixed-R, one lot each — plus the quiet-tape clip below 22pt entry ATR and the native 1×ATR protective stop on the loss side. Repriced on every real trade print, 60-minute hold cap.
Costs$1.50 per lot round trip, $2.00 per point (MNQ). Two lots a fire, so $3.00 a fire before anything happens.
Bars1-minute bars built from the 5-second bars with integer flooring (bar_ts − bar_ts % 60) — deliberately, because the report's own instrument-failure list flags the float-division version as a silent no-op that reads 5-second bars as minutes.

Data actually consumed: 5,172,092 trade ticks (08-10 00:00:00 → 08-14 21:00:00 UTC), 13.96 million level-1 book rows, 6,780 one-minute bars inside the tick window.

Read that table and the pattern is not subtle. All three winners are SHORT fades. All three losers are MOMENTUM entries taken in whipsaw or chop. The three winners also fired 3.0, 9.0 and 9.6 minutes ahead of ignition, which by the decay ladder further down should be dead ground — a fade that is right about the level does not care much when you took it, because the level is where the money is. The three losers fired 6–7 minutes early, chasing something that had not started.

The same six with every filter switched off

Now strip the ATR floors, the amplitude floor, the extension ceiling, the flip requirement and all three vetoes. This is the "leave everything on, damn the filters" book — the fair upper bound on what the gates we own could physically have done.

DO NOT GET EXCITED ABOUT THE +$379.50

It is a handful of $97 target-hits. Strip the best one and it falls to +$269.50; strip the best three and it falls to +$69.50 across 24 remaining fires — about fourteen dollars a day, on a hindsight-selected population, in-sample, with no arming rule attached to it. Day by day it is +$96.50 / +$373.00 / +$191.00 / −$281.00 / $0.00, and leaving out the best day (08-11) drops the whole book to +$6.50 — so one Tuesday is the book. Costs are not the problem: stress the fee to $3.00 a lot round trip and it is still +$298.50, to $6.00 and it is still +$136.50. Twenty-seven fires and a single load-bearing day is the problem. And note the last day in that list: Friday 08-14 had six sat-out runs and drew zero fires from six gates with every filter removed.

The line that matters is the second-to-last pair. Even with every filter removed, 33 of the 57 testable runs draw no fire at all — and those 33 carry $4,167 of the $7,619. The biggest run of the entire week, the +236pt monster at 08-13 13:32 with a $472 ceiling, was touched exactly once in this whole study: ungated capitulation_long fired 115 seconds ahead of it and banked +$18.00. Eighteen dollars of four hundred and seventy-two.

Why each gate misses — the mechanism, clause by clause

For every eligible run I recorded how far down its own rule chain each gate ever got in the ten-minute window, and kept the deepest reach. This turns "it didn't fire much" into "here is the exact sentence that stopped it".

grind_long — there was no established up-trend to ride, and that is the whole story

Where the gate stoppedRuns (of 23)Mode
VWAP slope not up — no established up-trend14both
ATR floor 22pt not met4mechanical only
Price not riding above VWAP (ext < 0.3 ATR)2both
Already stretched (ext > 4.0 ATR)2mechanical only
FIRES1mechanical

Sixty-one percent of the up-runs never even reached grind's first test. A continuation gate needs a trend to continue; these runs did not have one, they were the trend arriving. That is the census finding restated from inside the gate: big moves rupture flat tape.

abs_veto_long / abs_veto_short — the five-bar thrust simply isn't there yet

Where the gate stoppedabs_veto_long (23 up-runs)abs_veto_short (34 down-runs)
No 5-bar thrust ≥ 1.5 ATR1618
Amplitude floor: tape too thin (atr% < 0.04%)56
Thrust is the wrong way04
No volume surge on the thrust bar12
55-second absorption VETO (burst absorbed)03
FIRES11

Seven in ten up-runs (16 of 23) and just over half the down-runs (18 of 34) never produce a 1.5-ATR five-minute burst in their own direction in the ten minutes before they go. Of course they don't — the burst is the run. The amplitude floor then removes another 5–6 each, correctly: the pre-run tape is genuinely thin.

rgv_short — the ATR floor stands it down on two-thirds of the tape

Where the gate stoppedMechanical (34 down-runs)Ungated (34 down-runs)
ATR floor 20pt not met22—
Regime stand-down (vol too high / slope too steep)99
Not stretched ≥ 1.5 ATR above VWAP216
No fresh down-turn07
FIRES12

This is the clearest single number in the section. The ATR≥20 floor stands rgv_short down on 22 of its 34 eligible runs — and independently of the gate, I measured the peak ATR reached anywhere in each run's ten-minute pre-window: only 6 of the 34 down-runs ever get to 20 points at all. Take the floor away and the binding clause simply moves to "not stretched above VWAP" on 16 runs, and the gate still only fires twice. The floor is not what is stopping rgv_short from catching runs; the geometry is. Both fires it did get were winners, so nothing here argues for touching it.

Three trades either side. I am not going to pretend that overturns the 08-02 re-derivation, which measured require_flip on the whole book with an ordering-correct win rate and found the loose version (capit_loose) at −$5,250 over 238 fires. What I will say is that on run tape specifically, the flip requirement arrives too late twice out of three, and that is a shadow-book question with a clean shape.

exhaustion_short — barely exists, but everything it does is right

Where the gate stoppedRuns (of 34)

FIRES2

Mechanical and ungated are identical — neither the 5-second confirm-veto nor the veto_counter_regime entry veto blocked a single thing on this population, so the gate's two live safety catches cost nothing here. It fired twice and won twice, +$192.50. Note the second row: on ten runs the gate correctly refused because the flow did move price. That is a fade gate declining to fade a real move, which is exactly right — and it is also the most interesting negative signal in this whole section, because "heavy flow that IS moving price" is a clean, cheap, already-implemented detector of a move that is genuinely underway.

The killer number: near a run, our gates fire at roughly chance

Here is the check that turns "they didn't fire much" into "they are not looking at anything predictive". Across the whole tick-covered week I counted every minute (bar gates) or 20-second sample (tape gates) in which each gate's condition was true, then asked what share of those fell inside one of the 57 ten-minute pre-run windows. Those windows cover 9.2% of the minute bars and 8.4% of the tape samples — that is the chance rate. A gate that knew something about imminent runs would sit well above it.

GateModeSignal-minutes / samples all weekInside a pre-run windowSharevs chance
grind_longmechanical44818.2%1.97× (n=44)
grind_longungated1,118928.2%0.89×
capitulation_longmechanical19315.8%1.88× (n=19)
capitulation_longungated79810.1%1.21×
abs_veto_longmechanical25416.0%1.73× (n=25)
abs_veto_longungated231208.7%0.94×
rgv_shortmechanical11100%10.8× (n=1 — meaningless)
rgv_shortungated12254.1%0.44×
exhaustion_shorteither8112.5%1.49× (n=8)
abs_veto_shortmechanical44715.9%1.72× (n=44)
abs_veto_shortungated252156.0%0.64×

Every ungated cell — the only ones with enough signal to mean anything — sits between 0.44× and 1.21× chance, and four of the five are below chance. The mechanical rows all read 1.7–2.0×, which looks encouraging until you notice they are built on 19–44 signal-minutes across a whole week — that is 8, 3, 4 and 7 raw observations landing in the windows. There is a real and slightly hopeful hint in there (the filtered versions are more concentrated near runs than the raw versions, consistently, across four independent gates), and I am flagging it rather than dismissing it — but at these counts it is one or two coincidences wide. The honest statement is that none of our six gates is meaningfully more likely to be live before a big run than at a random moment of the week.

One caveat stated plainly: these are signal-minutes, not trades. grind_long showing 1,118 ungated signal-minutes does not mean 1,118 entries — the live desk holds one position per slot and was router-benched for stretches of the week. The column measures how often the condition was TRUE, which is the right measure for this particular question and the wrong one for any other.

Where the money actually is: the timing-decay ladder

This is the table I would pin on the wall. It takes hindsight completely for granted — you know which runs, you know the direction — and asks only how early you enter. Same 57 runs, same Lot A 1.5R + Lot B wide-chandelier exit, same tick-honest repricing, entry stepped backwards from the ignition bar.

THE WHOLE SECTION IN THREE SENTENCES

Enter at the ignition bar with perfect direction and these 57 runs pay $66.60 each, 75% of them green. The money halves within thirty seconds, holds a soft shelf worth $18–$29 a run out to four minutes, falls to +$6.80 at five minutes, and is negative from seven minutes out — with strip-best-3 negative in every row past five minutes.

Now put the gates on that ladder. When they do fire, they fire a median of 6.6 minutes early mechanically and 6.7 minutes early ungated (mechanical range 3.0–9.6 minutes). That is the exact rung where the ladder crosses zero and starts paying −$18.80 a run. The six gates are not losing at these runs because their exits are wrong or their thresholds are wrong. They are firing in the one part of the window where being right about the direction is worth nothing.

One honest difference from the last time this ladder was run: the shelf out to four minutes is new. Last week the curve was through zero by two minutes; this week there is real money at two, three and four minutes early (+$28.10, +$17.70, +$21.70 a run). I do not think that is a discovery, I think it is a quieter tape with slower ignitions, and the fact that it moved this much on a one-week sample is itself the caveat — do not build anything on the exact shape of this curve. What has replicated across both weeks is the two ends: entering at the ignition bar is worth several times entering a minute before it, and entering seven minutes before it is worth less than nothing.

Regime segmentation — scored on home turf, as the discipline requires

Nothing above should be read as one number sprayed across the whole tape, so here is the same population cut by regime at ignition. Buckets key on entry ATR and the 30-bar efficiency ratio measured on the last completed bar before the run, plus a 60-minute range-break test — not on the clock. This week's ATR quartiles across the sat-out runs came out at 8.3pt (p25) and 15.1pt (p75), and those are the cut points. Both are dramatically lower than last week's 12.3 / 25.4, which tells you the headline fact about this week's tape on its own.

Only 3 of the 57 big runs ignited out of a proven trend (ER ≥ 0.45), and exactly one out of a clean one. Forty-five ignited out of chop — normal or dead — or violent whipsaw, and the remaining nine out of BUILDING, which is direction forming but not yet proven. That is the same uncomfortable finding as last week, and it is now two weeks old rather than one: the runs are not the continuation of something we could see. They are ruptures in tape that looked like nothing.

Three honest reads. (1) Every bucket is between n=0 and n=14, and I am not going to pretend a policy can be built from that. (2) The one consistent sign is the negative one: VIOLENT-WHIPSAW loses in both modes — three mechanical fires at −$115.50 and four ungated at −$192.50, and it holds the two worst individual trades in the study (grind −$120 and abs_veto_short −$91). If any regime rule survives this section it is a bench rule, not an arm rule. (3) Whipsaw was also the losing bucket last week, which is the one cross-week agreement in the regime table — but note it is the opposite of what the blind ladder says (below), where whipsaw is the best regime. Those two facts together say something specific: whipsaw runs are worth a lot if you are in them and expensive if you are guessing at them. The gates are guessing.

Time-of-day is the cleaner split than regime: with perfect direction a US-session run is worth four times an overnight one ($41.00 vs $10.10 a run) at more than half again the win rate. That is a real and stable-looking asymmetry and it belongs in Movement 3's design brief.

The R-target sweep — and what it says about the cheat-sheet guesses

The discipline says every R is a guess until it is swept, so I swept it. Six and 27 fires is not a population you can sweep anything on, so I swept on the blind one-minute-early book instead (perfect direction, 57 runs), with the quiet-tape clip switched off — with the clip on, the R target is a no-op on any entry under 22pt ATR and the sweep would be measuring nothing. Both lots at the same R so the number reads cleanly.

R targetAll 57Win %US (26)Overnight (31)Dead-chop (10)Normal-chop (24)Whipsaw (11)Building (9)

2.0R+1,47549%+1,274+201+18+251+956+69

4.0R+3,58544%+2,811+774+220+791+1,751+438
6.0R+4,34944%+3,071+1,278+424+1,103+1,928+509

What is PROVEN here, and what is not. This is a hindsight-direction population, so it cannot prove an R for a live gate — nothing here says "set abs_veto_short to 4R". What it can prove, because the entries are identical in every row and only the target moves, is the shape of exit that run tape rewards, and that shape is unambiguous: the curve rises monotonically from 0.5R all the way to 6.0R while the win rate falls from 63% to 44%. There is no plateau below 4R and no turnover anywhere. Every single regime column is negative at 0.5R except whipsaw, and every single one is positive at 4R. This is the identical finding as last week on a completely different tape, which is the closest thing to replication this section has produced.

Robustness on the best cell, and this one holds up better than last week's. Leave-one-day-out on the 6.0R headline of +$4,349: without 08-10 +$2,882, without 08-11 +$3,662, without 08-12 +$4,204, without 08-13 +$3,239, without 08-14 +$3,409. Positive on all five folds, worst fold still 66% of the headline — no single session carries it, which is a genuinely better result than last week's 49% swing. The finding remains "small targets are wrong on run tape", not "use 6R" — a 6R target on a real gate with a real win rate is a different animal from a 6R target on hindsight-perfect direction.

The three live filters that moved money on this population

grind_long's 08-08 config change — re-fired under every cell we have shipped

Last week this section named ext_hi 2.0 as "the single filter standing between us and these runs". The ceiling was deleted on 08-08 and the ATR floor went 10 → 22 at the same time, so I re-fired grind on this week's 23 up-runs under four cells — identical entries logic, identical exits — to see which knob actually binds.

Cellext ceilingATR floorFiresNet $Win %Runs blocked by the ATR floor
deployed (08-08)none (4.0 default)221−120.000%4
wk32 cell (08-01)2.0101−120.000%1
ext_hi 3.0 candidate3.0221−120.000%1
no ATR floor at allnone (4.0 default)05−178.0020%0

Two clean conclusions, and they both go against last week's write-up.

  • The extension ceiling is not the binding constraint on run tape. 2.0, 3.0 and "none" all produce the same single fire and the same −$120. Deleting it bought us nothing on this population. The clause that actually kills grind is "VWAP slope not up", on 14 of 23 runs, and no setting of ext_hi touches that.
  • The ATR≥22 floor is the only knob doing work, and it is doing GOOD work. Remove it and grind takes four extra trades — −$86, −$31, +$97, −$38 — for a net −$58. The four runs it blocked had entry ATRs of 20.6, 6.8, 9.5 and 8.7 points. That is the floor stopping a trend-continuation gate from buying in dead tape, which is precisely what it was reverted to do on 08-08.

The one fire that survives every cell is 08-10 13:56 — a +109pt up-run out of VIOLENT-WHIPSAW at 29.1pt ATR. Grind got in six minutes early and both lots stopped for −$120. Being allowed to trade the biggest whipsaw of the week was not the same as being able to trade it.

The 55-second absorption veto — a small net positive this week

Mechanical and ungated fire from the identical bar and differ only by the 55-second wait plus the absorption test, so the two can be separated.

RunMoveUngated (enter now)MechanicalEffect of the veto
08-10 00:22−59+97.00vetoed−97.00
08-12 15:30−47−69.00vetoed+69.00
08-13 15:43−61−78.00vetoed+78.00
08-10 15:20−62−91.00−91.000.00 — the 55s wait changed nothing
Net effect———+$50.00

Three blocks: two losers and one winner, netting +$50 saved. And on the one thrust that survived the test, the 55-second delay cost exactly nothing — both entries stopped for the same −$91. That is a different verdict from last week, where the wait was the villain (it cost $596.50 on two winners). I am flagging the disagreement rather than papering over it: on four fires in one week the veto is mildly positive, on five fires the week before it was strongly negative, and neither sample can settle it. The Saturday job is unchanged — sweep the wait at 0/15/25/40 seconds keeping the absorption test intact, on the FULL book, not on run tape.

The quiet-tape clip — a chop tool standing in the road, again

Every gate in data/exit_overrides.json carries the 08-02 quiet-tape clip: when entry ATR is below 22 points, Lot A banks a flat $40 and Lot B banks the larger of 1.75R and $60, and nothing else in the exit stack gets to run. I priced identical entries with the clip on and off.

MeasureAt ignition1 minute early5 minutes early
Runs where the clip was active at entry (ATR < 22pt)55 of 5755 of 5755 of 57
Clip ON (as deployed)+$3,799.00+$1,377.00+$389.50
Same entries, same exit, clip OFF+$4,929.50+$2,289.00+$1,222.00
Cost of the clip on run tape−$1,130.50−$912.00−$832.50

Fifty-five of fifty-seven runs this week ignited from tape quiet enough to trigger the clip — up from 35 of 53 last week, because this was a much quieter week (median peak pre-run ATR 12.0pt, against a clip threshold of 22). So on precisely the trades where a run is about to happen, the desk is wearing an exit that caps Lot A at forty dollars. The clip is doing exactly what it was built to do; nobody ever told it that a 12-point-ATR tape can produce a 236-point run. This is a regime carve-out question, not a delete question — the clip earns its keep on the whole book, which is where it was justified, and this population is selected for the one situation that makes it look worst. But it is now the second consecutive week it has cost roughly a thousand dollars on run tape, and that is worth a shadow.

The tape shape — the number that explains the whole null

Separately from any gate, I measured what the ten minutes before each run actually looked like: the peak ATR reached, the furthest price ever got from VWAP, the biggest five-bar thrust. This is the honest physical answer to "why didn't anything fire".

What the pre-run tape looked likeValue
Peak ATR in the 10 minutes before ignition (median of 57 runs)12.0pt
— lowest / highest across the 575.5pt / 37.7pt
Up-runs that EVER reach grind_long's ATR≥22 floor3 of 23
Down-runs that EVER reach rgv_short's ATR≥20 floor6 of 34
Runs that EVER reach capitulation_long's ATR≥10 floor35 of 57
Runs where a 5-bar thrust EVER reaches 1.5 ATR (abs_veto's trigger, either direction)42 of 57
Peak distance from VWAP (median)2.8 ATR

There it is in one line. Three of the 23 up-runs and six of the 34 down-runs ever get anywhere near the magnitude floors our two floor-carrying gates require. Those floors were derived on the whole book, where they are right — the ATR≥22 revert alone was worth thousands on the live book, and it blocked losers here too. But on the specific population of "moves we sat out", they are structurally incompatible with the tape: the runs come out of quiet, and the floors exist to keep us out of quiet. That is not a bug and it is not a bug to fix by lowering the floors — the no-floor grind test above shows lowering them loses money. It is the reason a NEW instrument is needed, which is Movement 3's brief.

The verdict

HONEST NULL — and here is what it is a null about

DON'T ARM. There is no version of "leave the six gates on and let them catch the misses" that makes money this week. Mechanically it is +$15.50 on six fires — nominally green, but strip the single best trade and it is −$94.50, and it rests entirely on one Tuesday (leave 08-11 out and it is −$191.50). Ungated it is +$379.50 on 27 fires, which is +$69.50 once three trades come out and +$6.50 once 08-11 comes out. Neither is a book you would put a lot behind, and both are in-sample on a hindsight-selected population with no arming rule attached.

But the null is specific, not general, and the specific part is where next week's work is:

  • It is not an exit problem. Proven by the timing ladder: give the same exits perfect entries at the ignition bar and these 57 runs pay +$3,799 at 75% win. The exits are fine. (The one exit finding that is real is the quiet-tape clip, worth $832–$1,130 on this tape depending on entry timing.)
  • It is not a threshold problem. Proven by the ungated book: strip every filter off all six gates and 33 of 57 runs still draw no fire at all, carrying $4,167 of the $7,619 ceiling and nine of the fifteen biggest moves. And the filters we do have were, on balance, helping this week — grind's ATR floor saved $58, the 55s veto saved $50, capitulation's ATR floor saved $42; only require_flip cost anything ($158.50).
  • It is a timing-and-mechanism problem. Proven three ways: the gates fire at 0.44–1.21× chance in the pre-run windows once you have enough signal to measure, four of five below chance; when they do fire it is a median of 6.6–6.7 minutes early, where the blind ladder pays −$18.80 a run; and the tape they would have to fire in has a median peak ATR of 12 points against floors of 20 and 22. Our six gates are three continuation gates that need the move to have already happened and three fade gates that need a spent move to sell into. A big run is neither of those things until it has started.
  • The one green thread worth pulling. The three mechanical winners were all SHORT FADES, all with both lots reaching target, two of them fired 9–10 minutes early. Fades do not care about timing the way momentum does, because they are aimed at a price rather than at a moment. That is three trades and I will not dress it up — but it is the only positive mechanism in the section and it points somewhere.

Disposition — every lead touched in this movement

★ REV2 — the four SHADOW rows below are now plays

Rev 1 dispositioned four leads in this table as SHADOW and none of them reached the action card, which under this desk’s own doctrine is a lost week of incubation — a shadow slate costs nothing and needs no Saturday deploy. They are now BUILD #14 (the two short faders as run-catchers), #15 (capitulation_long “flip OR 90-second timeout”), #16 (suspend the quiet-tape clip on a range-break ignition — second consecutive week at roughly a grand) and #17 (exhaustion_short’s “the flow DID move price” clause, inverted into a bench instrument). The PARKED and REFUTED rows are unchanged and deliberately carry no action.

It was also the worst cell last week on a different tape. Wins often, loses money — the classic small-target signature.
THE ONE STONE STILL UNTURNED

Everything in this movement tested entries before ignition, because that is what "could we have caught it" normally means. The decay ladder says the highest-paying rung by a distance is the one at zero (+$66.60 a run, 75% green) and that the money is still real at thirty seconds (+$29.40) and holds a shelf out to four minutes. We have never built a gate whose job is to arm on the first thirty seconds of a move that has already started, on 5-second bars, with no attempt to predict anything.

Two things this week added to that. First, the three winners here were all fades, and fades were the only entries whose money survived being early — because a fade aims at a price, not at a moment. Second, exhaustion_short handed us a ready-made ignition detector by accident: it refused ten runs because the aggressive flow WAS moving price. Inverted, that clause is a twenty-second "a real move is underway" test that already runs on this desk's tape, costs nothing, and fires exactly where the ladder says the money is. Combine that with the US-session skew (+$41.00 a run vs +$10.10 overnight) and you have the shape of the thing Movement 3 should be trying to build.

Honest scope on all of it: one week, five trading days, 57 runs, in-sample, hindsight-selected, on the quietest tape of the summer. Nothing in this section is a Saturday deploy.

M3 The greenfield lab — the gold book-break hunt, and the four hunts that never ran

★ READ THIS FIRST — what is here and what is not

This movement was scheduled as five separate hunts: one per MNQ cause-cluster (VACUUM, FLOW-LED, OPEN-NEWS), a chop-day scalp lab, and a dedicated gold hunt. Four of the five never started. The serial runner ran out of budget after the rehabilitation phase overran — BUDGET: stopping section builds to protect the tail (0m left) — and the synthesis phase that reads all five never ran either. So there is no MNQ greenfield hunt in this report. That is a gap, not a null: nobody looked, so nobody found nothing. It is named in the disposition table and it is the first thing to reschedule.

The gold hunt did get far enough to save its signal file before it died, and that file is a real dataset: 397 MGC book breaks across 22 sessions from 16 July, each with the order-book features it was built to test and the forward outcome. It is scored below to the full discipline — placebo, strip-the-best-three, leave-one-day-out, the size escalation, the excursion statistic and the book-separation table. Additional gold work that DID complete is in Part 2.5 Part A (the MGC day rider, the coil bouncer, the London-open break fade) — that is where the two gold SHADOW candidates of the week live. This section is the fourth gold idea, and it is the one that failed.

The idea was a good one and it is the natural thing to try with L2 on a new instrument. Gold breaks a level; look at the order book at the moment it breaks; if the far side is empty — nothing resting in the way — the move should run, and if the far side is stacked it should stall. That is the far-side-depletion hypothesis, it is exactly what Movement 1 asks of the book on MNQ, and on 397 gold breaks it is not just absent, it is backwards. The raw signal makes −$291.00 at −$0.73 a go. The book features it was built on select the LOSERS. And the one feature that does separate — resting book imbalance — only works in a middle band and evaporates the moment you narrow to the biggest moves, which is the opposite of what the escalation was supposed to reveal.

ATR >= 2.0pt (the move has room)128−$2,262.50−$17.6847.7%−$3,328.00−$2,756.001.6

One row clears 95 and five rows are below 10. The rows below 10 are the hypothesis itself.

★ THE FINDING — an empty book ahead of a gold break is a WARNING, not a green light

Read the bottom of that table, not the top. “Far side is empty” loses −$2,059.50 over 192 signals and sits at the 4.8th percentile of its placebo. “Far side thin” is the 3.7th. “Empty far side AND a clean trend” is the 3.6th and loses −$27.91 a go. Being in the 4th percentile is not noise — it means that of 2,000 random ways to pick 192 of these breaks, 1,904 of them did BETTER. The feature carries real information and the sign is the other way round: on gold, a level that breaks into a vacuum is a level nobody is defending because nobody is there, and price comes straight back. That is a refutation of the gate as specified, and simultaneously the most promising thing in this section — it is a fade lead, not a break lead, and it is PARKED as one.

3 · The one filter that separates — and why it is still not a gate

Resting book imbalance is the exception. Skew the book our way at the moment of the break and the population turns from −$291 to +$1,795 over 134 signals, at the 96th percentile of its placebo, still +$590 after stripping its best three and still +$970 after dropping its best day. That is the only row in the study that survives all four checks. Then you cut it finer and it falls over:

book imbalance at the breaknnet $$/signalwin %strip-3worst LODO
book AGAINST us (imb < −0.10)101−$315.00−$3.1245.5%−$1,077.50−$771.00
book neutral (−0.10 to +0.10)161−$1,703.00−$10.5849.1%−$2,459.00−$2,271.50
book with us (+0.10 to +0.25)91$1,758.50$19.3251.6%$691.00$902.00
book strongly with us (> +0.25)44−$31.50−$0.7245.5%−$963.00−$521.00

This is the killer column and it is the fourth row. If book imbalance were a real mechanism, more of it would be better. It is not: the +0.10 to +0.25 band makes $19.32 a signal and the band ABOVE it — the book most strongly on our side — loses money. An edge that lives in one middle bucket and dies on either side of it is a bucket, not a mechanism.

4 · The escalation — full census, then the top 25, then the top 15

The scope’s instruction is explicit and it exists because marginal moves wash out the footprint the monsters might carry: if nothing works on everything, narrow to the biggest moves and hunt again. Here the escalation runs the wrong way, which is a result in itself.

Read the three tables together. On the full population the imbalance rule makes +$1,795 and survives strip-3 at +$590. Narrow to the 25 biggest moves and it still shows +$992.50 — but strip its best three and it is −$212.50, i.e. the whole thing is three prints. Narrow again to the top 15 and every single rule is negative; the best of them loses −$54.00. The footprint does not get cleaner as the moves get bigger. It gets worse. Whatever separates gold breaks, it is not something that concentrates in the monsters — and that is a genuinely useful thing to know before anyone spends another night on it.

5 · The excursion statistic — why no stop can save this

what was measuredvaluewhat it means
median favourable excursion3.28×ATRthe typical break does eventually go our way by about three ATR
median adverse excursion-3.04×ATR… and goes against us by almost exactly the same amount first
signals reaching 2×ATR our way66.0%two-thirds do offer a real move at some point
signals going 1×ATR against us first80.4%four-fifths hurt before they help — this is the number that kills it
signals that ‘ran’48.4%a coin flip, by the detector's own definition

Median MFE 3.28 ATR against median MAE −3.04 ATR is a symmetric distribution. There is no asymmetry to build a stop around: any stop tight enough to make the losers small takes out 80% of the population before it works, and any stop wide enough to survive costs more than the winners pay. This single table is why the exit rehabilitation that rescued exhaustion_short in Part 1.5 cannot rescue this — there, the entry beat 60 of 60 random controls; here, the entry is the coin flip.
