★ THE ONE STONE STILL UNTURNED

Both of this movement’s honest verdicts point at the same missing piece and it is not another feature. It is the OPEN/NEWS cluster on MNQ — 22 of this week’s 68 runs, roughly a third of an $8,024 ceiling, and no gate on this desk has ever been built for it. That hunt was scheduled tonight, was fifth in the queue, and the queue ran out. It costs one phase to find out and it should be first next week, ahead of any further gold work.

P3 REVISION 2 — the eight questions the report left open, answered on real data

Gaz — the self-proofread pass read the finished report back and found eight places where it raised something and then walked away from it, plus fourteen places where two sections disagreed. The fourteen are fixed in the sections themselves, each one tagged ★ REV2 where it sits, so you never have to hold two numbers in your head. This part is the eight questions, each one re-run from the tape, the trade table and the shadow book. Every number below is a computation I ran tonight, not a restatement.

Lead with what survived — the six that changed something

1 · The wide stop won the exhaustion_short head-to-head outright, so only ONE exit goes in the shadow book. On the gate’s own 87 signals the wide stop pays +$3,459.98 and the tight chandelier pays −$339.06. The chandelier sits at the 58th percentile of its own random-entry control — a coin. Its two rows come out of exit_overrides_proposed.json.

2 · The abs_veto_short gap is the LADDER, not the fills, and now it is measured rather than inferred. Across 46 live legs the desk fills a median 0.60 points BETTER than the repricer assumes. The forward A/B is testing the right thing.

3 · The router timing change is worth +$502 and every dollar of it is on non-trend days. Hold out the trending eighth and 45/5/3 is −$13.50. Ship it for the mechanism; do not quote the number.

4 · The day rider’s $1,617.50 is not inflated by your hands — if anything they cost $254. The rider’s own rule, replayed on the same five entries, pays $1,871.79. But zero of the five legs has a venue execution record, which is worse than the “four of five” Part 1 reported.

5 · The grind book is clean. The “+$159 on a 0.81R excursion” alarm was a false positive from a truncated statistic. 0 of 26 trades book more than their own tape could pay. That allegation is withdrawn.

6 · Banking one lot at +20 points is real but thin. +$219.50, all 25 winners kept, every fold positive — and −$45.00 once you strip its best three trades. SHADOW, and the cause of death for a deploy is named.

The two that changed nothing but needed saying: the counter-trend bench figure was already on the harsh ruler (Q6), and the OPEN/NEWS hunt cannot be fixed by reordering a queue (Q7).

Q1 · The biggest bucket of loss had no action against it. Now it does — and the diagnosis was wrong.

Part 1 §4 found the week’s largest single bucket of loss: $1,058 across 11 signals that were up a median 39 points and still went the whole way to the stop. It named the untested fix — bank one of the two lots at a fixed points-onside and let the other run — called it “the first thing I would test next week”, and then it never reached the plays card. So the report’s own largest diagnosed loss had nothing pointed at it.

First, the diagnosis, because it turned out to be the more important half. That 39-point figure is measured over a fifteen-minute window from entry. The median hold on those eleven signals was 92 seconds. Re-measure over the window that actually matters — the time we were in the position — and it collapses:

The 11 “exit fault” signals15 minutes from entry While we actually held it
Median favourable move39.2pt7.25pt
Reached 20pt+ onside11 of 112 of 11
Reached 12pt+ onside11 of 114 of 11
Median hold—92 seconds

Nine of the eleven made their 39-point move after our stop had already taken us out. So this is not “we had the money and gave every penny back”; for nine of them the money arrived when we were flat. That makes it a stop-width problem, which is the same answer Part 1.5 §7 reaches on a different gate-week and a different sample. Two independent routes to “the stop is too tight” is the strongest thing either section has.

Now the test itself. All 57 paired signals of the week, winners included — that is the §9.2 apply-to-all-lots honesty rule, and it is the rule that killed the break-even stop. 5-second bars folded from capture.db. The favourable touch has to happen in a bar strictly earlier than the bar Lot A actually exited in, so the rule can never be awarded money on the bar the trade died in. Lot B is untouched. Costs are the real $1.50 round trip at $2.00 a point.

Bank Lot A atWeek netvs the −$325.00 we booked Signals it fires onGained on losersGiven up on winners Winners kept greenWorst leave-one-day-outStrip its best 3 vs 400 random-time controls
+8pt−$292.00+$33.0037+$664.50−$631.5024/25−$193.00−$210.00100th
+10pt−$244.50+$80.5035+$612.00−$531.5024/25−$185.50−$174.50100th
+12pt−$193.50+$131.5034+$563.00−$431.5024/25−$85.50−$124.50100th
+15pt−$198.50+$126.5030+$394.50−$268.0025/25−$74.50−$114.00100th
+20pt−$105.50+$219.5012+$264.50−$45.0025/25+$48.50−$45.00100th
+25pt−$207.50+$117.504+$121.00−$3.5025/25−$14.00−$14.00100th
+30pt / +40pt−$325.00$0.000——25/25———

I added the +25 / +30 / +40 rungs because the best cell of the grid you asked for sat on its top edge, and a best-on-the-boundary is the standing curve-fit tell. With them in, you can see the shape: nothing at all reaches +30 points before its exit bar.

What the placebo does here is worth a sentence, because it is the test that matters. The control keeps exactly the same set of banked lots and the same trade durations, but banks Lot A at a random bar inside the trade instead of at the +X touch. If “get out sooner” were the whole story, random timing would do as well. It does not: banking at a random moment is worth −$485 to −$850 across the week against the live exit’s −$325, and the real rule beat 400 of 400 draws at every rung. The gain is in the +X trigger, not in leaving early.

The verdict, and the test that decides it

SHADOW, not deploy, and the cause of death for a deploy is STRIP-THE-BEST-THREE. At +20 the rule takes the week from −$325.00 to −$105.50, keeps every one of the 25 winning signals green, is positive on all five leave-one-day-out folds and beats every random control. But it fires on 12 of 57 signals, +$171 of the +$219.50 is Monday alone, and deleting its best three trades leaves −$45.00. It is also a one-cell spike rather than a plateau — +25 is worth half and +30 is worth nothing. A shadow slate settles all of that for free and costs no Saturday. It is BUILD #18.

And note what it does NOT do. On the eleven signals that started this whole question, banking at +20 recovers −$1,058 → −$34 — but only because two of them genuinely went 20 points our way. The other nine are untouched by any in-trade rule, because we were not in the trade when the move happened. The real fix for that bucket is stop width, and after that, re-entry.

Q2 · Two studies, two exits, same gate — which one goes in the shadow book?

Part 1.5 §7 concludes the fix for exhaustion_short is a 1.5×ATR stop with a 3.0×ATR target and a 15-minute cap, and BUILD #1 shadow-slates it. Part 2 §8d concludes the fix is a tight chandelier on both lots for the trend rungs, and writes it to data/exit_overrides_proposed.json as the deliverable. Two different exit families for the same gate, and neither section graded itself against the other. Only one of them was on the card.

They were measured on different populations with different engines, so “+$3,459.98” and “+$22.8 a signal” were never comparable numbers. Both have now been run on the same 87 tape-backed signals — the live gate’s own entries — on the same stored tick paths, under the same 15-minute cap, split by the §8b rung (1-minute ER(30): BIG-TREND at 0.50+, MED-TREND 0.30–0.50, and ATR ≥ 22 inside chop is the STAY-OUT bucket the ladder measures but does not trade).

RungSignals WIDE — 1.5×ATR stop / 3.0×ATR target TIGHT CHANDELIER ×2 — 1.0×ATR stop, 1.5×ATR trail
BIG-TREND4−$82.56  (−$20.6/sig)−$65.02  (−$16.3/sig)
MED-TREND19+$2,018.44  (+$106.2/sig)−$27.80  (−$1.5/sig)
SCALP-CHOP29+$533.64  (+$18.4/sig)−$38.27  (−$1.3/sig)
STAY-OUT (ATR ≥ 22 in chop)35+$990.46  (+$28.3/sig)−$207.97  (−$5.9/sig)
All 8787+$3,459.98−$339.06
Win rate not a kill criterion 54.0%40.2%
Strip the best 3 +$2,046.14−$1,176.06
Worst leave-one-day-out +$1,626.49−$954.68
Days green 9 of 125 of 12
The same paired random-entry control60 draws 100th percentile, entry alpha +$3,433.27 58th percentile, entry alpha +$69.37

The wide stop wins every rung that has any n behind it, and the chandelier does not survive its own control. At the 58th percentile against paired random entries the chandelier is indistinguishable from firing at random on this population — the same failure the live scalp exit shows at 47. The one cell where the chandelier is nominally ahead is BIG-TREND, on four signals, where both policies lose money.

And it is not the 15-minute cap doing it, which was the obvious objection since a chandelier is built to ride and §8b gave it sixty minutes. Run both on the 5-second tape at three horizons:

HorizonWIDE, per signalTIGHT CHANDELIER, per signal
15 minutes+$40.21−$3.60
30 minutes+$47.30−$6.02
60 minutes — §8b’s own horizon+$45.96−$6.45

A longer leash makes the chandelier worse, not better.

The answer: ONE proposal, and it is the wide stop

The wide stop goes in the shadow book. The two exhaustion_short rows in data/exit_overrides_proposed.json should be removed — they are now NOT-AN-ACTION #14, with the random-entry control named as the test that killed them.

To be fair to §8d: its cells were scored on the 716-entry exhaustion_rev shadow board, a much larger and different population, and I am not calling it wrong there. But a deploy acts on the LIVE gate, and on the live gate’s own entries this policy is negative on every rung at every horizon and cannot beat a coin. That is the population that decides it.

The rung boundary question is answered too, and the answer is that there isn’t one. The wide stop is not a trend policy that happens to survive chop — it is positive on MED-TREND, SCALP-CHOP and STAY-OUT alike. There is nothing to make rung-conditional. Ship the simpler thing. The grind_long MED-TREND row in the same proposal file is untouched by this — different gate, not tested here, and it is now BUILD #13 in its own right.

Q3 · Is the abs_veto_short gap the exit ladder, or is it just worse fills?

Part 2 §3 diagnoses a two-thousand-dollar live-versus-shadow gap on the short side as “the exit ladder is too wide”, and it infers that entirely from the exit-reason mix: same winner size, a third bigger losers. But this desk’s own standing finding is that live losses run 1.1–3.1× modelled, and “we get filled worse than the repricer assumes” produces exactly that same table. Nothing in the section measured a single fill.

So I measured every measurable leg against the price it was supposed to fill at. Getting the level right is the whole job here, and it needed three things done properly:

What had to be reconstructedHow, and why it matters
R, the stop distancestop_atr_mult (=1.0) × the ATR-14 the gate would have read at the entry minute, recomputed with deciders._atr’s own halt-aware rule on 1-minute bars folded from the 5-second tape. Using a naive ATR would mis-size every level after a session break.
Which ladder was runningPlain 1.5R/2.5R before 2 August; the quiet-tape clip after it (ATR < 22 → Lot A takes a flat $40, Lot B takes max(1.75R, $60)). Modelling one ladder across the whole window manufactures about nine points of fake target slippage on every quiet-tape leg — I did exactly that on the first pass and it produced a completely wrong answer.
The sign conventionSlippage = fill − level, so on a SHORT a positive number is adverse.
ExitLegsMeasurableMedian slip Mean slipp90Worst$ cost
STOP3535−0.95pt+0.68pt+10.80pt+19.84pt−$47.32
TARGET1111−0.25pt−4.32pt+2.25pt+3.25pt+$95.11
Every measurable leg4646 −0.60pt−0.52pt   +$47.78 in our favour

We fill a median 0.60 points BETTER than the repricer assumes. 24 of the 46 legs filled at their level or better. The tail the standing finding warns about is real but small: 12 stop legs filled more than a point beyond their stop and cost −$194.18 between them, and the other 23 handed $146.86 back, for a net stop cost of −$47.32.

Then the decisive step — re-run the shadow twin with that measured slippage applied to its own stops and targets, and see whether the gap closes:

abs_veto_55s SHORT, the shadow twin, 118 tradesNet
As scored+$1,828.50
With the measured slippage applied+$1,965.84
The live short side (clean ledger, 61 legs)−$265.00
Gap before → gap after$2,093.50 → $2,230.84
The answer: it is the ladder. The A/B is testing the right thing.

Fills explain none of the gap — applying them makes it marginally wider, because our fills are marginally better than modelled. “The ladder is too wide” and “we get filled worse” do produce the same table, and this one is the ladder. The tighter-ladder shadow A/B is now BUILD #12.

One correction while I was in there. The headline “63 legs / −$520.50” includes two quarantined rows (EXCLUDE:md_stream_atr_corruption_20260804, the two MAX_HOLD legs, −$255.50). On the clean ledger the live short side is −$265.00 over 61 legs and the gap is $2,093.50, not $2,349. The section’s own normalisation already strips those legs, so its −$159.00 strategy-only figure was right either way — it was only the headline that quoted rows the rest of the report excludes.

Q4 · How much of the day rider’s $1,617.50 survives a venue-verified accounting?

The rider produced $1,617.50 of the desk’s $1,292.50 — take it out and the week is red — and Part 1 says plainly that most of its trades have no venue execution record and two are hand-reconstructed with an exit reason (CLOCK_FLAT_RECON) that no code in this repo emits. It never asks the follow-on: what is the rider’s actual edge as opposed to its booked one?

Three separate questions, because they have three different answers.

First, the venue. It is worse than the report said.

DaySideBooked exitBooked $ Execution id?A size-2 fill at that price?
08-10SHORTCLOCK_FLAT_RECON+$39.00nono
08-11SHORTMANUAL_CLAIM+$189.00nono
08-12SHORTCLOCK_FLAT_RECON+$224.50nono
08-13LONGMANUAL_CLAIM+$731.00nono
08-14SHORTTRAIL+$434.00nono

Zero of five legs carries an entry_exec_id or an exit_exec_id, and zero has a matching size-2 execution in fills at the booked exit price. Part 1 says “four of five have no venue execution record” and reports the fills gap as closing on 08-14; on the clean ledger it is five of five, and the 08-14 TRAIL row is no better evidenced than the others. $0 of the $1,617.50 is venue-verified. That is a measurement gap, not an allegation — but it is a bigger one than the report claimed, and the re-check next Friday needs to be “5 of 5”, not “1 of 5”.

Second, the tape. The reconstruction is honest.

The two CLOCK_FLAT_RECON rows are a script’s best guess at what the 20:40Z flat was worth. Both check out against the tape: on 08-10 the tape closed the 20:40 minute at 29,750.50 against a booked exit of 29,749.00, and on 08-12 at 29,818.75 against 29,819.25. The $263.50 of reconstructed P&L is accurate to within about $4 net of what the tape says that flat was worth. Flag it as unverified at the venue, by all means; it is not invented.

Third, and this is the real question: the RULE versus the HANDS.

The rider’s own deterministic exit is the ATR-scaled trail — arm at 4×ATR ahead, then trail 2×ATR off the peak (ARM_ATR_MULT and TRAIL_ATR_MULT, read from day_rider.py, not guessed) — with the 20:40Z hard flat as the backstop. Replay that from the same five entries on the 5-second tape and compare with what was booked:

DayBooked asBooked $The rule’s exit The rule’s $Booked − rule
08-10CLOCK_FLAT_RECON+$39.00CLOCK_FLAT+$33.00+$6.00
08-11MANUAL_CLAIM+$189.00TRAIL+$347.57−$158.57
08-12CLOCK_FLAT_RECON+$224.50CLOCK_FLAT+$226.50−$2.00

Where the booked $1,617.50 actually comes fromLegs$Share

The answer: the booked number UNDERSTATES the rider, and the claims cost money this week

Only 27% of the rider’s week is an exit its own code chose. But the answer to “is the booked number flattered by discretion?” is no, the opposite: the rule, replayed on the same entries, pays $1,871.79 against $1,617.50 booked. The two manual claims came −$200.86 early between them, and the 08-14 trail booked $57.43 below the replay’s trail. So the rider’s edge is not an artefact of your hands.

Hold that lightly. It is five trades, the replay is on 5-second bars with fills modelled at the exact level and no slippage — a ceiling, like every reprice in this report — and every one of those five entries is itself unverified at the venue. Nothing here promotes the rider. It says the discretion is not what made the money, and it says the fills-gap fix has to cover 5 of 5 before any number from this desk is quoted as proven.

One thing this could NOT do as asked: the proofread suggested scoring the five trades against the 41-day Open Rider replay. That replay is a different strategy — a clock rider that re-enters every few minutes between 13:00 and 14:45Z with a 45-minute cap — so its days are not the live rider’s trades. Building the live rider’s own replay, which is what the table above is, answers the question the proofread was actually asking. See Q on the Open Rider in the fixes list for why the two must never be treated as one engine.

Q5 · Does the router’s 45/5/3 timing change survive on the days the router exists for?

SATURDAY #1 is worth +$502 on the live managed set and +$399 on the full historical set. It rests on 4 of 40 days, on a window Part 2.6 §4 itself calls “overwhelmingly ONE regime”, with this week running 84% chop — and the router is a trend instrument. The section flags that and does not test it.

So: rank all 40 Paris days by session efficiency (|close − open| over the sum of the 1-minute moves across the router’s own trading window, which is the same quantity its threshold reads), hold out the top eight, and score the shipped config against 45/5/3 on those eight alone. Everything is run through scripts/router_study.py’s own replay engine, which that section validated as byte-identical to the shipped direction_router.replay_marks, so this is the real router being measured.

Universe / sliceDaysNo router Shipped45/5/3Improvement
LIVE managed set — all 40 days40−$302.50+$189.50+$691.50+$502.00
  — the trending eighth8+$2.50+$160.50+$147.00−$13.50
  — the other 3232−$305.00+$29.00+$544.50+$515.50
FULL historical fader set — all 40 days40−$722.50+$584.50+$983.50+$399.00
  — the trending eighth8+$410.50+$613.00+$566.50−$46.50
  — the other 3232−$1,133.00−$28.50+$417.00+$445.50

And here are the days that actually move, which is the question underneath the question. Only four of forty change at all on the live set:

DaySession ERIn the trending eighth? Shipped45/5/3Delta
07-240.036no−$115.50+$235.00+$350.50
07-230.033no+$234.50+$351.00+$116.50
07-280.021no−$90.00−$41.50+$48.50
07-210.067YES+$128.50+$115.00−$13.50

On the full set seven days move; the two large positives are the same non-trend days (07-24 +$393.50, 07-23 +$161.50), and the two trending-eighth days that move are +$8.00 and −$54.50.

The answer: the caveat becomes a scoping rule, not a blocker

Every dollar of the +$502 is non-trend-day money, and on the trending eighth the change is mildly negative. The three days carrying it run session ER 0.036, 0.033 and 0.021 — the flat part of a flat sample. The one trending day that moves goes the wrong way.

I would still ship it, and I want to be precise about why, because the dollar figure is now off the table. The section’s third argument was always the strongest and it is untouched by this: 45/5/3 is not a number found by searching, it is the mechanical fix for two problems measured from the live ledger rather than from the grid — a 45-minute window smooths the exact one-point boundary flicker that produced 36 flips on one gate, and 5-minute steps close the actuation lag that cost −$242 this week. When a grid search and a hand-written ledger arrive at the same three constants from opposite directions, that is the evidence.

What changes on the card: the play now carries the regime split in its own number column, its verification requires the trend-day split to be reported separately, and its kill criterion adds “one genuine trend week where it is negative”. Do not quote +$502 as what this buys.

Q6 · Is the counter-trend bench priced with the generous ruler or the honest one?

The objection was sharp and it deserved a straight answer. SATURDAY #2 is quoted at n=26 / −$398.50, and Part 2.6 §3 proves the nightly’s scalp-2R ruler is $7.26 a trade more generous than the desk overall and +$25.02 a trade more generous specifically in TREND_UP — which is exactly the regime those 26 fires sit in. The report uses that ruler to refuse the exhaustion_short over-bench figure and then appears to use it to justify the bench. Both cannot be right.

They are not both right, and the resolution is that the premise is wrong: the −$398.50 was never on the nightly’s ruler. It is realised pnl_usd — what the desk actually banked on its own fills under the live two-lot exit. I re-derived it from scratch to be sure: every clean exhaustion_short leg over the last 30 Paris days, labelled with the router’s own regime at the moment of entry, and then priced BOTH ways.

Regime at entryLegsLIVE two-lot exit $/legNightly scalp-2R ruler$/leg Ruler gap per leg
TREND_UP — counter-trend, the bench case26 −$398.50−$15.33 −$168.93−$6.50+$8.83
CHOP72+$575.50+$7.99−$603.71−$8.38−$16.38
TREND_DOWN — aligned48−$335.00−$6.98+$451.21+$9.40+$16.38

The nightly’s ruler would price the same 26 legs at only −$168.93. It is $8.83 a leg MORE generous here — so using it would weaken the case for this bench by more than half, not inflate it. The report’s figure is the conservative one.

The two uses of the ruler are therefore consistent rather than contradictory: scalp-2R flatters trend and punishes chop (+$8.83 a leg in TREND_UP, +$16.38 in TREND_DOWN, −$16.38 in CHOP). That is precisely why the “$721 exhaustion_short over-bench” figure was refused — it was a trend-regime wins-missed number computed on the flattering ruler — and equally why the bench case, which is realised money, needs no discount.

The rest of the robustness reproduced exactly, on the live ruler: 26 legs across 8 days, negative on 7 of the 8, strip the worst day and it is still −$232.50, and leave-one-day-out gets stronger at −$623.00 when the only green day (07-30, +$224.50) is dropped.

The answer: the bench stands, on the ruler the desk actually trades

−$398.50 over 26 legs is the live figure and it is the one on the card. The honest caveat is the one the section already gives: n=26 is a SHADOW-grade sample being used for a LIVE change — but the change is a bench, which is the cheap direction to be wrong in, and it is red on 7 of 8 days with both robustness legs surviving. Review the first 15 benched episodes before treating it as settled.

Q7 · What actually stops the OPEN/NEWS hunt running — and is BUILD #2 enough?

The OPEN/NEWS cluster is 22 of this week’s 68 runs and roughly $2,596 of the $8,024 ceiling, no gate on this desk has ever been built for it, and its hunt has now been scheduled and not run. BUILD #2 moves it to the front of the queue. That is not enough, and the arithmetic says so plainly.

The 14 August run, from data/friday_durable.logMinutes
Window opened 22:07Z against a 05:15Z deadline428
Census freeze + gold census + progress page, before the runner started10
Reserved for the tail (assemble → proofread → rev2 → final)200
= BODY BUDGET220
part1_live 22.0 + part2_shadow 27.9 + part25_musings 25.1 + part2_6_router 24.0 + movement2_idle 15.4114.4
rehab — took its FULL cap and returned no artifact90.0
gf_MGC — died at 13.2 minutes, no artifact13.2
“BUDGET: stopping section builds to protect the tail (0m left)”, 01:54:59Z — 8 sections missing217.6 spent

Now the part that makes reordering pointless. The four MNQ hunts are capped at 90 minutes each in friday_phases.py:

What has to fitMinutesAs a share of the 220-minute body budget
gf_VACUUM + gf_FLOW-LED + gf_OPEN-NEWS + gf_chopscalp, at their caps360164%
Every body phase at its cap915416%
The tail’s own caps, against a 200-minute reserve255128% — the reserve is 55 minutes short before anything goes wrong

The OPEN/NEWS hunt has never fitted, in any ordering. Put it first and it consumes 41% of the entire body budget on its own, and the five sections that DID run this week — and produced the report’s best material — would be the ones dropped instead. That is a worse trade, not a better one.

And there is a second, independent failure that reordering does not touch

Even after the body budget ran out, the tail should have completed. It did not: assemble returned rc=0 after 25.6 minutes and the runner logged “assemble: rc=0 25.6m artifact=MISSING” followed by “TAIL PHASE FAILED: assemble — stopping (downstream depends on it)”. The artifact path contained a doubled-brace {WEEK} placeholder that resolved to a literal filename which cannot exist, so a phase that had genuinely succeeded was recorded as a failure and the proofread never ran on the night. That is fixed in commit 8df7eff — and it is why the proofread this revision answers is dated 06:06Z on Saturday rather than 02:20Z on Friday.

The answer: BUILD #2 needs a budget change attached, and it now has one

A scheduling promise without a budget change is the same promise that failed this week. Three options, any one of which works, in the order I would take them:

(a) Cap the rehab phase at 45 minutes. It took its full 90 and produced no artifact; the section that shipped was rebuilt by assemble from the JSON the phase had already written before it died. Ninety minutes bought nothing that forty-five would not have.

(b) Move the deadline out. A 22:07Z start needs roughly 11:30Z, not 05:15Z, to hold one greenfield hunt on top of the body.

(c) Split the greenfield hunts into their own run — a separate Saturday job with its own window, which is the only option that scales to all four.

BUILD #2 now carries this arithmetic in its own number column and its verification says to change the budget before the ordering. The two causes are separate and both have to be closed: the budget, and the {WEEK} plumbing bug that killed the tail on every run until today.

Q8 · Is the live grind book carrying mis-attributed fills?

Part 2 §6 records a grind_long trade that books +$159 live while its maximum favourable excursion was 0.81R — about $23 a lot at ATR 14.1 — and calls that “not physically reachable on the tape”, pointing at the known _stop_seq fill mis-attribution mechanism. That is not a shadow-tool problem. It is an allegation that the live P&L is wrong, and it was parked in a footnote.

So every one of the 26 grind_long trades in that window has been scored against its own full-life excursion: the highest price the tape printed between that trade’s own recorded entry and its own recorded exit. That is deliberately the most generous ceiling available — it ignores the stop entirely, so a trade that dipped through its stop and rallied still gets credit for the rally. A booked P&L above THAT cannot have come from the position’s own price path under any exit rule.

Trades checkedBook more than their own tape could pay $ of “impossible” P&L
260$0.00

Nothing in the grind book is unreachable, and the three that looked worst are the three that check out best:

TradeATRWatch’s rMFE Full-life MFEBookedPhysical ceiling
07-30 11:11:57 CHANDELIER14.10.81R (11.4pt)87.50pt+$159.00+$173.50
07-30 12:43:05 MANUAL_CLAIM28.50.18R (5.1pt)101.00pt+$156.50+$200.50
07-29 22:06:13 CHANDELIER27.23.72R170.50pt+$305.00+$339.50

Why the alarm fired, and it is a proper instrument note

two_ratchet_shadow_watch.rmfe() stops measuring at the first tick that is a full ATR against the entry. It is a pre-stop excursion by design — the excursion a trade could reach before its 1-ATR stop would have fired — and it is the right statistic for the question that watch asks. It is completely the wrong statistic for “how far did this trade go”. The 11:11 trade dipped an ATR against us early, then ran 87.5 points.

And the exit reconciles to the deployed rule to within a fifth of a point. It entered at 27,670.00 and the tape printed 27,757.50 while we held it. A peak of 87.5pt at ATR 14.1 is 6.21R, past the pure-6.0 chandelier’s lock, so the trail firms from start_k 3.5 to lock_k 0.5 × ATR = 7.05 points. 27,757.50 − 7.05 = 27,750.45, and we exited at 27,750.25. That is not a mis-attributed fill; that is the exit doing exactly what it was built to do on a 6.2R runner.

The answer: no integrity problem. The allegation is withdrawn.

0 of 26. There is no measurement gap to put on the card, and grind_long’s all-time −$454.00 over 98 lots on the clean ledger stands as reported.

What there IS is an instrument note with teeth, and it is the more useful finding: the watch’s rMFE column is truncated by construction and must never be read as a trade’s excursion. Part 2 §6 and its instrument-notes table now say so, and the “not physically reachable” sentence is gone. The wider lesson is the one this desk keeps relearning: a number that arrives without a failure mode is not a measurement — and that applies to the numbers we use to accuse our own books, not just to the ones we use to promote a gate.

Standing caveats on everything above

Every reprice is a ceiling. Q1, Q2, Q4 and Q5 all model fills at the exact level with no slippage. Q3 is the one place in this part where real fills were measured, and it says our execution is fine on this gate, on 46 legs — do not generalise that to the wide-stop policy in Q2, which would be filled at a 1.5×ATR stop in exactly the moves where a wide stop is thin.

Thin n, named. Q1 fires on 12 signals; Q2’s BIG-TREND rung is 4; Q4 is 5 trades; Q5’s whole result turns on 4 days out of 40. Nothing here is promoted to live on this revision’s evidence — the only card changes are shadow slates, a scoping, and one withdrawal.

Win rate is nowhere in any verdict above, per the standing rule. Every kill in this part is named: strip-the-best-three (Q1), a paired random-entry control (Q2), a leave-out-the-regime holdout (Q5), and a physical ceiling (Q8).
